Overview.

We start in
Section

2
by recalling the basics of univalent foundations and how to develop category theory therein.
In this section, we also recall the notions that are used throughout this paper.
We follow this up in
Section

3
by discussing categorical models of dependent type theory in univalent foundations.
More specifically, we explain why we use comprehension categories instead of categories with families to model the internal language of univalent categories.

Next we study various bicategories of comprehension categories.
We start in
Section

4
with the bicategory of full univalent comprehension categories (
Section

4.2
),
and we prove that it is univalent (
Proposition

8
).
The key idea behind the construction is to use displayed bicategories,
which we recall at the start of
Section

4
.
After that, we consider various type formers for comprehension categories in
Section

5
,
and we construct the bicategory of univalent full democratic finite limit comprehension categories (DFL comprehension categories) (
Section

5
).
These are full univalent comprehension categories that are democratic
and that support unit types, binary product types, equalizer types, and
\Sigma
-types.
We also prove that the resulting bicategory is univalent (
Proposition

11
),
and we give a sufficient condition for when a morphism of such comprehension categories is an adjoint equivalence (
Proposition

12
).

In the final sections of the paper, we study internal language theorems for univalent categories.
We start in
Section

6
with the internal language of univalent categories with finite limits,
which is given by extensional Martin-Löf type theory with unit types, binary product types, and
\Sigma
-types (
Section

6.3
).
More specifically, we construct a biequivalence between the bicategories of univalent categories with finite limits
and of DFL comprehension categories.
In
Section

7
we extend this biequivalence to locally Cartesian closed categories (
Section

7.2
).
The main idea here is to use displayed biequivalences, which we recall at the beginning of that section.
Next we extend this biequivalence using local properties in
Section

8
.
This allows us to deduce internal language theorems for various classes of toposes (
Section

8.2
).
Finally, we also consider categories with a universe in
Sections

9
and
10
.
In those sections, we define a notion of universe in categories with finite limits and comprehension categories,
and we extend the aforementioned biequivalence to toposes with a natural numbers object and a universe (
Section

10.2.6
).
We finish this paper with a discussion of related work in
Section

11
,
and we conclude in
Section

12
.

2.
Category Theory in Univalent Foundations

In this section,
we recall
univalent foundations

[
87
]
,
and the development of category theory therein
[
4
]
.
Univalent foundations is a version of Martin-Löf type theory
[
70
]

in which the
univalence axiom
holds
[
87
, Axiom 2.10.3]
.
This axiom allows us to identify types if they are equivalent,
which gives us a more extensional identity of types
while type checking remains decidable.
Note the difference with plain Martin-Löf type theory,
where types are identified in a fully intensional way
meaning that types are only considered to be equal if they are presented as the same object.

To formulate the univalence axiom precisely,
we use the map
\mathsf{idtoequiv}:A=B\rightarrow A\simeq B
,
which is defined for all types
A,B:\mathcal{U}
in some universe
\mathcal{U}

[
87
, Lemma 2.10.1]
.
This map is defined using path induction,
and it sends the identity path to the identity equivalence.
The univalence axiom states that
\mathsf{idtoequiv}
is an equivalence of types.

A consequence of the univalence axiom is that identity in univalent foundations is necessarily proof relevant,
because the identity type of the universe is equivalent to the type of equivalences.
It is thus sensible to classify types based on the complexity of their identity type.
The types with the simplest identity types are
propositions
and
contractible types
.
A
proposition
is a type for which every two inhabitants are equal,
and a
contractible type
is an inhabited proposition.
The identity type of a proposition is thus trivial, because it is equivalent to the unit type.
We also have the notion of
set
, which is a type
A
for which
x=y
is a proposition for all
x,y:A
.
Inhabitants of a set could be equal,
but there always is at most one proof of their equality.

Homotopy levels

[
87
, Definition 7.1.1]
generalize the notions of contractible types, propositions, and sets,
and for each
n\geq-2
we have a notion of
n
-types
.
We say that a type is a
(-2)
-type if it is contractible,
and a type
A
is an
(n+1)
-type if the type
x=y
is an
n
-type for all
x,y:A
.

Another central concept in univalent foundations is the
propositional truncation
.
Given a type
A
, the propositional truncation is a proposition
\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}
together with a map
|-|:A\rightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}

satisfying the following universal property:
if we have some proposition
P
and a map
A\rightarrow P
,
then we have a unique map
\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}\rightarrow P
.

The propositional truncation allows us to distinguish between equipped structure and structure that merely exists,
which is important in mathematics
[
30
]
.
For instance, let
T
be the type of terminal objects in
\mathsf{C}
.
An inhabitant of
T
equips
\mathsf{C}
with a terminal object.
The truncation of
T
, on the other hand, expresses the mere existence of a terminal object in
\mathsf{C}
.
Constructively, one can generally not obtain a terminal object from an inhabitant of
\mathopen{}\left\|T\right\|\mathclose{}
.

2.1.
Univalent Categories

Univalent foundations provides an interesting point of view on several areas of mathematics,
and among those is category theory.
Categories can be studied up to two notions of equivalence: isomorphism and adjoint equivalence.
This bifurcation is reflected in univalent foundations where we have two different notions of categories: setcategories and univalent categories
[
4
]
.
Setcategories are those categories whose types of objects form a set,
and univalent categories are those categories in which isomorphisms coincide with equality.
One uses setcategories to study category theory up to isomorphism,
whereas one uses univalent categories to study category theory up to adjoint equivalence
[
78
]
.
More specifically, setcategories are identified if they are isomorphic,
and univalent categories are identified if they are adjoint equivalent.

Throughout this paper, our focus is on univalent categories.
To define this notion, let us start by recalling categories
[
4
, Definition 3.1]
.
A category is given by
a type of objects,
a
set
of morphisms indexed by pairs of objects
with identity morphisms and a composition operation
satisfying the usual unitality and associativity laws.
Composition is written in diagrammatic order:
if we have
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow z
,
then we denote their composition by
f\cdot g:x\rightarrow z
.
We say that a category is
univalent
if the identity type of objects is equivalent to isomorphism of objects.

{defiC}

[
[
4
, Definition 3.6]
]

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category.
For all objects
x,y:\mathsf{C}
we have a map
\mathsf{idtoiso}_{x,y}
, which sends identities
x=y
to isomorphisms
x\mathrel{\cong}y
.
This map is defined using path induction, and it sends the identity path to the identity isomorphism.
We say that
\mathsf{C}
is
univalent
if the map
\mathsf{idtoiso}_{x,y}
is an equivalence of types for all
x,y:\mathsf{C}
.

Note that univalence can be defined for a wide variety of categorical structures
[
9
]
.
By the univalence axiom, various categories, such as the category of sets or groups, are univalent
[
32
]
.
Now let us look at some properties of univalent categories that we use throughout this paper.
First, note that the type of objects in a univalent category is not necessarily a set.
A counterexample is given by the category
\mathsf{Set}
of sets and functions.
The univalence axiom implies that identity of sets corresponds to isomorphism,
and as a consequence, the category
\mathsf{Set}
is univalent
and the type of sets is not a set itself.
Since identity of objects is equivalent to isomorphism
and since isomorphisms generally only form a set,
we do have that the type of objects is a
1
-type
[
4
, Lemma 3.8]
.

Proposition
\thethm
.

The type of objects in a univalent category is a
1
-type.

Another feature of univalent categories is that uniqueness up to isomorphism corresponds to actual uniqueness.
In category theory, objects such as limits and colimits are specified using universal mapping properties,
and that only guarantees their uniqueness up to isomorphism.
However, if we reason constructively,
there is a drawback to that:
the mere existence of limits in a setcategory
is, in general, insufficient to equip that category with limits
unless we assume the axiom of choice.
If we work with univalent categories,
it is possible to avoid the axiom of choice.
In such categories,
limits are unique up to identity,
which allows us to choose them if they exist.

Proposition 1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category.

•

The type of terminal objects for
\mathsf{C}
is a proposition.

•

The type expressing that
\mathsf{C}
is equipped with binary products, is a proposition.

•

The type expressing that
\mathsf{C}
is equipped with equalizers, is a proposition.

Proof 2.1
.

We only consider the case for terminal objects,
since the ideas are the same for binary products and equalizers.
The type of terminal objects is defined as a
\Sigma
-type,
whose inhabitants are pairs of an object
x:\mathsf{C}
together with a proof that
x
is terminal.
To check that two terminal objects are equal,
it suffices to check whether their underlying objects are equal,
because the type saying that some object is terminal,
is a proposition.
Since we assumed
\mathsf{C}
to be univalent,
every isomorphism in
\mathsf{C}
gives rise to an equality.
Hence, to check that two terminal objects
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
in
\mathsf{C}
are equal,
it suffices to give an isomorphism between
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
Since terminal objects are unique up to isomorphism,
we can construct the desired isomorphism,
and we can conclude that the type of terminal objects for
\mathsf{C}
is a proposition.

Note that
Proposition

1
generalizes to arbitrary (co)limits.
To illustrate how one can use
Proposition

1
,
we explain how we can choose a terminal object in a univalent category that merely has one.
Let us write
T
for the type of terminal objects in some given univalent category
\mathsf{C}
.
If we assume that
\mathsf{C}
merely has a terminal object, then we have an inhabitant of
\mathopen{}\left\|T\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Since the type
T
is a proposition,
we have a map
\mathopen{}\left\|T\right\|\mathclose{}\rightarrow T
by the recursion principle of the propositional truncation.
Hence, we also have an inhabitant of
T
, which gives us a terminal object in
\mathsf{C}
.

We see the same phenomenon in the proof that essentially surjective and fully faithful functors are adjoint equivalences.
Suppose that we have a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
that is both essentially surjective and fully faithful.
To show that
\mathsf{F}
is an adjoint equivalence,
we need to construct a functor
\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{C}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}
that is an inverse of
\mathsf{F}
up to isomorphism.
For each object
y:\mathsf{C}_{2}
, we must thus find a preimage
x:\mathsf{C}_{1}
.
However, these preimages are only unique up to isomorphism in general.
If we assume that
\mathsf{C}_{1}
is univalent,
then the preimages are actually unique
and we can construct
\mathsf{G}
while reasoning constructively.
All in all, we can again avoid the axiom of choice by assuming that some of the involved categories are univalent,
and we can constructively prove the following proposition
[
4
, Lemma 6.15]
.

Proposition 2
.

Suppose that we have categories
\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{2}
such that
\mathsf{C}_{1}
is univalent.
Then a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
is an adjoint equivalence
if
\mathsf{F}
is essentially surjective and fully faithful.

Finally, another key property of univalent categories is their structure identity principle
[
4
, Theorem 6.17]
.
This principle allows us to identify univalent categories if we have an adjoint equivalence between them.
We phrase this structure identity principle using
univalent bicategories

[
3
, Definition 3.1]
.
Recall that a bicategory is given by a type of objects, a type of 1-cells indexed by two objects,
and a
set
of 2-cells indexed by two 1-cells.
If we have 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow z
, then we denote their composition by
f\cdot g:x\rightarrow z
.
We write
\lambda:\operatorname{id}\cdot f\Rightarrow f
and
\rho:f\cdot\operatorname{id}\Rightarrow f
for the left and right unitor in a bicategory respectively,
and we write
\alpha:f\cdot(g\cdot h)\Rightarrow(f\cdot g)\cdot h
for the associator.
If we have 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g_{1},g_{2}:y\rightarrow z

and a 2-cell
\tau:g_{1}\Rightarrow g_{2}
,
then we write
f\vartriangleleft\tau:f\cdot g_{1}\Rightarrow f\cdot g_{2}
for the left whiskering.
Analogously, we write
\tau\vartriangleright g:f_{1}\cdot g\Rightarrow f_{2}\cdot g

for the right whiskering of
\tau:f_{1}\Rightarrow f_{2}
with a 1-cell
g
.
An example of a bicategory is
\mathsf{UnivCat}
, whose objects are univalent categories,
1-cells are functors,
and 2-cells are natural transformations.
Univalent bicategories are defined as follows.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a bicategory.

•

For all objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
we have a map
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{x,y}

sending identities
x=y
to adjoint equivalences
x\mathrel{\simeq}y
.
The map
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{x,y}
is defined using path induction,
and it sends the identity path to the identity adjoint equivalence.

•

For all 1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
we have a map
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,1}_{f,g}

sending identities
f=g
to invertible 2-cells
f\mathrel{\cong}g
.

We say that
\mathsf{B}
is
globally univalent
if the map
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{x,y}
is an equivalence for all
x,y:\mathsf{B}
,
and that
\mathsf{B}
is
locally univalent
if the map
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,1}_{f,g}
is an equivalence for all
f,g:x\rightarrow y
.
Finally, we say that
\mathsf{B}
is
univalent
if it is both locally and globally univalent.

Proposition 3
.

The bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}
is univalent.

Proposition

3
tells us that the identity type of univalent categories is equivalent to adjoint equivalences,
and that the identity type of functors is equivalent to natural isomorphisms.
This structure identity principle thus allows us to treat adjoint equivalences as identities.
As a consequence,
we can prove some property for all adjoint equivalences
by only verifying it for the identity equivalence,
and we can transfer properties and structure along adjoint equivalences for free.
We make this reasoning principle precise in the following proposition
[
3
, Propositions 3.3 and 3.4]
.

Proposition 4
.

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a univalent bicategory.

•

Let
\mathsf{P}
be a type family taking objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
and adjoint equivalences
l:x\mathrel{\simeq}y
.
To prove
\mathsf{P}(x,y,l)
for all
x,y:\mathsf{B}
and
l:x\mathrel{\simeq}y
,
it suffices to prove
\mathsf{P}(x,x,\operatorname{id}_{x})
for all
x:\mathsf{B}
.

•

Let
\mathsf{P}
be a type family taking objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
, 1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
, and invertible 2-cells
\tau:f\mathrel{\cong}g
.
To prove
\mathsf{P}(x,y,f,g,\tau)
for all objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
, 1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
, and invertible 2-cells
\tau:f\mathrel{\cong}g
,
it suffices to prove
\mathsf{P}(x,y,f,f,\operatorname{id}_{f})
for all
x,y:\mathsf{B}
and
f:x\rightarrow y
.

2.2.
Displayed Categories

Displayed categories

[
7
]
are fundamental when studying category theory in univalent foundations.
Their applications are plentiful,
and our main interest stems from Grothendieck fibrations
[
47
]
.
This is because
if we phrase Grothendieck fibrations in terms of displayed categories,
we can avoid referring to equality of objects when we express the existence of Cartesian lifts.
In the remainder of this section, we recall the concepts related to displayed categories that we use in this paper.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category.
A
displayed category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
consists of

•

a type
{\mathsf{D}}(x)
for each
x:\mathsf{C}
;

•

a set
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
for all objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
, and morphisms
f:x\rightarrow y
.

Inhabitants of
{\mathsf{D}}(x)
are called
objects over
x
,
and inhabitants of
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
are called
morphisms from
\overline{x}
to
\overline{y}
over
f
.
We also have a morphism
\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{x}}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}{x}}\overline{x}
for every
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
,
and if we have morphisms
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{y}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{z}
,
then we also have a composition
\overline{f}\cdot\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f\cdot g}\overline{z}
.
We require the usual associativity and unitality laws,
which are dependent equalities over the corresponding equality in
\mathsf{C}

[
7
, Definition 3.1]
.

Let
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
be a functor,
and let
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
be displayed categories over
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
respectively.
A
displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{F}
consists of

•

a map sending objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
to objects
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x}):{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
for each
x:\mathsf{C}
;

•

a map sending morphisms
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
to morphisms
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{y})

for all objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
, and morphisms
f:x\rightarrow y
.

We also require the functoriality laws
[
7
, Definition 3.11]
.

Given a natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}

and functors
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}

over
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{G}
respectively,
a
displayed natural transformation
\overline{\tau}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathsf{G}}
over
\tau

is given by a family of morphisms
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\tau(x)}\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\overline{x})
for each
x:\mathsf{C}
and
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
.
We also require that this family of morphisms is natural
[
7
, Definition 3.12]
.

Every displayed category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
gives rise to a category
\int{\mathsf{D}}
, called the
total category
,
and a functor
\pi_{\mathsf{D}}:\int{\mathsf{D}}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}
, called the
projection
.
A displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{F}
gives rise to a functor
\int{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}:\int{\mathsf{D}}\rightarrow\int{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}

that makes the following square commute up to definitional equality.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int{\mathsf{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}}

In addition, every object
x:\mathsf{C}
gives rise to a category
\mathsf{D}[x]
,
which we call the
fiber category
.
The type of objects of
\mathsf{D}[x]
is
{\mathsf{D}}(x)
,
and morphisms from
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
to
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)

are morphisms
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}_{x}}\overline{y}
.
Every displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{F}

gives rise to a functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}[x]:\mathsf{D}[x]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}[\mathsf{F}(x)]
.

Next we define
displayed isomorphisms
.
Given objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
, and an isomorphism
f:x\mathrel{\cong}y
,
a displayed isomorphism over
f
consists of morphisms
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{y}\rightarrow_{f^{-1}}\overline{x}
that compose to the identity.
We write
\overline{x}\mathrel{\cong_{f}}\overline{y}
for the type of displayed isomorphisms from
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
to
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
over
f:x\mathrel{\cong}y
.
Using
Section

2.2
, we can define
displayed adjoint equivalences
over adjoint equivalences in the usual way.

Just like categories,
displayed categories come with a notion of univalence.

{defiC}

[
[
7
, Definition 5.3]
]

Suppose that we have a displayed category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
.
We say that
\mathsf{D}
is
univalent

if for all objects
x,y:\mathsf{C}
with
p:x=y
and objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
over
x
and
y
respectively,
the map
\overline{x}=\overline{y}\rightarrow\overline{x}\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{idtoiso}_{x,y}(p)}}\overline{y}
defined by path induction is an equivalence of types.

If a displayed category
\mathsf{D}
is univalent,
then each fiber category
\mathsf{D}[x]
is univalent as well.
If both
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
are univalent,
then
\int{\mathsf{D}}
also is univalent.
The next notion of interest is that of
cleavings
.
Note that in the following definition we do not refer to equality of objects,
but instead we only talk about objects in
\mathsf{D}
over some object in
\mathsf{C}
.

{defiC}

[
[
7
, Example 3.18]
]

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a displayed category over
\mathsf{C}
.
A morphism
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
over
f:x\rightarrow y
is said to be
Cartesian

if for all objects
\overline{w}:{\mathsf{D}}(w)
and morphisms
g:w\rightarrow x
and
\overline{h}:\overline{w}\rightarrow_{g\cdot f}\overline{y}

there is a unique morphism
\overline{g}:\overline{w}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{x}
making the triangle below commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

A
cleaving
for
\mathsf{D}
is a map assigning to each morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)

an object
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
and a Cartesian morphism
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
.
The morphism
\overline{f}
is the
Cartesian lift
of
f
,
and if
\mathsf{D}
is equipped with a cleaving, then we say that
\mathsf{D}
is a
cloven fibration
.

A displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{F}
is said to be
Cartesian
if it maps Cartesian morphisms to Cartesian morphisms.

If we have a displayed category
\mathsf{D}
equipped with a cleaving,
then every morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
induces a functor
f^{*}:\mathsf{D}[y]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}[x]

[
56
]
.
This functor sends every object
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
to the domain of the Cartesian lift of
f
along
\overline{y}
.

Note that we phrased
Section

2.2
as structure on a displayed category.
An alternative would be to only assume that Cartesian lifts merely exist,
which would give rise to the notion of
fibrations
.
However, since Cartesian lifts are unique up to isomorphism, they are actually unique if
\mathsf{D}
is univalent.
A univalent displayed category
\mathsf{D}
is thus a cloven fibration if it is a fibration.

We also use
fiberwise limits
in displayed categories.
Our focus is on several special cases of fiberwise limits, namely the following.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a displayed category over
\mathsf{C}
with a cleaving.
We say that

•

\mathsf{D}
is equipped with a
fiberwise terminal object

if for all
x:\mathsf{C}
the category
\mathsf{D}[x]
is equipped with a terminal object
and if
f^{*}:\mathsf{D}[y]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}[x]
preserves terminal objects for all
f:x\rightarrow y
;

•

\mathsf{D}
is equipped with
fiberwise binary products

if for all
x:\mathsf{C}
the category
\mathsf{D}[x]
is equipped with binary products
and if
f^{*}:\mathsf{D}[y]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}[x]
preserves binary products for all
f:x\rightarrow y
;

•

\mathsf{D}
is equipped with
fiberwise equalizers

if for all
x:\mathsf{C}
the category
\mathsf{D}[x]
is equipped with equalizers
and if
f^{*}:\mathsf{D}[y]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}[x]
preserves equalizers for all
f:x\rightarrow y
.

Since fiber categories are univalent if
\mathsf{D}
is so,

Proposition

1
allows us to conclude that the type of terminal objects for
\mathsf{D}[x]
is a proposition if
\mathsf{D}
is univalent.
We can say the same for binary products and equalizers,
and thus we get an analogue of
Proposition

1
for the fiberwise limits that we consider in this paper.

Proposition 5
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a univalent displayed category over a category
\mathsf{C}
.

•

The type of fiberwise terminal objects for
\mathsf{D}
is a proposition.

•

The type expressing that
\mathsf{D}
is equipped with fiberwise binary products, is a proposition.

•

The type expressing that
\mathsf{D}
is equipped with fiberwise equalizers, is a proposition.

In this paper, we also use the
Beck–Chevalley condition
.
This condition is related to interpretation of quantifiers as adjoints in categorical logic
[
63
,
64
]
.
More specifically, dependent sums and dependent products are interpreted as left adjoints and right adjoints of weakening respectively.
The Beck–Chevalley condition expresses that quantifiers are preserved by substitution.
While we formulate the Beck–Chevalley condition for arbitrary squares of functors,
we only use it in the case that each of the involved functors is of the shape
s^{*}:\mathsf{D}[y]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}[x]

for some cloven fibration
\mathsf{D}
and some morphism
s:x\rightarrow y
.

{defi}

Suppose that we have a square of functors that commutes up to natural isomorphism as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{4}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

In addition, suppose that we have adjunctions
\mathsf{L}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.02295pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.02295pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.02295pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{1}}$}}}\hskip-12.02295pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[12.02295pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta_{1}}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\mathsf{G}_{1}

and
\mathsf{L}_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{12.02295pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-12.02295pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[12.02295pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{2}}$}}}\hskip-12.02295pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[12.02295pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta_{2}}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\mathsf{G}_{2}
.
This square satisfies the
Beck–Chevalley condition for left adjoints

if the following natural transformation is an isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{L}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{L}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{G}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{L}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{L}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{G}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{L}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{L}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}\vartriangleright(\mathsf{F}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{L}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{L}_{1}\vartriangleleft\tau\vartriangleright\mathsf{L}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{L}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{1})\vartriangleleft\varepsilon_{2}}

Analogously, we define the Beck–Chevalley condition for right adjoints.

Let us finish this section with an example of a displayed category.
The example of interest in this paper is the
arrow category
.

{exa}

[
[
7
, Example 3.18]
]

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category.
We define a displayed category
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
over
\mathsf{C}
as follows.

•

The objects over
y:\mathsf{C}
are pairs
x:\mathsf{C}
together with a morphism
g:x\rightarrow y
.

•

The morphisms over
f:y_{1}\rightarrow y_{2}
from
g_{1}:x_{1}\rightarrow y_{1}
to
g_{2}:x_{2}\rightarrow y_{2}

are morphisms
h:x_{1}\rightarrow x_{2}
making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

If
\mathsf{C}
is univalent, then so is
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.
The total category, which we denote by
\mathsf{C}^{\rightarrow}
, is the arrow category of
\mathsf{C}
,
and the forgetful functor
\pi_{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}:\mathsf{C}^{\rightarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}
sends every morphism to its codomain.
The fiber category
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}[x]
is equivalent to the slice category
\mathsf{C}/x
.
Cartesian morphisms in
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
correspond to pullback squares,
and if
\mathsf{C}
has pullbacks,
then we can equip
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
with a cleaving
[
56
, Proposition 1.1.6]
.
Given a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathsf{C}
,
the fiber functor
f^{*}:\mathsf{C}/y\rightarrow\mathsf{C}/x
sends morphisms
g:w\rightarrow y
to the pullback of
f
and
g
.
If
\mathsf{C}
is finitely complete, then
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has all fiberwise finite limits.

Every functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
gives rise to a displayed functor
\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}:\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
over
\mathsf{F}
.
This functor sends morphisms
g:x\rightarrow y
to
\mathsf{F}(g):\mathsf{F}(x)\rightarrow\mathsf{F}(y)
.
Finally,
every natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
induces a displayed natural transformation
\tau^{\downarrow}:\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{G}^{\downarrow}
.

3.
Models of Type Theory in Univalent Foundations

Before phrasing internal language theorems of various classes of univalent categories,
we first discuss the categorical semantics of dependent type theory.
In this discussion, our main question is what would be suitable categorical structures to model dependent type theory in univalent categories.
We start by recalling
categories with families

[
41
, Definition 1]
,
which were used by Clairambault and Dybjer to prove their internal language theorems
[
31
, Theorem 6.1]
.
Let us write
\mathsf{Fam}
for the category whose objects are pairs of a set
A:\mathsf{Set}

and a family
B:A\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
.

{defiC}

[
[
41
, Definition 1]
]

A
category with families
(CwF) consists of

•

a category
\mathsf{C}
with a terminal object
\langle\rangle
;

•

a functor
\mathsf{T}:\mathsf{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Fam}
that sends
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
to a set
\mathsf{Ty}_{\Gamma}
with a family
\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}:\mathsf{Ty}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
;

•

for all
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
and
A:\mathsf{Ty}_{\Gamma}
a context extension
\Gamma.A:\mathsf{C}
.

The context comprehension operation needs to satisfy a universal property,
which can be found in the literature
[
31
, Definition 2.2]
.

Here the category
\mathsf{C}
is the category of contexts and context morphisms,
inhabitants of
\mathsf{Ty}_{\Gamma}
and
\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(A)
represent types in context
\Gamma
and terms of type
A
respectively.
Throughout the years CwFs have been used to define various models of type theory such as the cubical sets model
[
23
]
.
One key feature of categories with families is that they can be presented as a generalized algebraic theory
[
41
,
22
]
.
As a consequence, one can directly define the initial CwF (i.e., the syntax) using quotient inductive-inductive types
[
12
,
10
]
.
We can also use categories with families as a general framework to define various notions of models in categorical logic
[
30
]
.

However, the notion of category with families that we gave in
Section

3
,
does not give a suitable framework to describe internal models of homotopy type theory
[
62
]
,
and in particular the internal language of univalent categories.
To understand why, let us consider the (univalent) category
\mathsf{Set}
of sets.
One might hope that
\mathsf{Set}
gives rise to a model of type theory in which types in the empty context are the same as sets,
but this is not the case if we were to use categories with families.
This is because in
Section

3
we require there to be a set of types in every context
\Gamma
.
That requirement gets violated by the univalence axiom, which prevents the type of sets from being a set itself.
The same issue occurs when showing that every univalent category with finite limits gives rise to a category with families,
because the type of objects in a univalent category forms, in general, only a 1-type by
Section

2.1
.
For the same reason, one needs to assume uniqueness of identity proofs to construct the set model when defining the syntax of type theory as a quotient inductive-inductive type
[
12
, Section 6]
.

All in all,
we either need to alter the notion of category with families to remove the restriction that types form a set,
or use another categorical structure to model dependent types in univalent categories.
In the categorical structure used, the collection of types in each context should not be required to form a set.
A wide variety of categorical models of dependent types,
such as categories with families
[
41
]
, categories with attributes
[
28
]
, C-systems
[
29
,
90
]
, and natural models
[
15
]
,
do not satisfy this requirement.

3.1.
Comprehension Categories

One categorical structure for interpreting dependent types that does satisfy our demands,
is given by
comprehension categories

[
55
,
56
]
.
We discuss other possibilities in
Remark

6
.
The notion of comprehension category is centered around that of
fibrations

[
47
]
,
which are used to model the category of contexts, the category of types depending on contexts, and the substitution operation on types.
The other core ingredient of comprehension categories is given by the
comprehension functor
,
which models context extension.
Following Ahrens and Lumsdaine, we define comprehension categories using displayed categories
[
7
, Definition 6.1]
.

{defiC}

[
[
55
, Definition 4.1]
]

A
comprehension category
is given by

•

a category
\mathsf{C}
with a terminal object
\langle\rangle
;

•

a displayed category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
;

•

a cleaving on
\mathsf{D}
;

•

a displayed functor
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
over the identity

such that
\chi
preserves Cartesian morphisms.
Pictorially, we represent this data as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

A comprehension category is called
full

if
\chi
is a fully faithful functor.
We say that a comprehension category is
univalent

if both
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
are univalent.

Since
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is a displayed functor over the identity,
it maps objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
to objects in
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
over
x
,
which are morphisms
f:y\rightarrow x
.
Every comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

gives rise to a triangle of functors, as depicted below, that commutes up to definitional equality.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int{\mathsf{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\rightarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cod}}

In the remainder of this paper, we use univalent full comprehension categories as our categorical model for dependent types.
The notion of univalent full comprehension category is less restrictive than that of a CwF,
because the former does not require the types to form a set.
Every univalent comprehension category gives rise to a pseudofunctor
\mathsf{Ty}:\mathsf{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\rightarrow\mathsf{UnivCat}

which lands in the bicategory of univalent categories.
Since the objects of a univalent category generally only form a 1-type,
the types in some context
\Gamma
are only required to be a 1-type.
This additional flexibility is advantageous, because it allows us to define the comprehension category of sets.

{exa}

We have the following comprehension category.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Set}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}^{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Set}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

This comprehension category is both full and univalent.

We can generalize
Section

3.1
to arbitrary univalent categories with finite limits.

{exa}

If
\mathsf{C}
is a univalent category with finite limits,
then we have the following comprehension category.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

Note that
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is a fibration if and only if
\mathsf{C}
has pullbacks
[
56
]
.
The resulting comprehension category is full and univalent,
and we denote it by
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.

It is important to note that we do not assume that the cleaving in
Section

3.1
is split.
While in set-theoretic foundations splitness is necessary to model dependent type theory,
this is not so if we assume univalence.
There are rules in type theory saying that the substitution
\operatorname{id}^{*}(A)
is equal to
A
in type theory,
and that substitution commutes with composition of context morphisms.
In the context of fibrations,
these rules translate to the Cartesian lift of the identity being equal to the identity,
and the Cartesian lift of a composition being equal to the composition of the Cartesian lifts.
These two requirements only hold up to isomorphism in general,
and they only hold up to equality if the fibration is split.
However, if we assume that the involved categories are univalent,
then we always have the desired identities,
because every isomorphism gives rise to an identity.
The coherence problem remains:
since one would not force the initial model to be a set,
there could be multiple ways to prove that two types are equal.
One can solve this problem for the groupoid-syntax of type theory with a universe and
\Pi
-types
[
11
, Theorem 16]
.

Remark 6
.

Finally, let us remark that there are other notions of models of dependent types that also satisfy our demands,
such as
generalized category with families

[
34
, Definition 10]
,
also known as
judgmental dependent type theories

[
33
, Definition 3.0.1]
,
and
groupoid categories with families

[
11
, Definition 19]
.
In fact, the bicategory of generalized categories with families and of comprehension categories are biequivalent
[
34
, Theorem 3]
.
The notion of groupoid category with families is in spirit close to CwFs,
and they give us a syntactic viewpoint on higher-dimensional models of type theory.
Other alternatives are
display map categories

[
86
]
,
type categories

[
77
]
, and
D-categories

[
43
]
.
A 2-categorical comparison between various notions of categorical model for dependent type theory is given by Ahrens et al.
[
5
]
.
The reason why we use comprehension categories is because the comprehension functor plays a prominent role in our proof
(see
Proposition

14
).

3.2.
Interpreting Type Theory in Comprehension Categories

Let us finish this section by recalling how to interpret type theory with explicit substitution
[
37
,
36
]
in a comprehension category
\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.
Specifically, we introduce notation that allow us to reason type theoretically about comprehension categories
meaning that we discuss how contexts, context morphisms, types, and terms are interpreted.
We also discuss various operations on types and terms,
such as substitution and variables.
Our development follows the work by Najmaei et al.
[
74
]
,
and in particular, we consider a coercion operation on terms
that allows us to coerce terms of type
A
to terms of type
B
if we have a morphism from
A
to
B
.
The material in this section can be used to interpret the groupoid syntax of type theory in comprehension categories
[
11
]
.

Contexts, types, and terms.

We start by describing how the basic judgments are interpreted.
We interpret objects and morphisms in
\mathsf{C}
as contexts and context morphisms respectively,
and types in a context
\Gamma
are given by objects
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
.
Terms of type
A
in context
\Gamma
are interpreted as sections of
\pi_{A}:\Gamma.A\rightarrow\Gamma
,
i.e., a morphism
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma.A
such that the following composition is the identity.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}

We write
\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(A)
for the collection of terms of type
A
in context
\Gamma
.

Operations on types.

Next we look at substitution of types.
Given a context morphism
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta
and a type
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Delta)
,
we interpret the substitution
s^{*}(A):{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
as the domain of the following Cartesian lift.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

We have isomorphisms
\operatorname{id}_{\Delta}^{*}(A)\mathrel{\cong}A
and
(s_{1}\cdot s_{2})^{*}(A)\mathrel{\cong}s_{2}^{*}(s_{1}^{*}(A))

in the fiber categories,
because taking Cartesian lifts is pseudofunctorial.
In addition,
every morphism
f:A\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}B
gives rise to a morphism
s^{*}(f):s^{*}(A)\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}s^{*}(B)
.
If
\mathsf{D}
is univalent, then these isomorphisms induce actual identities.

Operations on contexts.

The empty context is the terminal object
\langle\rangle
and we have unique maps
\diamond_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\rightarrow\langle\rangle
.
Context extension is interpreted using the functor
\chi
:
if we have a type
A
in context
\Gamma
,
then from
\chi(A)
we obtain a context
\Gamma.A
and a morphism
\pi_{A}:\Gamma.A\rightarrow\Gamma
.
To interpret extension of context morphisms,
we assume that we have a context morphism
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta

and a term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(s^{*}(A))
.
We define
\langle s,t\rangle:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta.A
to be
t\cdot\chi(s)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.s^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Operations on terms.

To interpret substitution of terms,
we use that
\chi
preserves Cartesian morphisms.
We thus know that the following square is a pullback,
since
\overline{s}
is Cartesian.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.s^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

If we have a context morphism
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta

and a term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(A)
,
we get another term
s^{*}(t):\mathsf{Tm}_{\Delta}(s^{*}(A))
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.s^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

We also use that
\chi
preserves Cartesian morphisms to interpret the variable rule.
More specifically, if we have a type
A
in context
\Gamma
,
we have a term
\mathsf{q}
in context
\Gamma.A
of type
\pi^{*}(A)
,
which is defined as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Coercion of terms.

Finally, we consider a coercion operation on terms.
Following Coraglia and Emmenegger,
a morphism
\overline{f}:A\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}B
in
\mathsf{D}
indicates that we have a coercion from
A
to
B

[
35
]
.
Note that such morphisms are not unique in general.
We can use such morphisms to coerce terms of type
A
to terms of type
B
.
To do so,
we first observe that morphisms
\overline{f}:A\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}B
in
\mathsf{D}

give rise to morphisms
\chi(f):\Gamma.A\rightarrow\Gamma.B

making the following triangle commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}

If we have types
A,B:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)

and a morphism
f:A\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}_{\Gamma}}B
,
then every term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(A)
gives rise to a term
t\mathbin{\uparrow}f:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(B)

defined by the following composition.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(f)}

Coercion on terms is functorial
in the sense that we have
t\mathbin{\uparrow}\operatorname{id}_{A}=t

and
t\mathbin{\uparrow}(f\cdot g)=(t\mathbin{\uparrow}f)\mathbin{\uparrow}g
.

4.
The Bicategory of Univalent Full Comprehension Categories

Now we start our study of internal language theorems for univalent categories.
Following Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
]
we express our internal language theorems as biequivalences
between bicategories of models and of type theories.
Since we use univalent full comprehension categories as our models,
the bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
of univalent full comprehension categories acts as the bicategory of models throughout this paper.
In this section, we take a closer look at
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
, and we have two goals:
define this bicategory and prove that it is univalent.

Before we do so, let us recall the bicategory of comprehension categories defined by Coraglia and Emmenegger
[
34
, Definition 7]
.
The 1-cells
from a comprehension category
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}

are given by
lax morphisms
,
which consist of a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
that preserves terminal objects,
a Cartesian displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{F}
,
and a displayed natural transformation
\mathsf{F}_{\chi}
over the identity as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\chi}}

Lax morphisms preserve substitution up to isomorphism
and context extension up to a morphism.
A 2-cell from
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
to
(\mathsf{G},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{G}_{\chi})

is given by
a natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
,
a displayed natural transformation
\overline{\tau}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathsf{G}}
over
\tau

such that the compositions below are equal.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}_{\chi}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\downarrow}}

The bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
that we use, is slightly different from the bicategory defined by Coraglia and Emmenegger
[
34
]
.
Instead of all comprehension categories,
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
only has univalent full comprehension categories as objects.
Following Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
]
, we also restrict the morphisms: instead of all lax morphisms,
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
only contains pseudo morphisms.
Here we say that a lax morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
is a
pseudo morphism
if
\mathsf{F}_{\chi}
is a natural isomorphism
[
36
]
.
Such morphisms thus preserve context extension up to isomorphism.
We do not pose any restriction on the 2-cells.

To construct
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
, we use
displayed bicategories

[
3
, Definition 6.1]
,
which allow us to construct this bicategory and to prove its univalence modularly.
These techniques have also been used to construct the bicategories of
monoidal categories
[
94
]
,
double categories
[
91
]
,
and monads
[
92
]
.

We start this section by recalling some basic notions and theorems about displayed bicategories.
After that we use these techniques to define the bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
of univalent full comprehension categories.
We finish by proving that
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
is univalent.

4.1.
Displayed Bicategories

To construct the bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
,
we make extensive use of displayed bicategories.
When we construct some bicategory using displayed bicategories,
we start with some given bicategory
\mathsf{B}

and we define a displayed bicategory over it.
The objects, 1-cells, and 2-cells in this displayed bicategory describe
the structure and properties to be added to the objects, 1-cells, and 2-cells in
\mathsf{B}
.
Each displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
gives rise to a total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{D}}
,
whose objects are objects in
\mathsf{B}
together with the structure specified by the displayed bicategory.
The key feature of displayed bicategories is that we can modularly reason about the total bicategory:
we can prove that
\int{\mathsf{D}}
is univalent by solely considering
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{D}
,
and we can construct adjoint equivalences in
\int{\mathsf{D}}
from adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{D}
.

To construct
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
, we start with the bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}
of univalent categories, and functors.
We add the necessary structures to obtain full comprehension categories in layers,
and each layer is described by a displayed bicategory.
For instance, we construct a displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{UnivCat}
whose objects over
\mathsf{C}
are fibrations.
By showing that each of these displayed bicategories are univalent,
we get a simple and modular proof that
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
is univalent.
In addition,
we use the notion of displayed adjoint equivalence to describe adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
.

Displayed bicategories generalize displayed categories to the 2-dimensional setting.
Intuitively, a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over a bicategory
\mathsf{B}

represents structure and properties to be added to the objects, 1-cells, and 2-cells of
\mathsf{B}
.
Every displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
gives rise to a
total bicategory

\int{\mathsf{D}}

and a
forgetful pseudofunctor

\pi_{:}\int{\mathsf{D}}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
that forgets the added structure and properties.
More precisely, we define displayed bicategories as follows.

{defiC}

[
[
3
, Definition 6.1]
]

A
displayed bicategory

\mathsf{D}
over a bicategory
\mathsf{B}
is given by

•

a type
{\mathsf{D}}(x)
of
objects over
x
for all objects
x:\mathsf{B}
;

•

a type
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
of
morphisms over
f
for all 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathsf{B}
and objects
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
over
x
and
y
respectively;

•

a set
\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
of
2-cells over
\tau
for all 2-cells
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
and morphisms
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{y}
over
f
and
g
respectively.

We write
\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{x}}
and
\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{f}}
for the identity 1-cells and 2-cells respectively,
and we write
\overline{f}\cdot\overline{g}
and
\overline{\tau}\cdot\overline{\theta}
for their vertical compositions.
A complete list of operations and laws is found in the literature
[
3
, Definition 6.1]
.

Every displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
gives rise to a
total bicategory

\int{\mathsf{D}}
,
whose objects are pairs
x:\mathsf{D}
and
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)

and a
forgetful pseudofunctor

\pi_{\mathsf{D}}:\int{\mathsf{D}}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
.

To understand this notion, let us consider an example,
namely the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
of terminal objects over the bicategory of univalent categories.
Here the objects over a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
are terminal objects for
\mathsf{C}
,
the 1-cells over a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
are proofs that witness that
\mathsf{F}
preserves terminal objects
and the type of 2-cells over a natural transformations
\tau
is the unit type.
The total bicategory of this displayed bicategory is the bicategory of univalent categories with a terminal object,
and the forgetful pseudofunctor maps a category with a terminal object to its underlying category.
Yet another example is the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Mon}}
of monoidal structures over
\mathsf{UnivCat}

[
94
]
.
The objects over
\mathsf{C}
are monoidal structures for
\mathsf{C}
,
the 1-cells over
\mathsf{F}
are structures that witness that
\mathsf{F}
is a lax monoidal functor,
and the 2-cells over
\tau
are proofs that
\tau
is a monoidal transformation.
The bicategory of univalent monoidal categories is the total bicategory of this displayed bicategory,
and we also have the forgetful pseudofunctor,
which maps every monoidal category to its underlying category.

Next we look at how to construct adjoint equivalences and invertible 2-cells in the total bicategory.
Here the main idea is that an adjoint equivalence in
\int{\mathsf{D}}
can be obtained from an adjoint equivalence in
\mathsf{B}

together with some additional structure in
\mathsf{D}

and similarly for invertible 2-cells.
The necessary extra structure is given by
displayed adjoint equivalences
and
displayed invertible 2-cells
respectively.
These notions are defined similarly to their non-displayed versions,
except that we use displayed objects, 1-cells, and 2-cells.

{defiC}

[
[
3
, Definitions 7.1 and 7.2]
]

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{B}
.
Given 1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
and an invertible 2-cell
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
,
a
displayed invertible 2-cell
over
\tau
from
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
to
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{y}

consists of displayed 2-cells
\overline{\tau}:\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
and
\overline{\tau^{-1}}:\overline{g}\Rightarrow_{\tau^{-1}}\overline{f}

that compose to the identity.
We denote the type of invertible 2-cells over
\tau
by
\overline{f}\cong_{\tau}\overline{g}
.

Given a 1-cell
\mathsf{l}:x\rightarrow y
,
a
displayed adjoint equivalence

over an adjoint equivalence
\mathsf{l}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\mathsf{r}

from
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
to
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)

consists of displayed 1-cells
\overline{\mathsf{l}}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{l}}\overline{y}
and
\overline{\mathsf{r}}:\overline{y}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{r}}\overline{x}

and displayed invertible 2-cells
\overline{\eta}:\operatorname{id}\Rightarrow_{\eta}\overline{\mathsf{l}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{r}}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}:\overline{\mathsf{r}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{l}}\Rightarrow_{\varepsilon}\operatorname{id}

satisfying the usual triangle laws.
The type of adjoint equivalences over
l
is denoted by
\overline{x}\simeq_{\mathsf{l}}\overline{y}
.

Every displayed invertible 2-cell
\overline{\tau}
over
\tau
gives rise to an invertible 2-cell
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
in
\int{\mathsf{D}}
.
In addition, if we have a displayed adjoint equivalence
\overline{\mathsf{l}}
over an adjoint equivalence
\mathsf{l}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\mathsf{r}
,
then we get an adjoint equivalence in
\int{\mathsf{D}}
whose left adjoint is given by
(\mathsf{l},\overline{\mathsf{l}})
.

Every displayed 1-cell in
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
is a displayed adjoint equivalence,
because every adjoint equivalence of categories preserves terminal objects.
It also is the case that every displayed 2-cell in
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
is invertible,
because the type of 2-cells over
\tau
is the unit type.
Adjoint equivalences and invertible 2-cells in the total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}

thus correspond to adjoint equivalences and natural isomorphisms respectively.
While every displayed 2-cell in
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Mon}}
is invertible,
displayed 1-cells in
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Mon}}
are not necessarily adjoint equivalences.
Instead, displayed adjoint equivalence over
\mathsf{F}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\mathsf{G}
,
correspond to structures that witness that
\mathsf{F}
is a strong monoidal functor.

Displayed bicategories also allow us to modularly prove that a bicategory is univalent.
Instead of proving directly that the total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{D}}
is univalent,
it is often simpler to consider
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{D}
separately
and to prove that both
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{D}
are univalent.
For this purpose, we define
univalent displayed bicategories
.

{defiC}

[
[
3
, Definition 7.3]
]

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{B}
.

•

We say that
\mathsf{D}
is
locally univalent

if for all objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
,
1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
with
p:f=g
,
displayed objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
,
and displayed 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{y}

the map
\overline{\mathsf{idtoiso}}^{2,1}_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}:\overline{f}=\overline{g}\rightarrow\overline{f}\cong_{\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,1}_{f,g}(p)}\overline{g}

defined by path induction is an equivalence of types.

•

We say that
\mathsf{D}
is
globally univalent

if for all objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
with
p:x=y

and displayed objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(y)
over
x
and
y
respectively,
the map
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{\overline{x},\overline{y}}:\overline{x}=\overline{y}\rightarrow\overline{x}\simeq_{\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{x,y}(f)}\overline{y}

defined by path induction is an equivalence of types.

•

Finally, we say that
\mathsf{D}
is
univalent
if it is both locally and globally univalent.

Note that in
Section

4.1
it suffices to restrict to identities
rather than arbitrary adjoint equivalences and invertible 2-cells in the base bicategory
\mathsf{B}
.
The resulting definition is equivalent to the one by Ahrens et al.
[
3
, Definition 7.3]
.
The key theorem about displayed univalence is as follows
[
3
, Theorem 7.4]
.

Proposition 7
.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a univalent displayed bicategory over a univalent bicategory
\mathsf{B}
.
Then the total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{D}}
is univalent.

Both
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
and
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Mon}}
are univalent,
and hence their total bicategories are so as well.
Throughout this paper, we are interested in additional properties that displayed bicategories could satisfy.
The first property, which we call
locally preordered
,
says that each type
\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
is a proposition.
The second property, which we call
locally groupoidal
,
says that each displayed 2-cell
\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
over an invertible 2-cell is invertible as well.
Finally, we say that a displayed bicategory
has trivial 2-cells
if the type
\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}

is contractible for all displayed 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{y}
, and 2-cells
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
.
Note that every displayed bicategory with trivial 2-cells is both locally preordered and groupoidal.
The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
has trivial 2-cells,
because the type of 2-cells over
\tau
is the unit type.
While
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Mon}}
is both locally preordered and groupoidal,
it does not have trivial 2-cells,
because not every natural transformation is monoidal.
The concrete examples of displayed bicategories that we consider in the remainder of this paper,
are locally preordered and groupoidal.

We end this recap with several constructions on displayed bicategories.

{exa}

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a bicategory and suppose that we have a type family
\mathsf{P}
on the objects of
\mathsf{B}
.
We also assume that we have a type family
\mathsf{Q}
on triples of objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}

such that
\mathsf{P}(x)
and
\mathsf{P}(y)
hold,
and 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
.
Suppose that
\mathsf{Q}(\operatorname{id}_{x})
holds for all
x:\mathsf{B}
such that
\mathsf{P}(x)
holds
and that
\mathsf{Q}(f\cdot g)
holds whenever
\mathsf{Q}(f)
and
\mathsf{Q}(g)
hold.
We define the
displayed subbicategory

\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
as the displayed bicategory whose

•

objects over
x
are proofs of
\mathsf{P}(x)
;

•

1-cells over
f:x\rightarrow y
are proofs of
\mathsf{Q}(f)
;

•

type of 2-cells over
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
is the unit type.

This displayed bicategory is univalent whenever both
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{Q}
are valued in propositions.
We write
\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
for
\int{\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})}
.
Note that
\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
has trivial 2-cells.

As a special case of the previous example we have the full subbicategory.
Given a type family
\mathsf{P}
on the objects of
\mathsf{B}
,
the
displayed full subbicategory

\mathsf{DFullSub}(\mathsf{P})
is the displayed bicategory whose

•

objects over
x
are proofs of
\mathsf{P}(x)
;

•

type of 1-cells over
f:x\rightarrow y
is the unit type;

•

type of 2-cells over
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
is the unit type.

This displayed bicategory is univalent if the type family
\mathsf{P}
is valued in propositions.
We denote
\int{\mathsf{DFullSub}(\mathsf{P})}
by
\mathsf{FullSub}(\mathsf{P})
.

Suppose that we have displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{B}
.
We define their
product

\mathsf{D}_{1}\times\mathsf{D}_{2}
as the displayed bicategory whose

•

objects over
x:\mathsf{B}
are pairs of
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}_{2}}(x)
;

•

1-cells over
f:x\rightarrow y
from
(\overline{x}_{1},\overline{y}_{1})
to
(\overline{x}_{2},\overline{y}_{2})

are pairs
\overline{f}:\overline{x}_{1}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{x}_{2}
to
\overline{g}:\overline{y}_{1}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}_{2}
;

•

2-cells over
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
from
(\overline{f_{1}},\overline{g_{1}})
to
(\overline{f_{2}},\overline{g_{2}})

are pairs
\overline{\tau}:\overline{f_{1}}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{f_{2}}
and
\overline{\theta}:\overline{g_{1}}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g_{2}}
.

If both
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
are univalent, then so is
\mathsf{D}_{1}\times\mathsf{D}_{2}
.

Let
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
be displayed bicategories over
\mathsf{B}
and
\int{\mathsf{D}_{1}}
respectively.
We define the
\Sigma
-displayed bicategory

\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}_{1}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
as the displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{B}
whose

•

objects over
x:\mathsf{B}
are pairs of
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}(x)
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}_{2}}\>(x,\overline{x})
;

•

1-cells over
f:x\rightarrow y
from
(\overline{x}_{1},\overline{y}_{1})
to
(\overline{x}_{2},\overline{y}_{2})

are pairs
\overline{f}:\overline{x}_{1}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{x}_{2}
to
\overline{g}:\overline{y}_{1}\rightarrow_{(f,\overline{f})}\overline{y}_{2}
;

•

2-cells over
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
from
(\overline{f_{1}},\overline{g_{1}})
to
(\overline{f_{2}},\overline{g_{2}})

are pairs
\overline{\tau}:\overline{f_{1}}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{f_{2}}
and
\overline{\theta}:\overline{g_{1}}\Rightarrow_{(\tau,\overline{\tau})}\overline{g_{2}}
.

If we have univalent displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
that are both locally preordered and groupoidal,
then
\Sigma_{\mathsf{D}_{1}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
is univalent as well.

4.2.
The Construction

Now we construct the bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
using displayed bicategories.
The idea is to build up this bicategory step by step.
Starting with univalent categories,
we layerwise add structures and properties to obtain full univalent comprehension categories.
Each displayed bicategory represents one piece of the necessary data,
and we use different layers to indicate the dependencies between the displayed bicategories.

More specifically, we start with the bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}
of univalent categories.
On top of this bicategory, we define two displayed bicategories:

\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
, which adds a terminal object,
and
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}
, which adds a univalent displayed category (
Section

4.2
).
We take their product, which we denote by
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
,
and we continue by defining the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}
over
\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}
(
Section

4.2
).
Here we select out those displayed categories that are equipped with a cleaving.

Objects of
\int{\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}}
consists of a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with a terminal object
and a univalent displayed category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
with a cleaving.
On top of
\int{\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}}
we define yet another displayed category, namely
\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}
(
Section

4.2
).
This bicategory corresponds to the one defined by Coraglia and Emmenegger
[
34
, Definition 7]

except for the fact that we require the involved categories to be univalent.
We finish the construction by taking a subbicategory to obtain
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
(
Section

4.2
).
A pictorial overview of the construction is found in
Figure

1
.
That diagram shows the total bicategories of each displayed bicategory used to construct
\mathsf{FullCompCat}

and the pseudofunctors between them.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\mathsf{FullCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int{\mathsf{UnivCat}}}

Figure 1.
The displayed bicategories used to construct
\mathsf{FullCompCat}

We start by defining
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
and
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
over
\mathsf{UnivCat}
as
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})

where
\mathsf{P}
expresses that a univalent category has a terminal object
and where
\mathsf{Q}
expresses that a functor preserves terminal objects.

We also define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}
over
\mathsf{UnivCat}
as follows.

•

The objects over univalent categories
\mathsf{C}
are univalent displayed categories
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
.

•

The 1-cells over functors
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}

from a displayed category
\mathsf{D}_{1}
over
\mathsf{C}_{1}

to a displayed category
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{C}_{2}

are displayed functors
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
from
\mathsf{D}_{1}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{F}
.

•

The 2-cells over a natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}

from a displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{F}

to a displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{G}

are displayed natural transformations from
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
to
\overline{\mathsf{G}}
over
\tau
.

We define
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
to be
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}\times\mathsf{UnivDispCat}
.

Explicitly, objects in
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
over
\mathsf{C}
are terminal objects,
and 1-cells over
\mathsf{F}
are proofs that
\mathsf{F}
preserves terminal objects.
The type over 2-cells over a natural transformation
\tau
is the unit type.
The objects of the total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}
consist of a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with a terminal object
\langle\rangle

and a displayed univalent category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
.
Morphisms from
(\mathsf{C}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{1})
to
(\mathsf{C}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{2})
consist of a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
that preserves terminal objects
and a displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{F}
.
For each such morphism we have an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}:\langle\rangle_{1}\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle_{2}

where
\langle\rangle_{1}
and
\langle\rangle_{2}
are the terminal objects of
\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{2}
respectively.
Finally, a 2-cell from
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}})
to
(\mathsf{G},\overline{\mathsf{G}})
consists of a natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}

and a displayed natural transformation
\overline{\tau}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathsf{G}}
over
\tau
.

Next we add a cleaving to the structure.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}
over
\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}
as
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})

where
\mathsf{P}
expresses that a displayed category is equipped with a cleaving
and where
\mathsf{Q}
expresses that a displayed functor is Cartesian.

The objects
\int{\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}}
consist of a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with a terminal object
and a displayed univalent category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
together with a cleaving.
Morphisms from
(\mathsf{C}_{1},\mathsf{D}_{1})
to
(\mathsf{C}_{2},\mathsf{D}_{2})
consist of a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
that preserves terminal objects
and a Cartesian displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{F}
.
The 2-cells in
\int{\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}}
are the same as 2-cells in
\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}
.

Now we define the bicategory of comprehension categories.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}
over
\int{\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}}
as follows.

•

The objects over
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D})
are Cartesian functors
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
over the identity.

•

The 1-cells over
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}})
are displayed natural transformations as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\chi}}

•

The 2-cells over
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
are proofs that the following compositions are equal.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Note that
\int{\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}}
corresponds to the bicategory defined by Coraglia and Emmenegger
[
34
, Definition 7]

with the only difference being that we require the involved categories to be univalent.
We finish the construction by taking a subbicategory.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\int{\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}}
as
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})

where
\mathsf{P}
expresses that a comprehension category is full
and where
\mathsf{Q}
expresses that a lax morphism is a pseudo morphism.
We write
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
for
\int{\mathsf{FullCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}}
.

Now we have the desired bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
,
whose objects are full univalent comprehension categories
and whose morphisms are pseudo morphisms.
We finish this section by verifying that this bicategory is univalent.

Proposition 8
.

The bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
is univalent.

Proof 4.1
.

We repeatedly use
Proposition

7
,
so we need to verify that
\mathsf{UnivCat}
and each of the displayed bicategories defined in this section are univalent.
The bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}
is known to be univalent
[
3
, Proposition 3.19]
.
To prove that
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}
is univalent, we use the same steps as the proof for
\mathsf{UnivCat}
.
The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
is univalent, since it is defined as a subbicategory.
Here we use that the type of terminal objects in a univalent category is a proposition (
Proposition

1
).
Since both
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}
and
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
are univalent,
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
is univalent as well.
Next we note that
\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}
also is univalent, because it is defined as a subbicategory
and because the type of cleavings for a univalent displayed category is a proposition.
For a proof that the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}
is univalent, we refer the reader to the formalization.
Finally,
\mathsf{FullCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}
is defined as a subbicategory as well, and thus it is univalent.
All in all, we conclude that
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
is univalent.

5.
Basic Type Formers for Comprehension Categories

Up to now we have constructed the univalent bicategory
\mathsf{FullCompCat}

whose objects are full univalent comprehension categories.
However, such comprehension categories only model the basic judgments of Martin-Löf type theory,
and they do not interpret any type former.
For internal language theorems, we also need to include the relevant types in our models.

Let us focus on the internal language of univalent categories with finite limits for now.
Recall that Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
, Theorem 6.1]
constructed
a biequivalence between the bicategory of categories with finite limits and democratic CwFs
that support extensional identity types and
\Sigma
-types.
For comprehension categories,
we consider democracy and similar type formers,
namely unit types, binary product types, equalizer types, and
\Sigma
-types.
We call comprehension categories that support all these type formers
univalent full democratic finite limit comprehension categories
,
or, for short,
DFL comprehension categories
.

Note that our choice of type formers differs from Clairambault and Dybjer.
While we explicitly require unit types and binary product types in the structure,
they derive these types using democracy and
\Sigma
-types respectively.
In addition, we use equalizer types instead of extensional identity types,
which are equivalent
[
56
, Theorem 10.5.10]
.
The reason for these differences is that they slightly simplify the proofs in
Section

6
.

The main goal in this section is to construct the univalent bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
of DFL comprehension categories,
and for that we use displayed bicategories.
More specifically, we define a displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
for each of the necessary type formers.
For instance,
we define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Unit}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
,
whose displayed objects over
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
represent unit types for
\chi
.
We also define the displayed bicategories
\mathsf{Prod}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{D}}
,
whose displayed objects over
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
give us fiberwise binary products and equalizers.
We use fiberwise binary products and equalizers to interpret product types and extensional identity types respectively.
In addition,
we define the displayed bicategories
