\mathsf{Sigma}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}
.
Displayed objects in
\mathsf{Sigma}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\chi
give us strong
\Sigma
-types for
\chi
,
and objects over
\chi
in
\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}
are proofs that
\chi
is democratic.
An overview of the construction can be found in
Figure

2
.
We finish this section with a sufficient condition for when a 1-cell in this bicategory is an adjoint equivalence.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Unit}_{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Prod}_{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Sigma}_{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{FullCompCat}}

Figure 2.
Construction of
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}

We start by defining the displayed bicategories
\mathsf{Prod}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{D}}
directly.
To do so, we use fiberwise limits (
Section

2.2
).

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Prod}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}

as the subbicategory
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
.
Here
\mathsf{P}
expresses that
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has fiberwise binary products,
and
\mathsf{Q}
expresses that, given a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
,
the fiber functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}[x]
preserves binary products for all
x:\mathsf{C}_{1}
.

We also define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}

as the subbicategory
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
.
Here
\mathsf{P}
expresses that
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has fiberwise equalizers,
and
\mathsf{Q}
expresses that, given a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
,
the functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}[x]
preserves equalizers for all
x:\mathsf{C}_{1}
.

The comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
from
Section

3.1
supports both binary product types and equalizer types.
The fiber of
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
along
x
is the slice category
\mathsf{C}/x
,
which inherits finite limits from
\mathsf{C}
.
Given a morphism
f:x_{1}\rightarrow x_{2}

the functor
f^{*}
sends morphisms
g:y\rightarrow x_{2}
to the pullback of
g
along
f
.
Since this functor is right adjoint to composition,
it preserves all limits,
and in particular, binary products and equalizers.

For unit types, we require something stronger than just fiberwise terminal objects,
since we also require that the comprehension functor preserves terminal objects.
To understand why, suppose that we have a full comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

and that
\mathsf{D}
has a fiberwise terminal object
\mathds{1}
.
We write
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}
for the terminal object in the fiber
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
.
We have two ways to interpret terms of type
A
in context
\Gamma
in such a comprehension category:
either as sections of
\pi_{A}

or as morphisms from
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}
to
A
in
\mathsf{D}
over the identity.
If we require that the comprehension category is full and that
\chi
preserves terminal objects,
then these two interpretations agree.
Note that
\chi
maps a terminal object
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
to a terminal object
if and only if
\pi_{\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}}
is an isomorphism.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Unit}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}

as the subbicategory
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
.
Here
\mathsf{P}
expresses that
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has fiberwise terminal objects
and that the morphism
\pi_{\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}}
is an isomorphism for every
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
,
and
\mathsf{Q}
expresses that, given a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
,
the functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}[x]
preserves terminal objects for all
x:\mathsf{C}_{1}
.

If
\mathsf{C}
is a category with finite limits,
then
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports unit types.
The slice category
\mathsf{C}/x
always has a terminal object,
namely the identity
\operatorname{id}:x\rightarrow x
.
We denote this terminal object by
\mathds{1}_{x}
.
In fact, every isomorphism
f:y\rightarrow x
is a terminal object in
\mathsf{C}/x
.
The functor
f^{*}
preserves terminal objects,
since the pullback of an isomorphism is again an isomorphism.
Finally, the morphism
\pi_{\mathds{1}_{x}}
is an isomorphism,
because it is the identity.

Next we look at
\Sigma
-types, and these are interpreted as left adjoints of weakening
[
64
]
.
In addition, we require the Beck-Chevalley condition for left adjoints (
Section

2.2
) to guarantee stability under substitution.
Since we also want to have first and second projections for our
\Sigma
-types,
we look at
strong
\Sigma
-types

[
56
, Definition 10.5.2]
.

{defi}

A full univalent comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has
\Sigma
-types
if

•

each functor
\pi_{A}^{*}:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma.A)
has a left adjoint
\Sigma_{A}
with unit
\eta
and counit
\varepsilon

where
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
;

•

the Beck–Chevalley condition for left adjoints holds for every pullback square.

In addition, we say that
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has
strong
\Sigma
-types
if
for all contexts
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
and types
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
and
B:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma.A)

the following map is an isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi_{A}^{*}(\Sigma_{A}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\Sigma_{A}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma.A.\eta(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}^{*}}

Finally, we define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Sigma}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
as
\mathsf{DFullSub}(\mathsf{P})

where
\mathsf{P}
says that a full univalent comprehension category has strong
\Sigma
-types.

Note that in
Section

5
we do not require that the morphisms preserve
\Sigma
-types.
This is automatic since we have an isomorphism
\Gamma.A.B\mathrel{\cong}\Gamma.\Sigma_{A}B

[
31
, Proposition 3.5]
.
In addition, the type expressing that a full univalent comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has strong
\Sigma
-types, is a proposition.
This is because the type of left adjoints for a functor is a proposition
[
4
, Lemma 5.2]
.

The comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports strong
\Sigma
-types.
Since the functor
f^{*}
maps morphisms to their pullback along
f
,
it is right adjoint to the composition functor.
Concretely,
if we have
f:y\rightarrow x
,
then the functor
\Sigma_{f}
maps
g:z\rightarrow y
to their composition
g\cdot f:z\rightarrow x
.
One can show that the Beck–Chevalley condition is satisfied for each pullback square
[
82
,
83
]
.
Note that the resulting
\Sigma
-types are strong,
because the morphism
\Gamma.A.B\rightarrow\Gamma.\Sigma_{A}B

is equal to the identity,
and thus an isomorphism.

Finally, we consider democracy for comprehension categories
[
31
, Definition 2.6]
.
Roughly speaking, democracy for comprehension categories expresses that the contexts are generated by the types,
and one can intuitively capture this idea with the following phrase:
a comprehension category is democratic if every context has a representative.
We make this idea formal in the following definition.

{defi}

A full univalent comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

is called
democratic

if for all contexts
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}

there is a type
\widehat{\Gamma}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle

and an isomorphism
\gamma_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Gamma}
.
We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
as
\mathsf{DFullSub}(\mathsf{P})

where
\mathsf{P}
says that a full univalent comprehension category is democratic.

Note that the comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is democratic,
because every
x:\mathsf{C}
is represented by the morphism
\diamond:x\rightarrow\langle\rangle
.
Note that an equivalent way to formulate democracy is by saying that
\chi[\langle\rangle]
is split essentially surjective.
Let us make a couple of remarks on this definition.
First, the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}
is univalent.
This is because being democratic is a proposition.
Note that we use that
\mathsf{D}
is univalent in the proof.

Proposition 9
.

The type expressing that a full univalent comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

is democratic,
is a proposition.

Proof 5.1
.

Suppose that we have a context
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
, types
A_{1},A_{2}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
,
and isomorphisms
\gamma_{1}:\Gamma\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle.A_{1}

and
\gamma_{2}:\Gamma\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle.A_{2}
.
We must show that the pairs
(A_{1},\gamma_{1})
and
(A_{2},\gamma_{2})
are equal.
Since
\mathsf{D}
is univalent,
it suffices to construct an isomorphism
i
that makes the following triangle commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle.i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}

Note that we have the following isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}

Since
\chi
is fully faithful,
we get the desired isomorphism
i:A_{1}\mathrel{\cong}A_{2}
.

Second, we do not require the morphisms to preserve democracy,
whereas Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
, Definition 3.6]
add such a requirement.
This requirement is in fact redundant, as witnessed by the following proposition.

Proposition 10
.

Suppose that we have democratic full comprehension categories
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
and
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
,
and a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from
\chi_{1}
to
\chi_{2}
.
Then
for each
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}_{1}

there is an isomorphism
d_{\Gamma}:\mathsf{F}(\widehat{\Gamma})\mathrel{\cong}\diamond_{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}^{*}(\widehat{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)})

making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Gamma})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\widehat{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle.\diamond_{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}^{*}(\widehat{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle.\mathsf{F}(\widehat{\Gamma})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\gamma_{\Gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{F(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\chi}(\widehat{\Gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\diamond_{\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle},\mathsf{q}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle.d_{\Gamma}}

Proof 5.2
.

Note that the maps
\gamma_{F(\Gamma)}
and
\mathsf{F}(\gamma_{\Gamma})
are isomorphisms,
since functors preserve isomorphisms and by definition of democracy.
In addition,
\mathsf{F}_{\chi}(\widehat{\Gamma})
is an isomorphism, because we look at pseudo morphisms.
Finally, one can also show that
\langle\diamond_{\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle},\mathsf{q}\rangle
is an isomorphism using that
\mathsf{F}
preserves terminal objects.
Since
\chi
is fully faithful, there must be a unique isomorphism
d_{\Gamma}
making the diagram above commute.

Now we have all the necessary ingredients to define the bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}

and to prove that it is univalent.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
to be

\mathsf{Unit}_{\mathsf{D}}\times\mathsf{Prod}_{\mathsf{D}}\times\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{D}}\times\mathsf{Sigma}_{\mathsf{D}}\times\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}.

We write
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
for the total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}}
.

Proposition 11
.

The bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
is univalent.

Proof 5.3
.

It suffices to show that each of the displayed bicategories defined in this section is univalent.
Using
Proposition

1
, we get that
\mathsf{Unit}_{\mathsf{D}}
,
\mathsf{Prod}_{\mathsf{D}}
, and
\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{D}}
are univalent.
The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{Sigma}_{\mathsf{D}}
also is univalent,
because the type expressing that a full univalent comprehension category has dependent sums,
is a proposition.
Since we define
\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}
as a full subbicategory, we get from
Proposition

9
that
\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}
is univalent.

Before continuing, we briefly describe the objects, morphisms, and 2-cells in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
.
Objects in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
are democratic full univalent comprehension categories
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

that support unit types, binary product types, equalizer types, and
\Sigma
-types.
We call such comprehension categories DFL comprehension categories for short.
We write
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}
for the terminal object in
{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
,
and we write
A\times B
for the binary product of two types
A,B:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
.
The
\Sigma
-type of
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
and
B:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma.A)
is denoted by
\Sigma_{A}B
,
and, given a context
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
, we write
\widehat{\Gamma}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
for its representative
and
\gamma_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Gamma}
for the isomorphism.
Finally, we have a type
t_{1}=_{\mathsf{ext}}t_{2}
for all terms
t_{1},t_{2}:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(A)
.
This is because
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
give rise to morphisms
\overline{t_{1}},\overline{t_{2}}:\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow A
in
\mathsf{D}
over the identity,
and the type
t_{1}=_{\mathsf{ext}}t_{2}
is defined to be the equalizer of
\overline{t_{1}}
and
\overline{t_{2}}
.
We can show that
t_{1}=_{\mathsf{ext}}t_{2}
is stable under substitution.

A morphism from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}

consists of a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
of full univalent comprehension categories from
\chi_{1}
to
\chi_{2}

such that
\overline{\mathsf{F}}[x]
preserves finite limits for all
x:\mathsf{C}_{1}
.
Finally, 2-cells in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
are the same as 2-cells in
\mathsf{FullCompCat}
.
We can also show that the comprehension category constructed in
Section

3.1
gives rise to democratic full univalent comprehension category.

{exa}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits.
The comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is univalent,
because
\mathsf{C}
is univalent,
and it is full,
because
\operatorname{id}
is fully faithful.
Because the slice category
\mathsf{C}/x
inherit its limits from
\mathsf{C}

and because the functors
f^{*}
are right adjoints,
this comprehension category has fiberwise terminal objects, binary products, and equalizers.
We can also show that
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports strong
\Sigma
-types,
since the left adjoint of
f^{*}
is composition,
and it is democratic,
because every
x:\mathsf{C}
is represented by
\diamond:x\rightarrow\langle\rangle
.
Hence,
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is a DFL comprehension category.

We end this section by looking at adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
.
More specifically, we show that a 1-cell
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
is an adjoint equivalence
if
\mathsf{F}
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
are.
We use this condition in
Section

6
to establish the biequivalence between the bicategory of univalent categories with finite limits and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
.

Proposition 12
.

A 1-cell
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}

is an adjoint equivalence
if
\mathsf{F}
is an adjoint equivalence of categories
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
is a displayed adjoint equivalence over
\mathsf{F}
.

Proof 5.4
.

To prove this proposition,
we use displayed adjoint equivalences to construct adjoint equivalences in total bicategories (
Section

4.1
).
We start by noting that adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{UnivCat}
are the same as adjoint equivalences of categories,
and that displayed adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{UnivDispCat}
are the same as displayed adjoint equivalences of displayed categories.
Next we note that there is a displayed adjoint equivalence in
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{T}}
over each adjoint equivalence in
\mathsf{UnivCat}
,
since equivalences preserve terminal objects.

Now we look at displayed adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}
.
More specifically, we construct a displayed adjoint equivalence in
\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}
over each adjoint equivalence
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}})
in
\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}
.
Here we use
Proposition

4
,
and thus it suffices to only consider identity adjoint equivalences in
\int{\mathsf{UnivDispCat}_{\mathsf{T}}}
.
We can directly construct a displayed adjoint equivalence of the identity,
since the identity functor is Cartesian.

Using the same idea, we can show that a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
in
\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}

is an adjoint equivalence if
\mathsf{F}
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
are adjoint equivalences
and if
\mathsf{F}_{\chi}
is a natural isomorphism.
As a consequence, a 1-cell
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
in
\mathsf{FullCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}
is an adjoint equivalence
if
\mathsf{F}
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
are,
since
\mathsf{F}_{\chi}
is a natural isomorphism by the definition of
\mathsf{FullCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}
.

Finally, since
\mathsf{Sigma}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{Dem}_{\mathsf{D}}
are defined as full subbicategories,
there is a displayed adjoint equivalence in those displayed bicategories over every adjoint equivalence in
\mathsf{FullCompCat}_{\mathsf{D}}
.
For
\mathsf{Unit}_{\mathsf{D}}
,
\mathsf{Prod}_{\mathsf{D}}
, and
\mathsf{Eq}_{\mathsf{D}}
, we use the same idea as for
\mathsf{Cleaving}_{\mathsf{T}}
.
From this, we conclude that a 1-cell
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
is an adjoint equivalence
if
\mathsf{F}
is an adjoint equivalence
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
is a displayed adjoint equivalence over
\mathsf{F}
.

6.
The Internal Language of Categories with Finite Limits

Now we have everything in place to prove our analogue of the first internal language theorem by Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
, Theorem 6.1]
.
More specifically, our goal in this section is to show that
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
is biequivalent to the bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}
of univalent categories with finite limits.
This theorem thus says that the internal language of univalent categories with finite limits is
extensional Martin-Löf type theory with unit types, binary product types, and
\Sigma
-types.
Note that we already showed in
Section

5
how categories with finite limits give rise to DFL comprehension categories.

Let us start this section by recalling the definitions of
\mathsf{FinLim}
and of biequivalences.
Recall that a category has finite limits if it has a terminal object and pullbacks
and that a functor preserves finite limits if it preserves terminal objects and pullbacks.

{defi}

We define the bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}
as the subbicategory
\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
of
\mathsf{UnivCat}

where
\mathsf{P}
says that a univalent category has finite limits
and
\mathsf{Q}
says that a functor preserves finite limits.

{defiC}

[
[
57
, Definition 6.2.8]
]

A
biequivalence
from a bicategory
\mathsf{B}_{1}
to a bicategory
\mathsf{B}_{2}

consists of pseudofunctors
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
and
\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{B}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{1}

and pseudotransformations
\eta:\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{B}_{2}}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}\cdot\mathsf{F}
and
\varepsilon:\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{B}_{1}}

such that
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are adjoint equivalences in the bicategory of pseudofunctors.
The type of biequivalences from
\mathsf{B}_{1}
to
\mathsf{B}_{2}
is denoted by
\mathsf{B}_{1}\simeq\mathsf{B}_{2}
.

Since
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are adjoint equivalences,
we have
pseudotransformations
\eta^{-1}
and
\varepsilon^{-1}

and four invertible modifications

m_{1}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.16666pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.16666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\eta\cdot\eta^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.16666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 27.16666pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

m_{2}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.05557pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.05557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\eta^{-1}\cdot\eta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.05557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 26.05557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

m_{3}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.86453pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.86453pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\varepsilon\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.86453pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 26.86453pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

and
m_{4}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 13.75345pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.75345pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\varepsilon\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.75345pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.75345pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

To construct a pseudotransformation
\zeta:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}

that is an adjoint equivalence,
we need to give another pseudotransformation
\xi:\mathsf{G}\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}

and invertible modifications
m_{1}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.98607pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.98607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\zeta\cdot\xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.98607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 22.98607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{F}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
and
m_{2}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.98607pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.98607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\xi\cdot\zeta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.98607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 22.98607pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{G}}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Note that it is not necessary to verify the triangle coherences,
because equivalences can be refined into adjoint equivalences.
However, we have a simpler way to verify that a pseudotransformation is an adjoint equivalence.
Instead of constructing the aforementioned transformations and modifications,
it suffices to verify that
\zeta
is a pointwise adjoint equivalence
[
57
, Proposition 6.2.16]
.

Proposition 13
.

Let
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
be bicategories such that
\mathsf{B}_{2}
is univalent,
and let
\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
be pseudofunctors.
A pseudotransformation
\zeta:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}

is an adjoint equivalence in the bicategory of pseudofunctors
if each
\zeta(x)
is an adjoint equivalence.

Proof 6.1

(Proof sketch)
.

We prove this the same way as
Proposition

12
,
and we need that
\mathsf{B}_{2}
is univalent so that we can use
Proposition

4
.
Here we use the construction of the bicategory of pseudofunctors via displayed bicategories
[
3
, Definition 9.6]
.

Now we set out to construct a biequivalence between
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
and
\mathsf{FinLim}
.
To do so, we use
Proposition

13
, and it suffices to construct

•

a pseudofunctor
\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{FinLim}\rightarrow\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
(
Section

6.1
);

•

a pseudofunctor
\mathsf{U}:\mathsf{DFLCompCat}\rightarrow\mathsf{FinLim}
(
Section

6.2
);

•

a pseudotransformation
\xi:\mathsf{H}\cdot\mathsf{U}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{FinLim}}
(
Section

6.3
);

•

a pseudotransformation
\zeta:\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}}\Rightarrow\mathsf{U}\cdot\mathsf{H}
(
Section

6.3
).

We must also show that
\xi
and
\zeta
are pointwise adjoint equivalences (
15
and
16
).
Finally, we construct the desired biequivalence in
Section

6.3
.

6.1.
From Categories to Comprehension Categories

We start by constructing
\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{FinLim}\rightarrow\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
.
This pseudofunctor sends every univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits to the following comprehension category.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

Note that this comprehension category is full, because the identity functor is fully faithful,
and it is univalent, because
\mathsf{C}
is so.

As discussed in
Section

5
,
the comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has the desired type formers.
Since
\mathsf{C}
is finitely complete, the displayed category
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has all fiberwise finite limits.
In addition, we can equip
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
with a cleaving for the same reason.
The comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
also is democratic,
because every context
\Gamma
is represented by the type
\diamond_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\rightarrow\langle\rangle
.
Finally, the substitution functor
s^{*}
has a left adjoint for each morphism
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta
.
This left adjoint is given by composition, and it satisfies the Beck–Chevalley condition
[
82
,
83
]
.

Problem 6.2
.

To construct a pseudofunctor
\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{FinLim}\rightarrow\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
.

Construction 6.0
(for Problem
6.2
)
.

We already described the action of
\mathsf{H}
on objects.
Next we describe the action on 1-cells.
Given a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
,
we get the following morphism of comprehension categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

The functor
\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}
preserves fiberwise finite limits, because
\mathsf{F}
preserves finite limits.
Finally, every natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
gives rise to the 2-cell
(\tau,\tau^{\downarrow})
.
∎

The difference between our construction of
\mathsf{H}
and the one by Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
, Proposition 5.2]
lies in the fact
that we use the arrow category of
\mathsf{C}
while they need to use the right adjoint splitting
[
18
,
51
]
.
To understand this difference, we reformulate their construction using displayed categories.
Let
\mathsf{C}/\Gamma
be the displayed category over
\mathsf{C}
whose objects over
\Delta
are context morphisms
\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
.
The construction by Clairambault and Dybjer uses the displayed category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
whose objects over
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}

are displayed functors
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}/\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\rightarrow}
over the identity.
If we work in set-theoretic foundations,
then this displayed category is a split fibration over
\mathsf{C}
equivalent to
\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

In univalent foundations,
we need to assume that the objects of
\mathsf{C}
form a set
to guarantee that in
\mathsf{D}
is a split fibration
[
75
]
.

6.2.
From Comprehension Categories to Categories

Next we construct the pseudofunctor
\mathsf{U}:\mathsf{DFLCompCat}\rightarrow\mathsf{FinLim}
.
This pseudofunctor sends every DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
to the univalent category
\mathsf{C}
of contexts
[
31
, Proposition 4.1]
.
The main work thus lies in showing that
\mathsf{C}
is finitely complete.

To prove that the category of contexts is finitely complete,
we show that the comprehension functor
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
of a DFL comprehension category
is an adjoint equivalence of displayed categories.
This gives us an equivalence
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{C}/\Gamma
of fiber categories for every context
\Gamma
,
and, in particular, we have equivalences
\mathsf{D}[\langle\rangle]\simeq\mathsf{C}/\langle\rangle\simeq\mathsf{C}
.
Since
\mathsf{D}
has fiberwise finite limits,
each fiber category
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
is finitely complete,
and thus
\mathsf{C}
also has all finite limits.

To prove that
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is an adjoint equivalence,
we show that it is both essentially surjective and fully faithful.
Note that this argument is constructively valid,
because
\mathsf{D}
is univalent.
We also assumed that the comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is full,
meaning that the functor
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is fully faithful.
Hence, it suffices to prove that
\chi
is essentially surjective,
and for that we follow Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
, Proposition 2.9]
.

Proposition 14
.

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a DFL comprehension category.
Then the functor
\chi
is essentially surjective.

Proof 6.3
.

To prove this, we show that the fiber functor
\chi[\Gamma]
is essentially surjective for each
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
.
Let
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
be a context morphism.
Our goal is to construct a type
A
such that
\pi_{A}
is isomorphic to
s
in the slice category
\mathsf{C}/\Gamma
.

We start by introducing some notation.
Since our comprehension category is democratic,
we get types
\widehat{\Gamma}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
and
\widehat{\Delta}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
,
and isomorphisms
\gamma_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Gamma}

and
\gamma_{\Delta}:\Delta\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Delta}
.
Let us write
\widehat{\Delta}:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
and
\widehat{\Gamma}:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta})

for
\diamond^{*}(\widehat{\Delta})
and
\diamond^{*}(\widehat{\Gamma})
respectively.

Next we construct two terms of type
\widehat{\Gamma}
in context
\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}
.
To do so, we construct the two morphisms from
\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}
to
\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}.\widehat{\Gamma}
.
First, we define the map
l
using the following diagram and the universal mapping property of the pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}.\widehat{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\diamond})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}

Second, we also define the map
r
using the universal mapping property of the pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}.\widehat{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\diamond})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Delta}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\diamond})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}

Note that
l
and
r
are terms by construction.

Since extensional identity types can be derived from equalizers,
we have a type
l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r
in context
\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}
.
We define the desired type
A
to be
\Sigma_{\widehat{\Delta}}(l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r)
, which is a type in context
\Gamma
.
Note that we have the following isomorphism, because we assumed
\Sigma
-types to be strong.

\Sigma_{\widehat{\Delta}}(l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r)\mathrel{\cong}\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}.(l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r)

We also have a morphism
f
from
\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}.(l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r)
to
\Delta
,
which is defined as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}.(l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\diamond})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Delta}^{-1}}

To construct the inverse of
f
, we define a context morphism
h:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}

and a term
t
of type
h^{*}(l)=h^{*}(r)
in context
\Delta
.
We define
h
using the universal property of the pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\widehat{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{s}{\diamond})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}

To define the term
t
,
it suffices to prove that the underlying morphisms of
\pi_{\mathds{1}_{\Delta}}\cdot h^{*}(l)
and
\pi_{\mathds{1}_{\Delta}}\cdot h^{*}(r)
are equal.
For a proof, we refer the reader to the formalization.

All in all, we constructed a type
A
and an isomorphism between
\Gamma.A
and
\Delta
.
This isomorphism lifts to the slice category
\mathsf{C}/\Gamma
,
and thus
\chi[\Gamma]
is essentially surjective.

Now we construct the desired pseudofunctor.

Problem 6.4
.

To construct a pseudofunctor
\mathsf{U}:\mathsf{DFLCompCat}\rightarrow\mathsf{FinLim}
.

Construction 6.0
(for Problem
6.4
)
.

We map every DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
to the category
\mathsf{C}
.
Note that
\mathsf{C}
is univalent, because we assumed our comprehension category to be univalent.
In addition,
\mathsf{C}
has all finite limits,
because we have equivalences
\mathsf{C}\simeq\mathsf{C}/\langle\rangle\simeq\mathsf{D}[\langle\rangle]

and because
\mathsf{D}
has all fiberwise finite limits.

Every 1-cell
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from a DFL comprehension category
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}

is mapped to
\mathsf{F}
by
\mathsf{U}
,
so we need to show that
\mathsf{F}
preserves finite limits.
To do so, we use the following natural isomorphisms.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}[\langle\rangle]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}/\langle\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}[\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}/\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}[\langle\rangle]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}[\langle\rangle]
preserves finite limits by assumption,
and thus
\mathsf{F}
preserves finite limits as well.
Finally, every 2-cell
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
is mapped to
\tau
.
∎

In
Section

6.2
we transferred properties and structure along adjoint equivalences and natural isomorphisms.
More specifically, we used that a category
\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
has finite limits if we have an equivalence
\mathsf{C}\simeq\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{C}
has all finite limits.
We also used that
\mathsf{G}
preserves finite limits if
\mathsf{F}
preserves finite limits and if we have a diagram as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Since these categories are univalent,
both principles follow directly from
Proposition

4
.

6.3.
The Pseudotransformations

We finish the construction by defining the necessary pseudotransformations
and by proving that they are adjoint equivalences.

Problem 6.5
.

To construct a pseudotransformation
\xi:\mathsf{H}\cdot\mathsf{U}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{FinLim}}
.

Construction 6.0
(for Problem
6.5
)
.

Given a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits,
the functor
\xi(\mathsf{C}):\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}
is defined to be the identity.
If we have a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
,
the naturality square
\xi(f)
, depicted below, is defined to be identity pointwise.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

This data satisfies the coherence laws for pseudotransformations.
∎

Proposition 15
.

The pseudotransformation
\xi
is a pointwise adjoint equivalence.

Proof 6.6
.

This follows from the fact that adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{FinLim}
are the same as adjoint equivalences of categories.

The construction of the other pseudotransformation is more interesting,
and we use
Proposition

14
to prove that it is an adjoint equivalence.

Problem 6.7
.

To construct a pseudotransformation
\zeta:\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}}\Rightarrow\mathsf{U}\cdot\mathsf{H}
.

Construction 6.0
(for Problem
6.7
)
.

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a DFL comprehension category.
Note that
\mathsf{U}\cdot\mathsf{H}
maps
\chi
to
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.
As such, we need to construct a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}
and a displayed functor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.
We take
\mathsf{F}
to be the identity and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
to be
\chi
.
Note that
\chi
preserves fiberwise finite limits,
because it is an adjoint equivalence by
Proposition

14
.

Now suppose that we have a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})

from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}

to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
.
To construct the naturality square
\zeta(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
,
we need a natural transformation
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}

and a displayed natural isomorphism
\overline{\tau}
over
\tau
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau}}

For
\tau
we take the identity
and for
\overline{\tau}
we take
\mathsf{F}_{\chi}
.
Note that
\overline{\tau}
is a natural isomorphism,
because we restricted ourselves to pseudo morphisms.
∎

Proposition 16
.

The pseudotransformation
\zeta
is a pointwise adjoint equivalence.

Proof 6.8
.

By
Proposition

12
,
it suffices to show that
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}
and
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
are adjoint equivalences
for each DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.
One can verify directly that the identity is an adjoint equivalence,
and we know from
Proposition

14
that
\chi
is a displayed adjoint equivalence over the identity.

We conclude this section by putting all parts of the desired biequivalence together.

Problem 6.9
.

To construct a biequivalence between the bicategories
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
and
\mathsf{FinLim}
.

Construction 6.0
(for Problem
6.9
)
.

The necessary pseudofunctors are given in
Sections

6.1
and
6.2
,
and the pseudotransformations are given in
Sections

6.3
and
6.3
.
These pseudotransformations are shown to be pointwise equivalences in
15
and
16
,
so by
Proposition

13
they are adjoint equivalences in the bicategory of pseudofunctors.
Hence, we have a biequivalence between
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
and
\mathsf{FinLim}
.
∎

7.
The Internal Language of Locally Cartesian Closed Categories

Up to now we have only considered the internal language theorem for univalent categories with finite limits,
and we showed that their internal language is given by
extensional Martin-Löf type theory with unit types, binary product types, and
\Sigma
-types.
In the remainder of this paper, we extend this theorem to classes of categories with more structure
and to more expressive versions of type theory.
We start by considering locally Cartesian closed categories,
and we show that their internal language also supports
\Pi
-types.
This statement was the theorem originally proven by Seely
[
83
, Theorem 6.3]

and later corrected by Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
, Theorem 6.1]
.

Our goal in this section is to construct a biequivalence between
the bicategories
\mathsf{LCCC}
of univalent locally Cartesian closed categories
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
of DFL comprehension categories that support
\Pi
-types.
Let us start by defining these.

{defi}

A category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits is said to be
locally Cartesian closed
if
the pullback functor
f^{*}:\mathsf{C}/y\rightarrow\mathsf{C}/x
has a right adjoint
for all morphisms
f:x\rightarrow y
.
A functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}

preserves exponentials

if the Beck–Chevalley condition for right adjoints holds for the following square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}/y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}/\mathsf{F}(y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}/x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}/\mathsf{F}(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}/y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}/x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Here the functor
\mathsf{F}/x:\mathsf{C}_{1}/x\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}/\mathsf{F}(x)
sends a morphism
g:w\rightarrow x

to
\mathsf{F}(g):\mathsf{F}(w)\rightarrow\mathsf{F}(x)
.

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FinLim}
as the subbicategory
\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})

where
\mathsf{P}
says that a univalent category with finite limits is locally Cartesian closed
and where
\mathsf{Q}
says that a functor that preserves finite limits also preserves exponentials.
The total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}}
is denoted by
\mathsf{LCCC}
.

{defi}

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a full univalent comprehension category.
We say that
\chi

supports
\Pi
-types
if

•

for every
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
the functor
\pi_{A}^{*}:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma.A)

has a right adjoint
\Pi_{A}
with unit
\eta
and counit
\varepsilon
;

•

the Beck–Chevalley condition for right adjoints holds for every pullback square.

If
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports
\Pi
-types,
then the functor
s^{*}:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathsf{D}}(\Delta)
has a right adjoint
whenever we have a commutative triangle as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}

We use this observation to say when a 1-cell

(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}

preserves
\Pi
-types.
This is because
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
only preserves context extension up to isomorphism
meaning that we have the following commutative triangle.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma.A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma).\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\pi_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A)}}

If
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
has
\Pi
-types,
then
\pi_{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A)}^{*}
has a right adjoint
and thus
\mathsf{F}(\pi_{A})^{*}
as well.

{defi}

Let
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
be a 1-cell from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
.
We say that
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})

preserves
\Pi
-types
if
for all objects
x,y:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and morphisms
f:x\rightarrow y
the Beck-Chevalley condition for right adjoints holds for the following square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}[y]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}[\mathsf{F}(y)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}[x]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}[\mathsf{F}(x)]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}[y]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}[x]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Finally, we define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
to be
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})

where
\mathsf{P}
says that
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports
\Pi
-types
and
\mathsf{Q}
says that
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
preserves
\Pi
-types.
We write
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
for the total bicategory
\int{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}}
.

Before we continue, let us make a couple of remarks on
Sections

7
and
7
.
First, both
\mathsf{LCCC}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
are univalent.
Since
\mathsf{DSub}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Q})
is univalent if both the families
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{Q}
are valued in propositions (
Section

4.1
),
it follows that the displayed bicategories
\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}
are univalent,
and thus
\mathsf{LCCC}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
are so as well.
Second, the displayed 2-cells of
\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}
are trivial,
since the type
\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
is the unit type for all displayed 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{y}
, and 2-cells
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
.

To prove that
\mathsf{LCCC}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
are biequivalent, we extend the biequivalence defined in
Section

6
using
displayed biequivalences

[
3
, Definition 8.9]
.
The notion of displayed biequivalences lifts the notion of biequivalence to the setting of displayed bicategories,
and we can use them to construct biequivalences modularly.
More specifically,
if we have a biequivalence from
\mathsf{B}_{1}
to
\mathsf{B}_{2}
and displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
respectively,
then a displayed biequivalence specifies the data and properties necessary to extend the given biequivalence to the total bicategories
\int{\mathsf{D}_{1}}
and
\int{\mathsf{D}_{2}}
.

7.1.
Displayed Biequivalences

Our main goal is to show that we can use displayed biequivalences to modularly construct biequivalences of bicategories,
which we show in
Section

7.1
.
Since we already have a biequivalence between
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
and
\mathsf{FinLim}
,
it suffices to only consider the displayed bicategories
\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}

in order to construct a biequivalence between
\mathsf{LCCC}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
.
To do so,
we have to show,
among others,
that
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports
\Pi
-types
whenever
\mathsf{C}
is locally Cartesian closed,
and that
\mathsf{C}
is locally Cartesian closed
whenever
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports
\Pi
-types.
Note that our approach is more modular compared to directly constructing a biequivalence
\mathsf{LCCC}\simeq\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
,
because we can reuse
Section

6.3
.

We start by recalling displayed biequivalences.
In the next sections, we give concrete examples of displayed biequivalences.
To define this notion, Ahrens et al.
[
3
, Definition 8.9]

first defined displayed pseudofunctors, displayed pseudotransformations, and displayed invertible modifications.
Recall that every pseudofunctor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
comes with invertible 2-cells

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}(x):\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{F}(x)}\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}(\operatorname{id}_{x})

and
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}(f,g):\mathsf{F}(f)\cdot\mathsf{F}(g)\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}(f\cdot g)
,
which we call the
identitor
and
compositor
respectively.

{defiC}

[
[
3
, Definition 8.2]
]

Let
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
be bicategories,
and let
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
be displayed bicategories over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
respectively.
A
displayed pseudofunctor

\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
from
\mathsf{D}_{1}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}

consists of

•

a map sending
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}(x)
to
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x}):{\mathsf{D}_{2}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
for each
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}
;

•

a map sending
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
to
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{y})

for all objects
\overline{x}
over
x
and
\overline{y}
over
y
, and all 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
;

•

a map sending
\overline{\tau}:\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
to
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{\tau}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f})\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(\tau)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{g})

for all 1-cell
\overline{f}
and
\overline{g}
over
f
and
g
respectively;

•

a displayed invertible 2-cell
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{i}}(\overline{x}):\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})}\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}(x)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{x}})
for each object
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}(x)
;

•

a displayed invertible 2-cell
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\overline{f},\overline{g}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f})\cdot\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{g})\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}(f,g)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f}\cdot\overline{g})

for all 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\Rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{y}\Rightarrow_{g}\overline{z}
.

This data is required to satisfy various laws,
for which we refer the reader to the literature
[
3
, Definition 8.2]
.

Let us give an example of a displayed pseudofunctor.
We define
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
to be the displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{UnivCat}
such that

•

objects over
\mathsf{C}
are objects
x:\mathsf{C}
;

•

1-cells over
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
from
x:\mathsf{C}_{1}
to
y:\mathsf{C}_{2}
are isomorphism
f:\mathsf{F}(x)\mathrel{\cong}y
;

•

2-cells over
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
from
f:\mathsf{F}(x)\mathrel{\cong}y
to
g:\mathsf{G}(x)\mathrel{\cong}y
are proofs that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{G}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

We define
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}_{2}}
to be
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}\times\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
.
Concretely, objects over
\mathsf{C}
in
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}_{2}}
are pairs of objects
x,y:\mathsf{C}
.
We have several examples of displayed pseudofunctors between the displayed bicategories
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
and
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}_{2}}
.
For instance, we have a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\Delta}
from
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
to
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}_{2}}
that maps
x
to the pair
(x,x)
.
The actions on displayed 1-cells and 2-cells are defined similarly.
We also have two displayed pseudofunctors from
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}_{2}}
to
\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
:
one maps
(x,y)
to
x
and the other maps
(x,y)
to
y
.
We denote these displayed pseudofunctors by
\overline{\pi_{1}}
and
\overline{\pi_{2}}
respectively.
None of these displayed pseudofunctors are part of a displayed biequivalence.

Another example of a displayed pseudofunctor is the identity.
Specifically,
if we have a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}
,
then we have a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\operatorname{id}}:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}\mathsf{D}
,
which maps
\overline{x}
to
\overline{x}
.
We can also compose displayed pseudofunctors.
Given
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
and
\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{D}_{3}
,
we have a composition
\overline{\mathsf{F}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{D}_{3}
,
which maps
\overline{x}
to
\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}(x))
.

If
\tau
is a pseudotransformation from
\mathsf{F}
to
\mathsf{G}
,
then every 1-cell
f:x\rightarrow y

gives rise to an invertible 2-cell
\tau(f):\tau(x)\cdot\mathsf{G}(f)\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}(f)\cdot\tau(y)

witnessing naturality.

{defiC}

[
[
3
, Definition 8.3]
]

Let
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
be bicategories,
and let
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
be displayed bicategories over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
respectively.
Suppose that we also have pseudofunctors
\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}

and displayed pseudofunctors
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
and
\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
.
A
displayed pseudotransformation

\overline{\tau}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{\mathsf{G}}
over
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}

consists of a displayed 1-cell
\overline{\tau}(\overline{x}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\tau(x)}\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\overline{x})
for each
\overline{x}
over
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}

and a displayed invertible 2-cell
\overline{\tau}(\overline{f}):\overline{\tau}(x)\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\overline{f})\Rightarrow_{\tau(f)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f})\cdot\overline{\tau}(y)
for each
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
.
This data is subject to various laws,
which the reader can find in the literature
[
3
, Definition 8.3]
.

We have a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\delta_{1}}
from
\overline{\Delta}\cdot\overline{\pi_{1}}:\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}\cdot\operatorname{id}}\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
to
\overline{\operatorname{id}}:\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}

over the pseudotransformation
\lambda:\operatorname{id}\cdot\operatorname{id}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}
,
which is defined to be the identity on every object.
Given a category
\mathsf{C}
and an object
x:\mathsf{C}
,
we need to construct a morphism
\overline{\delta_{1}}(x):x\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}x
.
Concretely, that means we need to give an isomorphism
x\mathrel{\cong}x
,
for which we take the identity isomorphism.
We can also define a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\delta_{1}}^{-1}:\overline{\operatorname{id}}\Rightarrow_{\lambda^{-1}}\overline{\Delta}\cdot\overline{\pi_{1}}
.
In the same way we can define displayed pseudotransformations
\overline{\delta_{2}}:\overline{\Delta}\cdot\overline{\pi_{1}}\Rightarrow_{\lambda}\overline{\operatorname{id}}
and
\overline{\delta_{2}}^{-1}:\overline{\operatorname{id}}\Rightarrow_{\lambda^{-1}}\overline{\Delta}\cdot\overline{\pi_{1}}
.

We also have the identity and composition operation on pseudotransformations.
If we have a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
,
then we have a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\operatorname{id}}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}\overline{\mathsf{F}}
,
which is defined to be
\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(x)}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(x)\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}_{x}}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(x)
for each
x
.
Given pseudotransformations
\overline{\tau}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{\mathsf{G}}
and
\overline{\theta}:\overline{\mathsf{G}}\Rightarrow_{\theta}\overline{\mathsf{H}}
,
then we also have a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\tau}\cdot\overline{\theta}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\tau\cdot\theta}\overline{\mathsf{H}}
.
We define
\overline{\tau}\cdot\overline{\theta}
to be
\overline{\tau}(x)\cdot\overline{\theta}(x):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(x)\rightarrow_{\tau(x)\cdot\theta(x)}\overline{\mathsf{H}}(x)
for each
x
.

{defiC}

[
[
3
, Definition 8.4]
]

Suppose that we have bicategories
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
pseudofunctors
\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
and pseudotransformations
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
.
In addition,
we assume that we have
displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
respectively,
displayed pseudofunctors
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
and
\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
,
and displayed pseudotransformations
\overline{\tau}_{1}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\tau_{1}}\overline{\mathsf{G}}
and
\overline{\tau}_{2}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\tau_{2}}\overline{\mathsf{G}}
.
A
displayed invertible modification

\overline{m}

over an invertible modification
m:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.42883pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.42883pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\tau_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.42883pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 19.42883pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\tau_{2}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
consists of
displayed invertible 2-cells
\overline{m}(\overline{x}):\overline{\tau}_{1}(\overline{x})\Rightarrow_{m(x)}\overline{\tau}_{2}(\overline{x})
for each
\overline{x}
over
x
.
This data is required to satisfy a naturality law,
which is given in the literature
[
3
, Definition 8.4]
.

Note that we have an invertible modification
m:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.03471pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.03471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\lambda\cdot\lambda^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.03471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{2}\lx@xy@tip{-2}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}{\hbox{\kern 28.03471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{id},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

which is defined to be the identity 2-cell pointwise.
We can construct a displayed invertible modification
\overline{m}
from
\overline{\delta_{1}}\cdot\overline{\delta_{1}}^{-1}
to the identity.
To do so,
let us assume that we have some category
\mathsf{C}
with an object
x:\mathsf{C}
.
From
\mathsf{C}
and
x
we get two displayed 1-cells,
namely
(\overline{\delta_{1}}\cdot\overline{\delta_{1}}^{-1})(\mathsf{C},x):x\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{C}}}x

and
\overline{\operatorname{id}}(\mathsf{C},x):x\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{C}}}x
.
Both these displayed 1-cells are the identity isomorphism.
Hence, we get the desired displayed 2-cell
from the fact that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

Analogously, we can construct a displayed invertible modifications from
\overline{\delta_{2}}\cdot\overline{\delta_{2}}^{-1}
to the identity.
We can also define the identity and composition of displayed invertible modifications,
but since we do not use these operations in the remainder,
we do not discuss their definitions.

Next we put these notions together to obtain the notion of displayed biequivalence.

{defiC}

[
[
3
, Definition 8.9]
]

Let
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
be bicategories,
and let
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
be displayed bicategories over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
respectively.
Suppose that we have a biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
where
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
.
A
displayed biequivalence
over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
consists of

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
;

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{D}_{1}
;

•

displayed pseudotransformations
\overline{\eta}:\overline{\operatorname{id}}\Rightarrow_{\eta}\overline{\mathsf{F}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}
and
\overline{\eta}^{-1}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}\Rightarrow_{\eta^{-1}}\overline{\operatorname{id}}
;

•

displayed pseudotransformations
\overline{\varepsilon}:\overline{\mathsf{G}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\varepsilon}\overline{\operatorname{id}}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}^{-1}:\overline{\operatorname{id}}\Rightarrow_{\varepsilon^{-1}}\overline{\mathsf{G}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{F}}
;

•

displayed invertible modifications witnessing that
\overline{\eta}
and
\overline{\eta}^{-1}
form an equivalence;

•

displayed invertible modifications witnessing that
\overline{\varepsilon}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}^{-1}
form an equivalence.

In the remainder of this paper,
we discuss numerous examples of displayed biequivalences.
The reason why we are interested in displayed biequivalences,
is because they give rise to biequivalences on the total bicategories.
We use this feature to construct biequivalences in a modular way,
because we can extend a given biequivalence to displayed bicategories.
To do so, we use the following construction
[
3
, Construction 8.11]
.

Problem 7.1
.

Given bicategories
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
respectively,
and a displayed biequivalence
(\overline{\mathsf{F}},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\overline{\eta},\overline{\varepsilon})
over a biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
,
to construct a biequivalence
(\int{\overline{\mathsf{F}}},\int{\overline{\mathsf{G}}},\int{\overline{\eta}},\int{\overline{\varepsilon}})
between the total bicategories
\int{\mathsf{D}_{1}}
and
\int{\mathsf{D}_{2}}
.

Construction 7.0
(for Problem
7.1
)
.

Each of the components is constructed by pairing.
For instance, the pseudofunctor
\int{\overline{\mathsf{F}}}
sends a pair
(x,\overline{x})
in
\int{\mathsf{D}_{1}}
to
(\mathsf{F}(x),\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x}))
in
\int{\mathsf{D}_{2}}
.
The actions on 1-cells and 2-cells are similar.
∎

In the remainder, we only consider displayed bicategories that are locally preordered and groupoidal.
These conditions give us a more convenient way to construct displayed biequivalences.
In the construction, we use an analogue of
Proposition

13
for displayed pseudotransformations,
which says that it suffices to check that
\overline{\eta}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}
are pointwise displayed adjoint equivalences.

Problem 7.2
.

Suppose that we have univalent bicategories
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}

and displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
be displayed bicategories over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
respectively,
such that
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}
are both locally preordered and groupoidal.
Given a biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
where
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}

and

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
;

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{G}}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{G}}\mathsf{D}_{1}
;

•

a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\eta}:\overline{\operatorname{id}}\Rightarrow_{\eta}\overline{\mathsf{F}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}
;

•

a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\varepsilon}:\overline{\mathsf{G}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\varepsilon}\overline{\operatorname{id}}

such that
\overline{\eta}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}
are pointwise displayed adjoint equivalences,
to construct a displayed biequivalence over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
.

Construction 7.0
(for Problem
7.2
)
.

To construct the desired displayed biequivalence,
it suffices to construct displayed pseudotransformations
\overline{\eta}^{-1}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}^{-1}
and the necessary invertible modifications.
We consider a more general statement,
namely that if a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\tau}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{\mathsf{G}}
is a pointwise displayed adjoint equivalence,
then we have a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\tau}^{-1}:\overline{\mathsf{G}}\Rightarrow_{\tau^{-1}}\overline{\mathsf{F}}

where
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}
is a pseudonatural adjoint equivalence.
For this statement, we only give a sketch.
Since we assumed the involved bicategories to be univalent,
the bicategory of pseudofunctors is univalent as well
[
3
, Theorem 9.8]
,
and thus we can assume that
\tau
is the identity by
Proposition

4
.
We define
\overline{\tau}^{-1}(x)
to be the inverse of
\overline{\tau}(x)

where we use that
\overline{\tau}(x)
is a displayed adjoint equivalence.
In the same way, we define the necessary displayed invertible modifications.
∎

In
Section

7.1
, we used that the involved bicategories are univalent
and that the involved displayed bicategories are locally preordered and groupoidal.
These assumptions helped to simplify the necessary constructions on a technical level,
because, for instance,
we do not have to verify of the coherences necessary for displayed pseudotransformations or modifications.
In addition, we can simplify the notions of displayed pseudofunctor and displayed pseudotransformation
if we assume the involved displayed bicategories are locally preordered and groupoidal.
Specifically, if
\mathsf{D}_{2}
is both locally preordered and groupoidal,
then, to construct a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}_{2}
, it suffices to give

•

a map sending
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}(x)
to
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x}):{\mathsf{D}_{2}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
for each
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}
;

•

a map sending
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
to
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{y})

for all objects
\overline{x}
over
x
and
\overline{y}
over
y
, and all 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
;

•

a map sending
\overline{\tau}:\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
to
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{\tau}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f})\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(\tau)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{g})

for all 1-cell
\overline{f}
and
\overline{g}
over
f
and
g
respectively;

•

a displayed 2-cell
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{i}}(\overline{x}):\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})}\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}(x)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{\overline{x}})
for each object
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}(x)
;

•

a displayed 2-cell
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\overline{f},\overline{g}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f})\cdot\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{g})\Rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}(f,g)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f}\cdot\overline{g})

for all 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\Rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{y}\Rightarrow_{g}\overline{z}
.

For a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\tau}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{\mathsf{G}}
, it suffices to give

•

a displayed 1-cell
\overline{\tau}(\overline{x}):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\tau(x)}\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\overline{x})
for each
\overline{x}
over
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}
;

•

a displayed 2-cell
\overline{\tau}(\overline{f}):\overline{\tau}(x)\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\overline{f})\Rightarrow_{\tau(f)}\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{f})\cdot\overline{\tau}(y)
for each
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
.

If
\mathsf{D}_{2}
further has trivial 2-cells, then the necessary displayed 2-cells exist automatically.

In the remainder, we use the following example of a displayed biequivalence.
This example says that if we have a biequivalence from
\mathsf{D}_{1}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}
and from
\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}^{\prime}
,
then we also have a biequivalence from
\mathsf{D}_{1}\times\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}\times\mathsf{D}_{2}^{\prime}
.

{exa}

Suppose that we have displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
,
and displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}_{2}
and
\mathsf{D}_{2}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{B}_{2}
.
We assume that each of these displayed bicategories are locally preordered and groupoidal,
and we also assume to have a biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
from
\mathsf{B}_{1}
to
\mathsf{B}_{2}
.
Finally, suppose that we have displayed biequivalences
(\overline{\mathsf{F}},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\overline{\eta},\overline{\varepsilon})
from
\mathsf{D}_{1}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}
over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)

and
(\overline{\mathsf{F}}^{\prime},\overline{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime},\overline{\eta}^{\prime},\overline{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
from
\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}^{\prime}
over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
.
Then we also have their
product displayed biequivalence
