(\overline{\mathsf{F}}\times\overline{\mathsf{F}}^{\prime},\overline{\mathsf{G}}\times\overline{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime},\overline{\eta}\times\overline{\eta}^{\prime},\overline{\varepsilon}\times\overline{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
,
which goes from
\mathsf{D}_{1}\times\mathsf{D}_{1}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{D}_{2}\times\mathsf{D}_{2}^{\prime}

and lives over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
.
Both the pseudofunctors
\overline{\mathsf{F}}\times\overline{\mathsf{F}}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\mathsf{G}}\times\overline{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}
,
and the pseudotransformations
\overline{\eta}\times\overline{\eta}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}\times\overline{\varepsilon}^{\prime}

are defined by pairing.

7.2.
Extending the Biequivalence to Locally Cartesian Closed Categories

We finish this section by constructing the desired displayed biequivalence between
\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}

over the biequivalence defined in
Section

6.3
.
Recall that this biequivalence consists of pseudofunctors

\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{FinLim}\rightarrow\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
(
Section

6.1
)
and
\mathsf{U}:\mathsf{DFLCompCat}\rightarrow\mathsf{FinLim}
(
Section

6.2
),
and pseudotransformations
\xi:\mathsf{H}\cdot\mathsf{U}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{FinLim}}
(
Section

6.3
)
and
\zeta:\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}}\Rightarrow\mathsf{U}\cdot\mathsf{H}
(
Section

6.3
).
To construct the desired displayed biequivalence, we need to construct

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\Pi}
from
\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
to
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{H}
(
Section

7.2
);

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\Pi}
from
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}
to
\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{U}
(
Section

7.2
);

•

a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\xi}_{\Pi}
from
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\Pi}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\Pi}
to
\overline{\operatorname{id}}
over
\xi
(
Proposition

17
);

•

a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\zeta}_{\Pi}
from
\overline{\operatorname{id}}
to
\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\Pi}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\Pi}
over
\zeta
(
Proposition

18
)

and we need to verify that
\overline{\xi}_{\Pi}
and
\overline{\zeta}_{\Pi}
are pointwise adjoint equivalences.
We start with
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\Pi}
.

Problem 7.3
.

To construct a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\Pi}:\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{H}}\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}
.

Construction 7.0
(for Problem
7.3
)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent locally Cartesian closed category.
We need to verify that
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports
\Pi
-types.
The necessary right adjoints exist, because
\mathsf{C}
is locally Cartesian closed.
In addition, the Beck-Chevalley condition for
\Pi
-types follows from the Beck–Chevalley condition of
\Sigma
-types
[
82
]
.
We must also show that for every functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
that preserves exponentials,
the displayed functor
\mathsf{F}^{\downarrow}
preserves
\Pi
-types.
This follows by definition.
∎

Problem 7.4
.

To construct a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\Pi}:\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{D}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{U}}\mathsf{LCCC}_{\mathsf{D}}
.

Construction 7.0
(for Problem
7.4
)
.

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a DFL comprehension category that supports
\Pi
-types.
We need to show that the category
\mathsf{C}
is locally Cartesian closed,
so we need to prove that the pullback functor
s^{*}:\mathsf{C}/\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{C}/\Delta
has a right adjoint for every
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
.
By
Proposition

14
we can assume that
s
is isomorphic to a map
\pi_{A}
for some type
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
,
and thus
s^{*}
has a right adjoint since
\chi
supports
\Pi
-types.
∎

Proposition 17
.

We have a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\xi}_{\Pi}:\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\Pi}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\Pi}\Rightarrow\overline{\operatorname{id}}
over
\xi
.

Proof 7.5
.

Recall that
\xi
was defined to be the identity functor pointwise.
The desired pseudotransformation exists, because the identity functor preserves exponentials.

Proposition 18
.

We have a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\zeta}_{\Pi}:\overline{\operatorname{id}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\Pi}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\Pi}
over
\zeta
.

Proof 7.6
.

To construct the desired pseudotransformation,
we need to show that
\zeta
preserves
\Pi
-types pointwise.
Recall that
\zeta
is a pointwise adjoint equivalence by
Proposition

16
.
Using equivalence induction (
Proposition

4
),
we can show that every adjoint equivalence of DFL comprehension categories preserves
\Pi
-types,
because the identity preserves
\Pi
-types.
From this, we conclude that the desired pseudotransformation exists.

Problem 7.7
.

The bicategories
\mathsf{LCCC}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\Pi}
are biequivalent.

Construction 7.0
(for Problem
7.7
)
.

The desired pseudofunctors and pseudotransformations
are constructed in
Sections

7.2
and
7.2

and in
17
and
18
respectively.
Both
\overline{\xi}_{\Pi}
and
\overline{\zeta}_{\Pi}
are pointwise adjoint equivalences by construction.
∎

8.
The Internal Language of Toposes

Up to now we have considered the internal language of univalent categories with finite limits
and univalent locally Cartesian closed categories.
In this section we extend these internal language theorems to various classes of toposes,
among which are pretoposes,
\Pi
-pretoposes, and elementary toposes.
Note that each of these classes is either an extension of categories with finite limits or of locally Cartesian closed categories,
so,
compared to those,
their internal languages come with additional type formers.
Compared to categories with finite limits,
pretoposes extend the internal language with the empty type, disjoint sum types, and quotients,
whereas elementary toposes extend the internal language of locally Cartesian closed categories with a subobject classifier type.

Each of the aforementioned type formers is treated similarly in the semantics.
For instance, a full comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports binary sum types if each fiber
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
has binary coproducts,
and if the functor
s^{*}:\mathsf{D}[\Delta]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
preserves binary products for each
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta
.
The type of natural numbers is treated the same: a comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
supports a natural numbers type
if each fiber
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
has a natural numbers object,
and if
s^{*}:\mathsf{D}[\Delta]\rightarrow\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
preserves natural numbers objects for each
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta
.
We can say the same about empty types, subobject classifier types, and quotient types.
Hence, a full comprehension category supports such a type former if each fiber has some categorical structure,
and if the substitution functors preserve that structure.

This idea was used by Maietti to prove soundness and completeness theorems for various dependent type theories
[
68
, Theorems 5.8 and 5.28]
.
More specifically, Maietti defines the notion of
local property

[
68
, Proposition 2.13]
,
which encapsulates the pattern we mentioned before,
and interprets numerous type formers, like quotients and disjoint sum types, with suitable local properties.
Specifically,
a local property is a property of categories that is closed under slicing.
Since a wide variety of categorical structures can be captured using local properties,
this gives a powerful method to study the semantics of various flavors of type theory.

Our goal in this section is to extend the biequivalence defined in
Section

6.3
along local properties.
To see what that entails, let us consider an example.
Recall that a category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits and finite coproducts is called
extensive

if coproducts are disjoint and stable under pullback.
Extensiveness gives an instance of a local property, since this property is closed under slicing.
Using this property we can restrict
\mathsf{FinLim}
to the bicategory of univalent extensive categories,
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
to the bicategory DFL comprehension categories for which each fiber is extensive
and each substitution functor preserves binary coproducts.
We show in this section that these two bicategories are biequivalent,
and we prove a general statement using arbitrary local properties.
To do so, we use the same methods as in
Section

7
.

We start this section by discussing local properties.
More specifically, we define this notion
and we extend the biequivalence from
Section

6.3
along local properties.
After that we give numerous examples of local properties,
and we instantiate the extended biequivalence to obtain internal language theorems for various classes of toposes.

8.1.
Local Properties

Now we define local properties.
The idea is that a local property is a property of categories that is closed under slicing.

{defi}

A
local property

\mathsf{P}
is given by

•

a proposition
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
for each univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits;

•

a proposition
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
for each functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}

such that
\mathsf{F}
preserves finite limits
and such that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C}_{1})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C}_{2})
hold.

We require the following axioms on this data.

•

For each univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{C}})
if
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
holds.

•

Given functors
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
and
\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{C}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{3}
that preserve finite limits,
we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G})
if both
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{G})
hold.

•

If
\mathsf{C}
is a univalent category with finite limits such that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
, we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C}/x)
for each object
x:\mathsf{C}
.

•

If
\mathsf{C}
is a univalent category with finite limits such that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
, we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(f^{*})
for each morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
.

•

If
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
is a functor preserving finite limits such that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
,
then we also have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F}/x)
for each
x:\mathsf{C}_{1}
.

There are several remarks to make on
Section

8.1
.
First, we require
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
to be propositions.
This requirement is satisfied for all our examples of interest, because we work with univalent categories.
For instance, we have a local property that expresses that a category has a subobject classifier.
The type of subobject classifier in a univalent category is a proposition, because subobject classifiers are unique up to isomorphism,
and thus unique if we assume our category to be univalent.
If our focus had been on categories that are not necessarily univalent,
then we would need to drop the requirement that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
is a proposition,
because, for instance, chosen subobject classifiers are not guaranteed to be unique.
In addition, one would need to add the requirements to
Section

8.1
saying that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})

are closed under adjoint equivalence and natural isomorphism respectively
to get a suitable notion of local property.

Second,
since we are working with univalent categories,
a local property is automatically invariant under equivalence.
More specifically,
if we have an adjoint equivalence
\mathsf{C}_{1}\simeq\mathsf{C}_{2}
between univalent categories,
then
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C}_{1})
holds if and only if
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C}_{2})
holds.
We can say the same for functors.
More specifically, suppose that we have a diagram as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Then
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
holds if and only if
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{G})
holds.
Both these statements follow from
Proposition

4
.
If we did not assume univalence,
then we would need to add both these requirements to
Section

8.1
,
and we would need to verify them for each instance of a local property.

Finally, our definition is slightly different compared to the one by Maietti.
We require the functors
\mathsf{F}/x:\mathsf{C}_{1}/x\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}/\mathsf{F}(x)
to be structure preserving whenever
\mathsf{F}
is,
while Maietti does not add this requirement.
The reason for this difference is that our focus is on biequivalences,
and thus we need to take morphisms into account.

Next we define the necessary displayed bicategories.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{P}
be a local property.
We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{FinLim}
to be
\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}},\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}})
.

A DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
satisfies
\mathsf{P}

if we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{D}[\Gamma])
for each
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}

and we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(s^{*})
for each
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta
.
A 1-cell
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
preserves
\mathsf{P}

if we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}[\Gamma])
for each
\Gamma
.
Now we define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
over
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
to be
\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{Q}^{\prime})
.
Here
\mathsf{Q}
says that a DFL comprehension category satisfies
\mathsf{P}

and
\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
says that a 1-cell preserves
\mathsf{P}
.

An object over a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits in
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
is thus a proof that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
holds,
and a morphism over a functor
\mathsf{F}
that preserves finite limits is a proof of
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
.
Objects in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
are DFL comprehension categories that satisfy
\mathsf{P}
fiberwise,
and morphisms over
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
are proofs that for each
\Gamma
we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}[\Gamma])
.
Note that both
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
are univalent.
This is because we define them as subbicategories,
and because local properties are valued in propositions.

Our goal is to construct a displayed biequivalence between
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}

over the biequivalence constructed in
Section

6.3
.

Problem 8.1
.

To construct a displayed biequivalence between
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}

over the biequivalence
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\xi,\zeta)

for each local property
\mathsf{P}
.

Construction 8.0
(for Problem
8.1
)
.

We first construct a displayed pseudofunctor
from
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
to
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}

over
\mathsf{H}
.
To do so, we must show that the comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
satisfies
\mathsf{P}
.
This is so, because local properties are closed under slicing.

Next we construct a displayed pseudofunctor
from
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}
to
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{D}}

over
\mathsf{U}
.
Concretely, we show that whenever we have some DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
that satisfies
\mathsf{P}
,
then we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
.
This follows from the fact that the property
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}
is preserved under equivalence,
and because we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{D}[\langle\rangle])
.
Analogously,
we show for each 1-cell
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
of DFL comprehension categories
we have that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
.

Finally, we must construct displayed pseudotransformations over
\xi
and
\zeta
.
For
\xi
,
it follows from the fact that we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\operatorname{id})
.
For
\zeta
,
we use that every adjoint equivalence satisfies
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}
,
which follows from
Proposition

4

and the fact that we have
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\operatorname{id})
.
∎

8.2.
Extending the Biequivalence to Toposes

Now our goal is to instantiate
Section

8.1
to various classes of toposes,
and to do so, we first define the local properties necessary to define these classes of toposes.
We start with the conjunction on local properties.

{exa}

Given local properties
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
,
we define their conjunction
\mathsf{P}\land\mathsf{P}^{\prime}
as follows.

•

The type
(\mathsf{P}\land\mathsf{P}^{\prime})_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
is defined to be
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})\land\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
.

•

A functor
\mathsf{F}
satisfies
(\mathsf{P}\land\mathsf{P}^{\prime})_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})

if we have both
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
and
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
.

Next we look at several type formers.
The first one is given by finite coproducts.
To interpret the empty type, we use strict initial objects,
and we say that an initial object is strict if every morphism into it is an isomorphism.
Binary coproduct types can be interpreted in a category if that category has binary coproducts
that are stable under pullback.
Another property of interest is
extensiveness

[
27
, Definition 2.1]
,
which allows us to interpret finite disjoint coproducts.
Recall that a category is
extensive
if it has finite coproducts
that are stable under pullback
and that are disjoint.
We say that a coproduct is disjoint if the pullback of all coproduct inclusions is an initial object.

{exa}

We define the local property
\mathsf{Initial}^{\mathsf{strict}}

such that a category
\mathsf{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{Initial}^{\mathsf{strict}}_{\mathsf{Cat}}
if
\mathsf{C}
has a strict initial object,
and a functor
\mathsf{F}
satisfies
\mathsf{Initial}^{\mathsf{strict}}_{\mathsf{Fun}}
if
\mathsf{F}
preserves initial objects.

Next we define the local property
\mathsf{Coproduct}^{\mathsf{stable}}
.
A category
\mathsf{C}
satisfies
\mathsf{Coproduct}^{\mathsf{stable}}_{\mathsf{Cat}}
if
\mathsf{C}
has binary coproducts that are stable under pullback,
and a functor
\mathsf{F}
satisfies
\mathsf{Coproduct}^{\mathsf{stable}}_{\mathsf{Fun}}
if
\mathsf{F}
preserves binary coproducts.

Finally, we define the local property
\mathsf{Extensive}
.
Categories satisfy
\mathsf{Extensive}_{\mathsf{Cat}}
if they are extensive,
and functors satisfy
\mathsf{Extensive}_{\mathsf{Fun}}
if they preserve finite coproducts.

The next type former of interest is given by quotient types.
We can interpret these in
exact categories

[
17
]
.
Recall that a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
in a category
\mathsf{C}
is a
regular epimorphism

[
24
, Definition 4.3.1]

if there exists
w:\mathsf{C}
and morphisms
g_{1},g_{2}:w\rightarrow x
such that
f
is the coequalizer of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.
A category with finite limits if said to be
regular

[
24
, Definition 2.1.1]

if regular epimorphisms are stable under pullback and
if for every morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
the kernel pair, which is the pullback of
f
along
f
, has a coequalizer.
An internal relation on
x
is given by a monomorphism
m:r\rightarrow x\times x
,
and each internal relation gives rise to a relation
\overline{r}_{w}
on the type
w\rightarrow x
of morphisms for each
w
.
This relation relates
f,g:w\rightarrow x
if there is
k:w\rightarrow r
making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\times x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

An internal equivalence relation
m:r\rightarrow x\times x
is an internal relation for which each relation
\overline{r}_{w}
is an equivalence relation.
We say that a regular category is
exact

[
24
, Definition 2.6.1]
if
for every internal equivalence relation
m:r\rightarrow x\times x

we have a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
such that
m
is the kernel pair of
f
.

{exa}

We define the local property
\mathsf{Regular}
such that
categories satisfy
\mathsf{Regular}_{\mathsf{Cat}}
if they are regular
and functors satisfy
\mathsf{Regular}_{\mathsf{Fun}}
if they preserve regular epimorphisms.

The local property
\mathsf{Exact}
is defined analogously.
Categories satisfy
\mathsf{Exact}_{\mathsf{Cat}}
if they are exact
and functors satisfy
\mathsf{Exact}_{\mathsf{Fun}}
if they preserve regular epimorphisms.

Next we look at the local property for
subobject classifiers
,
which represent an impredicative universe of all propositions.
Recall that a subobject classifier is given by a morphism that classifies all monomorphisms
[
58
]
.
Specifically,
a subobject classifier in a category
\mathsf{C}
with a terminal object
\langle\rangle
is given by an object
\Omega
with a morphism
\top:\langle\rangle\rightarrow\Omega

such that for every monomorphism
m:x\rightarrow y
there is a unique
\chi_{m}:y\rightarrow\Omega

making the following square a pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{m}}

{exa}

We define the local property
\mathsf{SubObjClass}
such that

\mathsf{SubObjClass}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
is the type of subobject classifiers in some category
\mathsf{C}

and
\mathsf{F}
satisfies
\mathsf{SubObjClass}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
if
\mathsf{F}
preserves subobject classifiers.

To see how subobject classifiers give rise to a universe of propositions,
we show that propositions in a DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
correspond to monomorphisms in
\mathsf{C}
.
This observation was already made by Maietti
[
68
]
,
who showed that internal propositions in the syntactic category correspond to monomorphisms.
Recall that a type
A
is said to be a proposition if for all
x,y:A
we have
x=y
.
We translate that notion to comprehension categories as follows.

{defi}

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a DFL comprehension category,
and let
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
be a type in context
\Gamma
.
Note that we have two terms of type
\pi^{*}(\pi^{*}(A))
in context
\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)
,
namely
l\coloneqq\mathsf{q}
and
r\coloneqq\pi^{*}(\mathsf{q})
.
We say that
A
is an
internal proposition

if we have an term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}(l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r)
.

The notion of internal proposition expresses that we have a term
\Gamma,x:A,y:A\vdash x=_{\mathsf{ext}}y
.
To show that internal propositions correspond to monomorphisms,
we first show that internal propositions can be characterized via their display maps.
Specifically, a type
A
is an internal proposition if and only if
\pi_{A}
is a monomorphism.
Throughout that proof, we use the concrete definitions of the underlying morphisms of
l
and
r
in
Section

8.2
.
Recall that we defined
l
as the following morphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A).\pi^{*}(\pi^{*}(A))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The term
r
is defined as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A).\pi^{*}(\pi^{*}(A))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where we defined
r^{\prime}
to be the following morphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Proposition 19
.

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a DFL comprehension category.
A type
A
is an internal proposition if and only if
\pi_{A}
is a monomorphism.

Proof 8.2
.

First, we assume that
A
is an internal proposition.
Suppose that we have morphisms
s_{1},s_{2}:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma.A
such that
s_{1}\cdot\pi=s_{2}\cdot\pi
.
We need to show that
s_{1}=s_{2}
.
Since
\chi
preserves Cartesian morphisms,
we have the morphism
f
, depicted below, by the universal mapping property of the pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

In addition,
since
A
is an internal proposition,
we have that
l=r
by the reflection rule.
Hence, the following compositions of morphisms are equal.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A).\pi^{*}(\pi^{*}(A))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}

Since
f\cdot l\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})=s_{1}

and
f\cdot r\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})=s_{2}
,
it follows that
s_{1}=s_{2}
.

Next we assume that
\pi_{A}
is a monomorphism,
and our goal is to construct a term
p:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}(l=_{\mathsf{ext}}r)
.
It suffices to show that
l=r
,
and by the universal mapping property of the pullback,
we need to show that
l\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})=r\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})

and that
l\cdot\pi=r\cdot\pi
.
Note that we have
l\cdot\pi=r\cdot\pi
, because both compositions are the identity.
To prove that
l\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})=r\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})
,
we use that
l\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})=\operatorname{id}

and
r\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})=\pi\cdot r^{\prime}
,
so we need to show that
\pi\cdot r^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}
.
Again we use the universal mapping property of the pullback,
and it suffices to show that
\pi\cdot r^{\prime}\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})=\chi(\overline{\pi})

and
\pi\cdot r^{\prime}\cdot\pi=\pi
.
The second equality follows, because
r^{\prime}\cdot\pi
is an identity.
Since
r^{\prime}\cdot\chi(\overline{\pi})
is an identity as well
and since we assumed
\pi_{A}
to be a monomorphism,
it is sufficient to show that the following compositions are equal.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A.\pi^{*}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(\overline{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}

This equality follows from the fact
\chi(\overline{\pi})
is a morphism in the arrow category.

Proposition 20
.

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a DFL comprehension category,
and let
A:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
be a type.
The morphism
\pi_{A}
is a monomorphism if and only if the unique map from
A
to
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}

in the fiber category
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
is a monomorphism.

Proof 8.3
.

We write
f
for the unique map from
A
to
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}
.
Note that we have a commuting triangle as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma.\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}}}

Since
\pi_{\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}}
is an isomorphism,

\pi_{A}
is a monomorphism if and only if
\chi(f)
is one.
By definition
\chi
is fully faithful, and hence it reflects monomorphisms.
Since
\chi
is essentially surjective by
Proposition

14
,
it also is an adjoint equivalence,
and thus it preserves monomorphisms.
Hence,
f
is a monomorphism if and only if
\chi(f)
is one.
The desired proposition now follows from the fact that
\chi(f)
is a monomorphism if and only if
\pi_{A}
is one.

If a DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
has a subobject classifier type
\Omega
,
then terms
\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\Omega)
correspond to morphisms
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\Omega
in
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
.
By the universal property of subobject classifier,
morphisms
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\Omega
correspond to monomorphisms into
\mathds{1}_{\Gamma}
.
By
19
and
20
such monomorphisms correspond to internal propositions.

Finally, we look at the types of natural numbers.
Such types can be interpreted in categories with a
parameterized natural numbers object

[
69
, Definition 2.1]
.
A parameterized natural numbers object in a category
\mathsf{C}
with binary products and a terminal object
T

is given by an object
n:\mathsf{C}
together with morphisms
z:\langle\rangle\rightarrow n
and
s:n\rightarrow n

such that for all objects
b,y:\mathsf{C}
together with morphisms
z^{\prime}:b\rightarrow y
and
s^{\prime}:y\rightarrow y

there is a unique
f:b\times n\rightarrow y
making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b\times n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b\times n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\operatorname{id},z\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\times s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}

Note that parameterized natural numbers object satisfy a stronger universal mapping property than ordinary ones,
and that the latter only gives a suitable notion in Cartesian closed categories.
For this reason, we use the parameterized version.

{exa}

We define the local property
\mathsf{NNO}^{\mathsf{P}}

such that
\mathsf{NNO}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{Cat}}(\mathsf{C})
is the type of parameterized natural numbers objects in
\mathsf{C}

and a functor
\mathsf{F}
satisfies
\mathsf{NNO}^{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathsf{Fun}}(\mathsf{F})
if
\mathsf{F}
preserves parameterized natural numbers objects.

Remark 21
.

Being locally Cartesian closed gives us yet another local property
[
68
]
.
This is because a category
\mathsf{C}
is locally Cartesian closed
if and only if every slice category
\mathsf{C}/x
is Cartesian closed.
While we could use this observation to directly establish an internal language theorem for locally Cartesian closed categories,
we chose not to do so.
If we were to use local properties to establish such a theorem,
then we would get a non-standard presentation of
\Pi
-types in comprehension categories:
instead of representing
\Pi
-types as right adjoints to weakening,
we would get that each fiber category is locally Cartesian closed.
The work we did in
Section

7
allowed us to connect locally Cartesian categories
to comprehension categories with a standard presentation of
\Pi
-types.

Now we are ready to define bicategories of various classes of toposes.
These are

pretoposes

[
73
]
,

arithmetic pretoposes

[
69
, Definition 2.6]
,

\Pi
-pretoposes

[
73
]
,

elementary toposes

[
66
]
,
and
elementary toposes with NNO

[
66
]
.
We define these classes using displayed bicategories and by defining suitable local properties.

{defi}

We define the bicategories of various classes of toposes and of their corresponding comprehension categories.
In
Table

2
, we define the local properties that we use.
The bicategories of toposes and comprehension categories are defined in
Tables

3
and
4
.
Each of these is defined as the total bicategory of the displayed bicategory in the right column.

Local Property

Definition

\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{PreTop}}

\mathsf{Extensive}\land\mathsf{Exact}

\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{PreTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}

\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{PreTop}}\land\mathsf{NNO}^{\mathsf{P}}

\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{ElemTop}}

\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{PreTop}}\land\mathsf{SubObjClass}

\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}

\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{ElemTop}}\land\mathsf{NNO}^{\mathsf{P}}

Table 4.
Bicategories of full univalent comprehension categories

Note that one can show that the category of sets is a
\Pi
W-pretopos,
and a topos if one assumes resizing axioms
[
80
]
.
Since the local properties
\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{ElemTop}}
and
\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
do not mention exponentials,
they only express that a univalent locally Cartesian closed category is a topos (with a natural numbers object).
Using the material in this section we directly obtain internal language theorems for each of these classes of toposes.

{exa}

By
Sections

7.1
and
8.1
,
we get the following biequivalences.

•

\mathsf{PreTop}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{PreTop}}
;

•

\mathsf{PreTop}_{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{PreTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
;

•

\mathsf{PreTop}_{\Pi}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{PreTop}_{\Pi}}
;

•

\mathsf{ElemTop}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}}
;

•

\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
.

9.
The Internal Language of Categories with a Family of Types

Up to now,
we have considered a wide variety of type formers,
including
\Pi
-types, quotients, and the natural numbers,
and we proved internal language theorems for various classes of categories supporting some of these types.
The final type former that we consider in this paper,
is universe types for elementary toposes.
Specifically, our goal
is to extend the biequivalence
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}\simeq\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
to include a universe
closed under all type formers in a topos
[
85
]
.

Our focus is on Tarski-style universes
[
71
]
,
which are given by a type of codes
and a map that translates each code to a type in the appropriate context.
In type theory,
we represent such a universe by a type
\mathcal{U}

and a type former that assigns to each term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{U})

a type
\mathsf{El}(t)
in context
\Gamma
.
There are various introduction rules for
\mathcal{U}
,
representing which type formers are contained in the universe.
For instance, one can postulate the existence of a code
\mathbb{n}:\mathcal{U}

such that
\mathsf{El}(\mathbb{n})=\mathbb{N}
,
or that
\mathcal{U}
is closed under
\Sigma
-types.
Note that we do not have an elimination rule for
\mathcal{U}
.

We use two steps to extend the biequivalence
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}\simeq\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
with a universe type.
First, we consider a family of types,
which is the main goal of this section.
Such a family gives us a Tarski-style universe that does not have any introduction rule
meaning that it is not guaranteed to be closed under type former.
In the next section, we further consider various type formers for such a family of types
following Streicher
[
85
]
.
We start by defining a notion of family of types in categories with finite limits,
and we show that this gives rise to a displayed bicategory (
Section

9.1
).
Next we do the same for comprehension categories (
Section

9.2
),
and we end this section by constructing the a displayed biequivalence
between the resulting displayed bicategories (
Section

9.3
).

9.1.
Categories with a Family of Types

The notion of universes in categories is well-established,
and it is commonly used in areas like algebraic set theory
[
14
,
19
,
59
]
.
In that setting,
one works with a category
\mathsf{C}
together with a predicate
\mathsf{P}
on the morphisms in
\mathsf{C}
,
and morphisms satisfying that predicate are called
small
.
This class of maps is required to be closed under pullback,
which expresses that smallness is preserved under substitution.
A universe for
\mathsf{P}
is a morphism
p:e\rightarrow u

such that each small map is some pullback of
p
.
There are various instances of such a universe,
and a concrete example comes from set theory,
assuming that we have some inaccessible cardinal
\kappa

[
59
]
.
A function satisfies the predicate
\mathsf{P}

if each fiber has cardinality at most
\kappa
.
The set
u
is defined as the set of all subsets of
\kappa

whose cardinality is less than
\kappa
,
and
e
consists of pairs
A\in u
together with
a\in A
.
Other instances include universes of presheaves
[
16
,
49
,
84
]
.

To understand this notion of universe,
we translate it to type theory
using the internal language of categories with finite limits.
A morphism
p:e\rightarrow u
gives us a type
\mathcal{U}
in the empty context
and a type
\mathcal{U}_{*}
in the context
\mathcal{U}
.
The resulting type
\mathcal{U}
gives rise to a Tarski-style universe.
Since every term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{U})
induces a context morphism
s:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
,
the substitution of
\mathcal{U}_{*}
along
s
gives a type in context
\Gamma
.
Note that we are not guaranteed that
\mathcal{U}
is closed under any type former,
meaning that it only gives us a family of types.

We take a different, but equivalent approach.
Our approach is based on directly translating the type theoretic rules for Tarski-style universes to categories.
While the resulting notion is slightly more complex,
it is closer to the usual type theoretic presentation.
This style is also advantageous if one has a concrete description of
\mathsf{El}
,
which one has for the universe of iterative sets
[
46
,
87
]

or for inductive-recursive universes
[
42
]
.
In
Remark

23
we explain why we chose this approach in more detail.

To define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}

whose objects over a category
\mathsf{C}:\mathsf{FinLim}
are families of types for
\mathsf{C}
,
we take two steps.
These two steps reflect that a family of types consists of two ingredients.
The first is a type
\mathsf{u}
of codes,
which we represent in a category
\mathsf{C}
as an object
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.
Hence,
we construct a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}

whose objects over
\mathsf{C}
are objects
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
over
\mathsf{FinLim}

as the displayed bicategory whose

•

objects over
\mathsf{C}
are objects
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
;

•

1-cells over
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
from
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
to
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}

are isomorphisms
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}:\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{u}_{2}
;

•

2-cells over
\tau:\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\mathsf{G}

from
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}:\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{u}_{2}

to
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{u}}:\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{u}_{2}

are proofs that the following triangle commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\mathsf{u}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{u}}}

Proposition 22
.

The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
is univalent.

Proof 9.1
.

We need to prove that
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
is both locally and globally univalent.
The local univalence of
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
follows from the fact that all 2-cells in
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
are invertible
and that 2-cells
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}\Rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}^{\prime}
are the same as identities
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}^{\prime}

for all
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}^{\prime}:\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{u}_{2}
.
To prove that
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
is globally univalent,
we first note that adjoint equivalences over
\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{C}}
from
\mathsf{u}_{1}
to
\mathsf{u}_{2}
are the same as isomorphisms
\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{u}_{2}
.
Since
\mathsf{C}
is univalent,
identities
\mathsf{u}_{1}=\mathsf{u}_{2}
also are the same as isomorphisms
\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{u}_{2}
.
Hence,
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
is univalent.

The second ingredient to a family of types is a map that assigns
to each term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{U})

a type
\mathsf{El}(t)
in context
\Gamma
.
This assignment must stable under substitution
in the sense that
s^{*}(\mathsf{El}(t))
and
\mathsf{El}(s^{*}(t))
are isomorphic.
In addition,
we require various coherence axioms for these isomorphisms.
We collect these features in the notion of an
elements map
.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits and let
\mathsf{u}
be an object in
\mathsf{C}
.
An
elements map

\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}
consists of

•

a map that assigns to each morphism
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
an object
\mathsf{el}(t):\mathsf{C}
and morphism
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t):\mathsf{el}(t)\rightarrow\Gamma
;

•

for all morphisms
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
and
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma

there is a morphism
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,t):\mathsf{el}(s\cdot t)\rightarrow\mathsf{el}(t)

making the following square a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(s\cdot t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

If we have morphisms
t_{1},t_{2}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
,
then every equality
t_{1}=t_{2}
gives rise to a isomorphism
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}:\mathsf{el}(t_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}(t_{2})
making the following triangle commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t_{2})}

An elements map is called
coherent

whenever the following diagrams commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\operatorname{id}\mathbin{\cdot}t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(\operatorname{id},t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}((s_{1}\cdot s_{2})\cdot t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s_{1}\cdot(s_{2}\cdot t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s_{2}\cdot t)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s_{1}\cdot s_{2},t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s_{1},s_{2}\cdot t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s_{2},t)}

To understand
Section

9.1
,
we view this definition from a type theoretic perspective.
Recall that in a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits,
contexts are interpreted as objects in
\mathsf{C}
,
types in context
\Gamma
as pairs of an object
A
together with a morphism
\pi:A\rightarrow\Gamma
,
and terms of such a type are interpreted as sections of
\pi
.
The object
\mathsf{u}
gives rise to an object in the empty context
using the unique map
\diamond:\mathsf{u}\rightarrow\langle\rangle
.
Since the following square is a pullback,
the object
\Gamma\times\mathsf{u}
interprets the universe type in arbitrary contexts
\Gamma
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\times\mathsf{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}

Since sections of the map
\pi_{1}:\Gamma\times\mathsf{u}\rightarrow\Gamma
correspond to morphisms
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
,
terms of type
\mathsf{u}
in context
\Gamma
are the same as such morphisms.
Hence,
the maps
\mathsf{el}
and
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}

assign to each term of type
\mathsf{u}
in context
\Gamma

a type in context
\Gamma
.

From the morphism
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}

we get that
\mathsf{el}(s\cdot t)
is isomorphic to the pullback of
\mathsf{el}(t)
along
s
.
Since substitution of types is given by pullback
and substitution of terms by composition,
we thus get an interpretation of the expected stability rule for
\mathsf{El}
up to isomorphism.
The coherences in
Section

9.1
tell us how to simplify
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,t)
in case
s
is either an identity or a composition.
Note that similar coherences are given for the groupoid syntax of type theory
[
11
, Definition 7]
.

Our notion of family of types corresponds to the more standard notion that uses representable classes of small maps,
where a family of types is given by a morphism.
Specifically, we show that every morphism gives rise to a family of types in our sense,
and that this assignment is an equivalence of types.

Problem 9.2
.

Given a univalent category
\mathsf{C}
with finite limits and an object
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
,
to construct an equivalence between the type of pairs of objects
e:\mathsf{C}
and morphisms
f:e\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

and the type of coherent elements maps for
\mathsf{u}
.

Construction 9.0
(for Problem
9.2
)
.

Given a coherent elements map for
\mathsf{u}
, we define
e
to be
\mathsf{el}(\operatorname{id}_{u})

and
f
to be
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\operatorname{id}_{u})
as in the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{u})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\operatorname{id}_{u})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{u}}

For the converse,
note that if we have a morphism
f:e\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
,
then each
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
gives rise to a pullback as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

We define
\mathsf{el}_{f}(t)
to be
x
and
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}_{f}(t)
to be
p_{1}
.
Suppose that we have morphisms
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
and
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
.
Then we have the following pullback squares.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

We define
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}_{f}(s,t)
to be
p_{2}
.
This data constitutes a coherent elements map.

We only give a sketch of a proof that these maps are inverses.
If we have a map
f:e\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
,
then we get the following pullback square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

Hence, we have an isomorphism between
\mathsf{el}_{f}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{u}})
and
f
in
\mathsf{C}/\mathsf{u}
,
giving us the desired identity.
In addition,
every coherent elements map for
\mathsf{u}

gives rise to a diagram as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t\cdot\operatorname{id}_{u})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\operatorname{id}_{u})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(t,\operatorname{id}_{u})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t\cdot\operatorname{id}_{u})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\operatorname{id}_{u})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{u}}

Since
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}

is an isomorphism between

\mathsf{el}(t)
and the pullback of
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\operatorname{id}_{u})
along
t
,
the other desired equality follows as well.
∎

Remark 23
.

We use a non-standard notion of family of types,
because it gives one more control over the map
\mathsf{el}
.
By picking a convenient representation for
\mathsf{el}
,
it becomes easier to establish that our family of types is closed under various type formers.
We illustrate this advantage via an example.
Inductive-recursive types
[
40
,
42
]
give us a convenient and flexible way to construct universes in the category of sets.
Specifically, we can construct a type
\mathsf{u}
together with a map
\mathsf{El}
sending inhabitants of
\mathsf{u}
to actual types using induction-recursion.
The type
\mathsf{u}
is defined inductively,
and the constructors of
\mathsf{u}
say which types are contained in the universe.
The map
\mathsf{El}
is defined as a recursive function whose domain is
\mathsf{u}
,
and this function maps each constructor of
\mathsf{u}
to its corresponding type.
Note that
\mathsf{u}
and
\mathsf{El}
are mutually dependent in the sense that constructors of
\mathsf{u}
can refer to
\mathsf{El}
.
For instance,
to say that our universe
\mathsf{u}
is closed under
\Sigma
-types,
we postulate that we have a constructor
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b):\mathsf{u}

for all
a:\mathsf{u}
and
b:\mathsf{el}(a)\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
.
We also need to specify how
\mathsf{El}
acts on the constructs
for which we say that
\mathsf{El}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b))=\Sigma(x:\mathsf{El}(a)),\mathsf{El}(b(x))
.

Using an inductive-recursive universe,
we can construct a coherent elements map for
\mathsf{u}
.
Doing so, requires us to show that every
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
gives rise to a map
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t):\mathsf{el}(t)\rightarrow\Gamma
.
We take
\mathsf{el}(t)
to be
\Sigma(x:\Gamma),\mathsf{El}(t(x))
and
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t)
to be the first projection.
If we instead construct a map
f:e\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
and use
Section

9.1
,
then we would define
e
to be
\Sigma(x:\mathsf{u}),\mathsf{El}(x)
and
f
to be the first projection.
However, the resulting elements map is slightly more complicated,
because it is constructed as the following pullback as we showed in
Section

9.1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum(x:\Gamma),\mathsf{El}(t(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

While both elements maps are equivalent,
the latter one requires us to take additional isomorphisms into account
when establishing that the resulting universe is closed under type formers.
Note that we can say the same for universes of iterative sets
[
46
]
.

Our next goal is to describe the displayed 1-cells and 2-cells in the desired displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
.
To do so, we define when functors and natural transformations preserve elements maps.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{2}
be univalent categories with finite limits,
let
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}
be objects,
and let
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
be coherent elements maps for
\mathsf{u}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}
respectively.
If we have a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
that preserves finite limits
and an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}:\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{u}_{2}
,
then an
elements preservation family
for
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})

consists of isomorphisms
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(t):\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})

for each
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}_{1}

such that the following diagrams commute
for all
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
and
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}_{1}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}_{1}(t))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(s\cdot t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(s\cdot t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(s)\cdot(\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,t))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(s\cdot t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(s),\mathsf{F}(t))}

There are a couple of things to note about
Section

9.1
.
The elements map is only preserved by
\mathsf{F}
via the isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(t):\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})

rather than an equality.
Note that the composition
\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}
,
displayed below,
indeed gives a morphism into
\mathsf{u}_{2}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}

In addition, we require two coherences for elements preservation families.
The first coherence says that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(t)
is an isomorphism in the slice category
\mathsf{C}_{2}/\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)
,
and the second coherence expresses that
\mathsf{F}
preserves
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}_{1}
.
For natural transformations, preservation of the elements map is defined as follows.

{defi}

Suppose that we have coherent elements maps
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
for
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}
respectively.
Let
\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
be functors that preserve elements maps,
and let
\tau
be a natural transformation such that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}=\tau(\mathsf{u})\cdot\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{u}}
.
We say that
\tau

preserves elements maps

if for all
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\tau(\Gamma)\cdot(\mathsf{G}(t)\cdot\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{u}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{G}(t)\cdot\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{el}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}_{2}(\tau(\Gamma),\mathsf{G}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}

Finally,
we put these notions together to obtain the desired displayed bicategory.

{defi}

We define
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{el}}
as the displayed bicategory over
\int{\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}}
whose

•

objects over
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{u})
are coherent elements maps for
\mathsf{u}
;

•

1-cells over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})
are elements preservation families for
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})
;

•

2-cells over
\tau
are proofs that
\tau
preserves elements maps.

We also define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}

to be
\Sigma_{\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}}\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{el}}
.

One can directly show that the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
is both locally preordered and groupoidal,
and that it is univalent.
In addition, adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
can be characterized as adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{FinLim}

Proposition 24
.

The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
is univalent.

Proof 9.3
.

By
Proposition

22
,
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
is univalent.
One can show that
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{el}}
is univalent as well,
and since both
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{ob}}
and
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{el}}
are locally preordered and groupoidal,
we get that
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
is univalent
[
3
, Proposition 7.9]
.

Proposition 25
.

Every displayed 1-cell in
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
over an adjoint equivalence is a displayed adjoint equivalence.

Proof 9.4
.

Since
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
is univalent,
this statement follows from the fact that all displayed 1-cells in
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
over the identity are adjoint equivalences.

9.2.
Comprehension Categories with a Family of Types

Next we consider families of types in comprehension categories,
and we show that we have a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
over
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}

whose objects over
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
are families of types.
Note that,
while we are only interested in DFL comprehension categories,
we define the relevant notions for arbitrary comprehension categories.
Our development is similar the treatment of families of types in categories with finite limits,
and in this section we take the same steps.

A universe for a comprehension category consists of an object
\mathsf{u}
in the empty context
together with a map that assigns to every term of type
\mathsf{u}
an actual type in the same context,
which is required to be coherently stable under substitution.
Note that if we do not require any closure condition,
then we only get a family of types.
Throughout this section,
we call
\mathsf{u}
the universe type of such a family of types.

We construct
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
in two steps,
and the first step is to define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
over
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}

whose objects over
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
are objects
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
.
Such an object represents our universe type.
To define
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
,
we use every morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from a comprehension category
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}

comes with an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}:\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle
,
since
\mathsf{F}
is required to preserve terminal objects.

{defi}

We define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}

over
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}
such that

•

the objects over a DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
are objects
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
;

•

the 1-cells over a morphism
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
from
\mathsf{u}_{1}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}\>\langle\rangle
to
\mathsf{u}_{2}:{\mathsf{D}_{2}}\>\langle\rangle

are isomorphisms
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}}}\mathsf{u}_{2}
;

•

the 2-cells over
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
from
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}}}\mathsf{u}_{2}
to
\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\mathsf{u}}:\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{G}_{\langle\rangle}}}\mathsf{u}_{2}

are proofs that the following triangle commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\mathsf{u}}}

Let us make some remarks on
Section

9.2
.
Displayed 2-cells in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
are expressed as a heterogeneous equality,
because
\overline{\tau}(\mathsf{u})\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\mathsf{u}}

and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}

are morphisms
over
\tau\>\langle\rangle\cdot\mathsf{G}_{\langle\rangle}:\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle\rightarrow\langle\rangle
,
and
\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}:\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle\rightarrow\langle\rangle

respectively.
These two morphisms are equal,
because their codomain is the terminal object.

In addition,
the object
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
gives rise to a type in every context via weakening.
We define
\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma}:{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
to be
\diamond_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathsf{u})
,
which is the following Cartesian lift.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\diamond_{\Gamma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond_{\Gamma}}

Often we write
\overline{\mathsf{u}}
instead of
\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma}
if
\Gamma
is clear from the context.
Note that the type
\overline{\mathsf{u}}
is stable under substitution.
Specifically,
if we have a context morphism
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
,
then we have an isomorphism
\mathsf{u}_{s}:s^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})\mathrel{\cong}\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Delta}
in the fiber
\mathsf{D}[\Delta]
.
Since
\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Delta}
is defined to be
\diamond_{\Delta}^{*}(\mathsf{u})
,
and
s^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})
is isomorphic to
s^{*}(\diamond_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathsf{u}))
,
we get the desired isomorphism from the fact that substitution is pseudofunctorial.

Finally,
the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
is both locally preordered and groupoidal.
It also is univalent.

Proposition 26
.

The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}
is univalent.

Proof 9.5
.

We prove this in the same way as
Proposition

22
.

We also define the notion of elements maps for comprehension category.
Such a map interprets the type former
\mathsf{El}
in a comprehension category:
it assigns to each term of the type
\mathsf{u}

a type in the appropriate context.

{defi}

Let
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
be a comprehension category
and let
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
be an object.
An
elements map

\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}
consists of

•

a map that assigns to each term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}})
a type
\mathsf{el}(t):{\mathsf{D}}(\Gamma)
;

•

for all context morphisms
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma

and terms
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}})

an isomorphism
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,t):s^{*}(\mathsf{el}(t))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}(s^{*}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s})

in the fiber category
\mathsf{D}[\Delta]
.

Often we write
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}
instead of
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,t)
whenever
s
and
t
are clear from the context.
Every equality
t_{1}=t_{2}
between terms
t_{1},t_{2}:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}})

gives rise to an isomorphism
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}:\mathsf{el}(t_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}(t_{2})
in the fiber
\mathsf{D}[\Gamma]
.
An elements map is said to be
coherent

whenever the following diagrams commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{id}^{*}(\mathsf{el}(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\operatorname{id}^{*}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{\operatorname{id}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(\operatorname{id},t)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{1}^{*}(s_{2}^{*}(\mathsf{el}(t)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(s_{1}\cdot s_{2})^{*}(\mathsf{el}(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{1}^{*}(\mathsf{el}(s_{2}^{*}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s_{2}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}(s_{1}^{*}(s_{2}^{*}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s_{2}})\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}((s_{1}\cdot s_{2})^{*}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s_{1}\cdot s_{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}^{*}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}

Note that the map
\mathsf{el}
is stable under substitution,
but only up to a coherent isomorphism.
To express the stability, we need to use the coercion operation on terms.
If we have
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})
and
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
,
then the term
s^{*}(t)
is of type
s^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})

rather than
\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Delta}
.
Since we have an isomorphism
\mathsf{u}_{s}:s^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})\mathrel{\cong}\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Delta}
,
we can coerce
s^{*}(t)
into a term of type
\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Delta}
.
We also require two coherence conditions for elements maps,
and these describe the isomorphism
\mathsf{u}_{s}
if
s
is the identity or composition.
Our coherence conditions agree with the ones given by Altenkirch et al.
[
11
]
.

Before we define what it means for functors to preserve elements maps,
we introduce some notation.
First, we describe how functors between comprehension categories act on terms.
The term
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)
is defined to be
\mathsf{F}(t)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\chi}
.
To see that this indeed gives rise to a section,
note that we have a commutative diagram as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma.A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma).\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Next note that we have an isomorphism
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{sub}}(s,A):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(s^{*}(A))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{F}(s)^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A))

as indicated in the diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(s^{*}(A))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(s)^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Delta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{sub}}(s,A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{s})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}(s)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

The morphism
\overline{\mathsf{F}(s)}
is Cartesian by definition,
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{s})
is so as well,
because
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
preserves Cartesian morphisms.
Since Cartesian lifts are unique up to isomorphism,
we get
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{sub}}(s,A):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(s^{*}(A))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{F}(s)^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A))
as desired.

Finally,
suppose that we have an isomorphism
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{u})\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}}}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}
,
which witnesses that
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
preserves the universe type in the empty context.
Then we can extend it to an isomorphism
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})\mathrel{\cong}\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}

that shows that
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
preserves universe type in arbitrary contexts
\Gamma
.
We define
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}}
as the following composition of morphisms.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\diamond_{\Gamma}^{*}(\mathsf{u}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\diamond_{\Gamma})^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{u}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\diamond_{\Gamma})^{*}(\mathsf{u})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\diamond_{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}^{*}(\mathsf{u})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{sub}}(\diamond_{\Gamma},\mathsf{u})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\diamond_{\Gamma})^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Since the codomain of both
\mathsf{F}(\diamond_{\Gamma})
and
\diamond_{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}
is a terminal object,
they are equal,
and thus we have an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}(\diamond_{\Gamma})^{*}(\mathsf{u})\mathrel{\cong}\diamond_{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}^{*}(\mathsf{u})
.
Now we are ready to define when functors preserve elements maps.

{defi}

Suppose that we have comprehension categories
\chi:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
and
\chi:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
,
and elements maps
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
for
\mathsf{u}_{1}:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}\>\langle\rangle
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:{\mathsf{D}_{2}}\>\langle\rangle
respectively.
Let
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
be a morphism of comprehension categories
from
\chi_{1}:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi_{2}:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
,
and let
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}}}\mathsf{u}_{2}
be an isomorphism.
An
elements preservation family

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}
for
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})

consists of an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(t):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}})

for each
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{1})

such that the following diagram commutes for all
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Gamma
and
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{1})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(s^{*}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(s)^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(s^{*}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(s)^{*}(\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(s^{*}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s})\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(s)^{*}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}})\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{\mathsf{F}(s)})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(s)^{*}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}

Next we define when natural transformations of comprehension categories preserve elements maps.
Let
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
and
(\mathsf{G},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{G}_{\chi})
be morphisms of comprehension categories
from
\chi:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{1}^{\downarrow}
to
\chi:\mathsf{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}^{\downarrow}
.
If we have a transformation
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
from
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
to
(\mathsf{G},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{G}_{\chi})
,
then each
\Gamma:\mathsf{C}
and
A:{\mathsf{D}_{1}}(\Gamma)
give rise to a morphism
\overline{\tau}(A):\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A)\rightarrow_{\tau(\Gamma)}\overline{\mathsf{G}}(A)
.
Since
\mathsf{D}_{2}
is a fibration,
we can equivalently represent that morphism as
[\overline{\tau}(A)]:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(A)\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}\tau(\Gamma)^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{G}}(A))
,
which lives in the fiber category
\mathsf{D}_{2}[\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)]
.

{defi}

Let
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
and
(\mathsf{G},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{G}_{\chi})
be morphisms of comprehension categories,
and let
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}:\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{F}_{\langle\rangle}}}\mathsf{u}_{2}

and
\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\mathsf{u}}:\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\mathrel{\cong_{\mathsf{G}_{\langle\rangle}}}\mathsf{u}_{2}
be isomorphisms.
Suppose that we have element preservation families

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}
and
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{el}}

for
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
and
(\mathsf{G},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{G}_{\chi})
respectively.
Let
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
be a transformation from
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
to
(\mathsf{G},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{G}_{\chi})
.
such that
\overline{\tau}(\mathsf{u}_{1})\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\mathsf{u}}=\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}
.
We say that
(\tau,\overline{\tau})

preserves elements maps

whenever the following diagram commutes for all
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{1})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau(\Gamma)^{*}(\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(t)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau(\Gamma)^{*}(\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\tau(\Gamma)^{*}(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}})\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\overline{\tau}(\mathsf{el}(t))]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau(\Gamma)^{*}(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{el}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}

To state the diagram in
Section

9.2
,
we need an equality as follows.

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}}=\tau(\Gamma)^{*}(\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{Tm}}(t)\mathbin{\uparrow}\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\overline{\mathsf{u}}})\mathbin{\uparrow}\mathsf{u}_{s}

This equation requires the assumption that
\overline{\tau}(\mathsf{u})\cdot\overline{\mathsf{G}}_{\mathsf{u}}=\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}
.
Now we put everything together to get the desired displayed bicategory.

{defi}

We define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{el}}

over
\int{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}}
such that

•

the objects over a pair
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{u}
are coherent elements maps for
\mathsf{u}
;

•

the morphisms over a pair
(\mathsf{F},\overline{\mathsf{F}},\mathsf{F}_{\chi})
and
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}
are elements preservation families for
\overline{\mathsf{F}}_{\mathsf{u}}
;

•

the 2-cells over
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
are proofs that
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
preserves elements maps.

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}

to be
