\Sigma_{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{ob}}}\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{el}}
.

The displayed bicategories
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{el}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
are locally preordered and groupoidal.
They also are univalent,
and we have a simple check to determine whether some displayed 1-cell is an adjoint equivalence.

Proposition 27
.

The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
is univalent.

Proof 9.6
.

This proposition is proven similarly to
Proposition

24
.

Proposition 28
.

Every displayed 1-cell in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
over an adjoint equivalence is a displayed adjoint equivalence.

Proof 9.7
.

Similar to
Proposition

25
.

9.3.
Extending the Biequivalence to Families of Types

We end this section by showing that
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
and
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
are biequivalent.
To do so,
we construct a displayed biequivalence over the biequivalence
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\xi,\zeta)

that we constructed in
Section

6.3
.
By
Section

7.1
it suffices to give

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}
from
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
to
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
over
\mathsf{H}
(
Section

9.3
);

•

a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}
from
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
to
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
over
\mathsf{U}
(
Section

9.3
);

•

a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\xi}_{\mathsf{U}}
from
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}
to
\overline{\operatorname{id}}
over
\xi
(
Section

9.3
);

•

a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathsf{U}}
from
\overline{\operatorname{id}}
to
\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}
over
\zeta
(
Section

9.3
)

such that
\overline{\xi}_{\mathsf{U}}
and
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathsf{U}}
are pointwise displayed adjoint equivalences.
Note that by
Proposition

28
and
Proposition

25
,
all displayed morphisms in
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
and
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
over adjoint equivalences are displayed adjoint equivalences,
so it suffices to construct the aforementioned pseudofunctors and pseudotransformations.

While each of the displayed bicategories that we considered in
Sections

7
and
8
, has trivial 2-cells,
neither
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
nor
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
has trivial 2-cells.
Their types of 2-cells are only guaranteed to be propositions
and they are not necessarily inhabited.
The construction of the desired pseudofunctors and pseudotransformations thus becomes more complicated.
To define a displayed pseudofunctor,
one must construct a suitable action on displayed 2-cells
and 2-cells that witness the preservation of the identity and composition,
and for a displayed pseudotransformation,
one must construct the displayed 2-cells that witness naturality.
Below we give a sketch of each of the components of the displayed biequivalence.
We only describe the action of objects for each of the pseudofunctors and pseudotransformations.

Problem 9.8
.

To construct
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}:\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{H}}\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
.

Construction 9.0
(for Problem
9.8
)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits,
let
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
be an object,
and let
\mathsf{el}
be a coherent elements map for
\mathsf{u}
.
From
\mathsf{u}
we get an object
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}}\>\langle\rangle
,
which is determined by the unique map
\mathsf{u}\rightarrow\langle\rangle
.
To construct an elements map for
\mathsf{u}
,
we use that terms
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})
in
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
correspond to morphisms
\overline{t}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
.
This is because we have the following pullback square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}\times\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}

Hence, every term
t:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})

gives rise to a type
\mathsf{el}(\overline{t})
in context
\Gamma
.
Since substitution in the comprehension category
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is interpreted as pullback,
this assignment is coherently stable under substitution.
Hence, we get a coherent elements map for
\operatorname{id}:\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
.
∎

Problem 9.9
.

To construct a displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}:\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{U}}\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
.

Construction 9.0
(for Problem
9.9
)
.

Suppose that we have a DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
,
an object
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
,
and a coherent elements map
\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}
.
Note that we have an object
\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
,
and our goal is to construct a coherent elements map for
\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}
.
Morphisms
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}
are the same as terms
\widetilde{t}:\mathsf{Tm}_{\Gamma}(\overline{\mathsf{u}}_{\Gamma})
,
because we have the following pullback square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}[\Gamma]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Hence, to define the desired elements map,
we send each morphism
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}

to
\mathsf{el}(\widetilde{t})
.
This assignment is coherently stable under substitution,
because
\mathsf{el}
is so.
∎

Problem 9.10
.

To construct a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\xi}_{\mathsf{U}}:\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}\Rightarrow_{\xi}\overline{\operatorname{id}}
.

Construction 9.0
(for Problem
9.10
)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits with an elements map
\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.
Recall that the pseudotransformation
\xi(\mathsf{C}):\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}(\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}(\mathsf{C}))\rightarrow\mathsf{C}
is defined to be the identity functor pointwise.
We first note that the universe type in
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}(\mathsf{C})
is
\mathsf{u}\rightarrow\langle\rangle
,
and thus the universe type in
\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}(\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}(\mathsf{C}))
is
\mathsf{u}
.
Hence,
we can take
\xi(\mathsf{C})_{\mathsf{u}}
to be the identity.
To construct the necessary elements preservation family,
we need to construct for every
t:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

an isomorphism between
\mathsf{el}(\overline{\widetilde{t}})

and
\mathsf{el}(t\cdot\operatorname{id})
.
The desired isomorphism is given by
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}
,
since one can show that
\overline{\widetilde{t}}=t\cdot\operatorname{id}
.
∎

Problem 9.11
.

To construct a displayed pseudotransformation
\overline{\zeta}_{\mathsf{U}}:\overline{\operatorname{id}}\Rightarrow_{\zeta}\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}
.

Construction 9.0
(for Problem
9.11
)
.

Suppose that we have a DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
,
an object
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
,
and a coherent elements map
\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}
.
Note that the underlying displayed functor of
\zeta(\chi):\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}
is
\chi
,
and that the universe type in
\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}}(\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}}(\chi))
is
\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}

We can thus define
\zeta(\chi)_{\mathsf{u}}
to be the identity.
The construction of the necessary elements preservation family is similar to
Section

9.3
.
∎

Problem 9.12
.

To construct a displayed biequivalence
(\overline{\mathsf{H}}_{\mathsf{U}},\overline{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{U}},\overline{\xi}_{\mathsf{U}},\overline{\zeta}_{\mathsf{U}})
from
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}
to
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
over
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\xi,\zeta)
.

Construction 9.0
(for Problem
9.12
)
.

The necessary displayed pseudofunctors are constructed in
Sections

9.3
and
9.3
,
and the necessary displayed pseudotransformations are constructed in
Sections

9.3
and
9.3
.
Note that these pseudotransformations are pointwise displayed adjoint equivalences by
28
and
25
,
and thus we have a displayed biequivalence by
Section

7.1
.
∎

10.
The Internal Language of Toposes with a Universe

We finish our development by considering elementary toposes with a natural numbers object
and a universe that is closed under the type formers in the internal language of such toposes.
In
Sections

8
and
9
,
we already gave the necessary prerequisites:
by
Section

8.2
,
we have a biequivalence
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
,
and by
Section

9.3
,
we have a displayed biequivalence
\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}}
.
We can combine these biequivalences
to obtain a biequivalence
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{u}}

between the bicategories of toposes with a natural numbers object and a universe object
and DFL comprehension categories with
\Pi
-types and a universe object,
and that satisfy
\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
fiberwise.

However,
this universe is not required to be closed under any type former
that one might expect.
We are not guaranteed that, for instance,
our universe contains the terminal object
or that it is closed under
\Sigma
-types.
Hence,
the main goal of this section is to extend the biequivalence
with type formers in the universe.
Our focus lies on the closure conditions for universes in toposes
given by Streicher
[
85
]
.
Specifically,
we look at
the natural numbers object,
the subobject classifier,
propositional resizing,

\Sigma
-types,
and
\Pi
-types.

To construct the desired biequivalence,
we use another construction,
namely
reindexing
.
If we have a biequivalence
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
then every displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}_{2}

gives rise to a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
over
\mathsf{B}_{1}

together with a displayed biequivalence from
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
to
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{F}
.
This construction allows us to only construct a single displayed bicategory in this section,
namely
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{U}}^{\mathsf{D}}
over the bicategory
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}

whose objects over an elementary topos with a universe
\mathsf{u}

represent that
\mathsf{u}
is closed under the aforementioned type formers.
Using reindexing, we obtain a biequivalent displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{u}}
.
We also have a description of when a universe
\mathsf{u}
in some DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

is closed under type formers.
Specifically,
\mathsf{u}
is closed under some type former
if the universe
\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}
in the category
\mathsf{C}
of contexts is closed under it.

To see why the resulting description of type formers is sensible,
we consider an example.
Suppose that we have some DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

that comes with a fiberwise natural numbers object
\mathbb{N}

and an elements map
\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle
.
To say that
\mathsf{el}
contains
\mathbb{N}
,
we need a term
\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathsf{Tm}_{\langle\rangle}(\mathsf{u})

and an isomorphism
\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}})\mathrel{\cong}\mathbb{N}
in the fiber category
\mathsf{D}[\langle\rangle]
.
We saw in
Section

9.3

that terms
\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathsf{Tm}_{\langle\rangle}(\mathsf{u})

are the same as morphisms
\langle\rangle\rightarrow\langle\rangle.\mathsf{u}
,
In addition,
isomorphisms
\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}})\mathrel{\cong}\mathbb{N}
in
\mathsf{D}[\langle\rangle]

are the same as isomorphisms
\langle\rangle.\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}})\mathrel{\cong}\langle\rangle.\mathbb{N}
in the slice category
\mathsf{C}/\langle\rangle

because
\chi
is fully faithful.
Since
\langle\rangle.\mathbb{N}
is a natural numbers object in
\mathsf{C}/\langle\rangle
,
we can thus express that
\mathsf{el}
contains
\mathbb{N}
by only referring to
\mathsf{C}
,
which corresponds to the description obtained from reindexing.

10.1.
Reindexing Displayed Bicategories

We start this section by discussing reindexing of displayed bicategories.
Specifically, we construct from each displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}_{2}

and each pseudofunctor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}

a new displayed bicategory
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
,
which is the reindexing of
\mathsf{D}
along
\mathsf{F}
.
We also show that we have a displayed biequivalence between
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
and
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{F}

if
\mathsf{F}
is a biequivalence.

The type
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}
of morphisms in
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
is defined to be
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{y}
.
However,
a difficulty comes up when one tries to define the identity and composition in
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
.
To define the displayed identity,
we need a morphism from
\overline{x}
to
\overline{x}
over
\mathsf{F}(\operatorname{id})
.
Since we only have an invertible 2-cell
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}:\operatorname{id}\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{F}(\operatorname{id})
in general,
we cannot necessarily transport
\overline{\operatorname{id}}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}\overline{x}
to obtain a morphism of type
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(\operatorname{id})}\overline{x}
.
We have the same problem for composition.
If we have
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{y}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(g)}\overline{z}
,
then their composition in
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
should be of type
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f\cdot g)}\overline{z}
.
However,
we only have a morphism
\overline{f}\cdot\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)\cdot\mathsf{F}(g)}\overline{z}
,
and an invertible 2-cell
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}:\mathsf{F}(f)\cdot\mathsf{F}(g)\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{F}(f\cdot g)
.

To define the identity and composition in
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
,
we assume that
\mathsf{D}
comes with additional structure,
namely a
local isocleaving

[
26
]
.
If a displayed bicategory is equipped with a local isocleaving,
then one is able to transport displayed 1-cells along invertible 2-cells in the base.
Local isocleavings are defined in the usual way via a lifting property.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{D}
be a displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{B}
.
A
local isocleaving
for
\mathsf{D}

is a map that assigns to every invertible 2-cell
\tau:f\Rightarrow g

and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{g}\overline{y}

a displayed 1-cell
\tau^{*}(\overline{g}):\overline{x}\rightarrow_{f}\overline{y}

and a invertible 2-cell
\overline{\tau}:\tau^{*}(\overline{g})\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
.

We can visualize local isocleavings using the diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{*}(\overline{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau}}

Just like every displayed categories over a univalent category can be equipped with an isocleaving
[
7
, Construction 5.12]
,
we can equip every displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over a locally univalent bicategory
\mathsf{B}
with a local isocleaving,
By
Proposition

4
,
we can assume that
\tau
is an identity,
and then we can take
\operatorname{id}^{*}(\overline{g})
to be
\overline{g}

and
\overline{\operatorname{id}}:\overline{g}\Rightarrow_{\operatorname{id}}\overline{g}
to be the identity.

{defi}

Suppose that we have bicategories
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
a pseudofunctor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
and a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}_{2}

that is locally preordered
and equipped with a local isocleaving.
We define the
reindexed displayed bicategory
,
denoted by
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
,
as the displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{B}_{1}
whose

•

objects over
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}
are objects
{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
;

•

morphisms over
f:x\rightarrow y
from
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
to
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(y))

are morphisms
\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{y}
;

•

2-cells over
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
from
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{y}
to
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(g)}\overline{y}

are 2-cells
\overline{\tau}:\overline{f}\Rightarrow_{\tau}\overline{g}
.

The displayed identity is defined as the following 1-cell.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}^{-1})^{*}(\overline{\operatorname{id}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}^{-1}}}

We define the displayed composition of
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{y}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}(g)}\overline{z}
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}\cdot\overline{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1})^{*}(\overline{f}\cdot\overline{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(f)\cdot\mathsf{F}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(f\cdot g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}}}

To define the right unitor in
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
,
we need to make a 2-cell from
(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1})^{*}(\overline{f}\cdot\overline{\operatorname{id}}^{*})
to
\overline{f}
over
\mathsf{F}(\rho)

where we write
\overline{\operatorname{id}}^{*}
for
(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}^{-1})^{*}(\overline{\operatorname{id}})
.
We have the following composition of 2-cells.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1})^{*}(\overline{f}\cdot\overline{\operatorname{id}}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\operatorname{id}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\rho}}

This composition lives over the following 2-cell.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(f\cdot\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\operatorname{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{c}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{i}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

This 2-cell is equal to
\mathsf{F}(\rho)
as desired,
since
\mathsf{F}
is a pseudofunctor.
The other operations on 2-cells are defined similarly.

Since we assumed
\mathsf{D}
to be locally preordered,

\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
is so as well,
because it inherits its 2-cells from
\mathsf{D}
.
For this reason, all bicategorical laws of
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
hold vacuously.
We can also show that
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
is univalent.

Proposition 29
.

If
\mathsf{D}
is univalent,
then so is
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})
.

Proof 10.1
.

This proposition follows from the fact
that invertible 2-cells and adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})

correspond to invertible 2-cells and adjoint equivalences in
\mathsf{D}
respectively.

Next we show that we can lift every biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
with
F:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}

to a displayed biequivalence
(\overline{\mathsf{F}},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\overline{\eta},\overline{\varepsilon})
where
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}
.
We make several simplifying assumptions in this construction,
namely that
\mathsf{D}
has contractible 2-cells
and that
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
are univalent.
However, to define
\overline{\varepsilon}
,
we need to make a further assumption:
there needs to be invertible 2-cells witnessing the triangle identities for
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)

[
48
, Definition 2.1]
.

To see why, we describe how
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
and
\overline{\mathsf{G}}
are defined concretely.
The displayed pseudofunctor
\overline{\mathsf{F}}
sends
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})}(x)
to
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
.
To define
\overline{\mathsf{G}}
,
we need to map every
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
to some
\overline{\mathsf{G}}(\overline{x}):{\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})}(\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(x)))
.
Here we use that
\mathsf{B}_{2}
is univalent.
Specifically,
since we have an adjoint equivalence
\eta(x):x\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(x))
,
every
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
can be lifted to obtain
\eta(x)^{*}(\overline{x}):{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(x)))
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta(x)^{*}(\overline{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(x))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\eta(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta(x)}

The existence of this lift follows from
Proposition

4
.
It suffices to assume that
\eta(x)
is the identity,
and we take
\eta(x)^{*}(\overline{x})
to be
\overline{x}
and
\overline{\eta(x)}
to be the identity.
Note that it would also have been sufficient to assume that
\mathsf{D}
is equipped with a global isocleaving
[
26
]
.

For the counit,
we need to find for each
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
a displayed morphism
\overline{\varepsilon}(\overline{x})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))^{*}(\overline{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{F}(x))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varepsilon}(\overline{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\varepsilon(x))}

Here one would expect to use the morphism
\eta(x)^{*}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))}\overline{x}
.
However,
this morphism does not lie in the fiber of
\mathsf{F}(\varepsilon(x))
,
but instead it lies in the fiber of
\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))
.
Hence,
to get the desired morphism,
we need to have an invertible 2-cell between
\mathsf{F}(\varepsilon(x))
and
\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))
,
which means that our biequivalence must be sufficiently coherent.

To solve this problem,
we use that every biequivalence can be refined into a coherent one
where the triangle identities are satisfied up to coherent invertible 2-cell
[
48
, Theorem 3.2]
.
Similar statements have been considered for quasi-invertible maps in homotopy type theory
[
87
, Theorem 4.2.3]
,
and for adjoint equivalence in a bicategory
[
48
, Theorem 1.9]
.
Since we only need the triangle identities,
we discuss those and not any coherence.

Problem 10.2
.

Given a biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
where
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
to construct a biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon^{\prime})

and for each
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}

an invertible 2-cell
\theta:\mathsf{F}(\varepsilon^{\prime}(x))\mathrel{\cong}\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))
.

Construction 10.0
(for Problem
10.2
)
.

We only show how
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is defined.
Note that we have pseudotransformations
\eta:\mathsf{G}\cdot\mathsf{F}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}
and
\varepsilon:\operatorname{id}\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}\cdot G
.
Since
\varepsilon
is an equivalence,
we also have a pseudotransformation
\varepsilon^{-1}:\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}
.
We define
\varepsilon^{\prime}
as the following composition.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{id}\cdot(\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G})\cdot(\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\cdot(\mathsf{G}\cdot(\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\cdot((\mathsf{G}\cdot\mathsf{F})\cdot\mathsf{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\cdot(\operatorname{id}\cdot\mathsf{G})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{-1}\vartriangleright(\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{G})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}\vartriangleleft\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}\vartriangleleft(\eta\vartriangleright\mathsf{G})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}\vartriangleleft\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

Here the pseudotransformations
\lambda
,
\lambda^{-1}
,
\alpha
, and
\alpha^{-1}
are defined to be the identity 1-cell pointwise,
and the whiskering operations are defined in the usual way.
Since
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is a composition of adjoint equivalences,
it is one as well.
∎

Problem 10.3
.

Given univalent bicategories
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
a biequivalence
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
where
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
,
and a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}_{2}
with trivial 2-cells,
to construct a displayed biequivalence
(\overline{\mathsf{F}},\overline{\mathsf{G}},\overline{\eta},\overline{\varepsilon})

over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G},\eta,\varepsilon)
where
\overline{\mathsf{F}}:\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})\rightarrow_{\mathsf{F}}\mathsf{D}
.

Construction 10.0
(for Problem
10.3
)
.

By
Section

10.1
,
we can assume without loss of generality
that we have invertible 2-cells

\theta:\mathsf{F}(\varepsilon^{\prime}(x))\mathrel{\cong}\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))

for each
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}
.
As discussed before,

\overline{\mathsf{F}}
sends
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{F}^{*}(\mathsf{D})}(x)
to
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(x))
.
For
\overline{\mathsf{G}}
we use that
\mathsf{B}_{2}
is univalent
and that we have an adjoint equivalence
\eta(x):x\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(x))
,
meaning that every
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}(x)
gives rise to an object
\eta(x)^{*}(\overline{x}):{\mathsf{D}}(\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(x)))
as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta(x)^{*}(\overline{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(x))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\eta(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta(x)}

We define
\overline{\eta}(\overline{x}):\eta(x)^{*}(\overline{x})\rightarrow_{\eta(x)}\overline{x}
to be
\overline{\eta(x)}
.
For
\overline{\varepsilon}
,
we use that
\mathsf{D}
can be equipped with a local isocleaving
since
\mathsf{B}_{1}
is univalent.
We define
\overline{\varepsilon}(\overline{x})
to be
\theta(x)^{*}(\overline{g})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))^{*}(\overline{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{F}(x)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta(x)^{*}(\overline{\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\varepsilon(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\theta(x)}}

Since
\overline{\eta(\mathsf{F}(x))}
is a displayed adjoint equivalence,

\overline{\varepsilon}(\overline{x})
is so as well.
∎

10.2.
Type Formers in Universes

Now we have the necessary tools to
prove the internal language theorem for elementary toposes with a natural numbers object and a universe.
Our starting point are the bicategories
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
and
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{u}}
.
The objects of
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
are univalent elementary toposes with a natural numbers object
and a universe that is not required to be closed under any type former.
Objects of
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{u}}
are DFL comprehension categories with
\Pi
-types and a universe object,
and that satisfy
\mathsf{Is}{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
fiberwise.

{defi}

We define the bicategory
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}

to be
\int{(\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathsf{FinLim}^{\mathsf{U}})}
,
and we define
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{u}}

to be
\int{(\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}\times\mathsf{DFLCompCat}^{\mathsf{U}})}
.
By
Sections

7.1
,
8.2
and
9.3

we have a biequivalence
(\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{u}},\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{u}},\xi_{\mathsf{u}},\zeta_{\mathsf{u}})

where
\mathsf{H}_{\mathsf{u}}:\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}\rightarrow\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{u}}
.

As discussed before,
categorical developments of universes are usually based on a notion of small maps,
and we can express that the universe is closed under certain type formers via suitable closure properties.
As an example, we consider
\Sigma
-types,
which are interpreted a category with finite limits via composition.
If we have a category
\mathsf{C}
together with a predicate
\mathsf{P}
on the morphisms of
\mathsf{C}
,
then saying that
\mathsf{P}
is closed under composition expresses that our universe is closed under
\Sigma
-types.
Similarly, we can define when our universe is closed under other type formers,
like the subobject classifier and
\Pi
-types.

Our approach is slightly different.
Since we are interested in models of type theory,
we want our categorical models to interpret the expected type theoretic rules.
Such rules are expressed as operations on terms that are stable under substitution up to isomorphism.
However,
if one uses small maps,
then one only talks about a
property
on the maps.
Closure properties of small maps thus express properties of our universe
rather than giving operations.
Angiuli and Gratzer use the same ideas to give a notion of universe in categories with families
[
13
, Structure 6.4.17]
,
although they require strict stability under substitution.

Depending on the context,
one uses a different collection of axioms for small maps
[
59
,
19
,
14
]
,
and we follow Streicher
[
85
]
.
Streicher considers the following closure properties for classes of small maps in an elementary topos:

•

small maps are closed under pullback;

•

small maps are closed under composition;

•

every monomorphism is small;

•

the unique map from
\mathbb{N}
to
\langle\rangle
is small;

•

the unique map from
\Omega
to
\langle\rangle
is small;

•

small maps are closed under dependent products.

From a type theoretic point of view,
each of these conditions expresses that our universe contains a certain type former.
Since small maps are closed under composition and dependent products,
our universe is closed under
\Sigma
-types and
\Pi
-types.
The universe also is closed under substitution,
because small maps are closed under pullback.
The universe also contains
\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega
,
because the maps from these objects to
\langle\rangle
are small.
Since monomorphisms correspond to internal propositions by
Proposition

19
,
our universe satisfies propositional resizing,
meaning that it contains every proposition.

In the remainder of this section,
we define when a universe contains
\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega
,
and when it is closed under
\Sigma
-types,
\Pi
-types, and propositional resizing.
We also study the relation between our development
and the closure properties of small maps that are used for classes of small maps as given by Streicher
[
85
]
.
To do so,
we use that each universe with an elements map gives rise to a class of small maps.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with an object
\mathsf{u}

and an elements map
\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}
.
A morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
is said to be
small

if there merely exists a morphism
a:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

and an isomorphism
h:\mathsf{el}(a)\mathrel{\cong}x
making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

An object
x:\mathsf{C}
is said to be
small

if the map
\diamond:x\rightarrow\langle\rangle
is small.

It is important to note that being small is a property for morphisms rather than structure,
as we do not require
a
and
h
to be chosen.
Small maps are closed under isomorphism.
One can prove this property using that
\mathsf{C}
is univalent,
but for a concrete proof,
we use the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

If
a
and
h
witness that
f
is small,
then
a
and
h\cdot h^{\prime}
witness that
f^{\prime}
is small.

To see why small maps are closed under pullbacks,
we use the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(s\cdot a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

If we assume
f:y\rightarrow\Gamma
to be small,
then we have a morphism
a:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
together with an isomorphism
y\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}(a)
.
Since
\mathsf{el}(s\cdot a)
is the pullback of
s
and
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a)
,
we must have an isomorphism
\mathsf{el}(s\cdot a)\mathrel{\cong}x
,
which proves that
g
is indeed small.

10.2.1.
Natural Numbers

Now we define various displayed bicategories over
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
,
whose objects over
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{u})
are structures that represent that
\mathsf{u}
is closed under some type former.
We start with the natural numbers object.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits,
and let
\mathsf{el}
be an elements map for an object
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.
Suppose that we have a parameterized natural numbers object
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathsf{C}
.
A
code for the natural numbers
for
\mathsf{el}

consists of a morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}:\langle\rangle\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

together with an isomorphism
\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}})\mathrel{\cong}\mathbb{N}
.

Note that we do not require a stability property in
Section

10.2.1
.
This is because the morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}:\langle\rangle\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
gives rise to a morphism
\overline{\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
by composition,
which is automatically stable under substitution.
In the language of small maps,
one expresses that the universe contains the natural numbers object
by saying that
\mathbb{N}
is small.
If
\mathsf{el}
has a code for the numbers,
then this closure property follows.

Proposition 30
.

If
\mathsf{el}
has a code for the natural numbers,
then
\mathbb{N}
is small.

Proof 10.4
.

The desired code and isomorphism are given by
\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}
respectively.

Recall that every functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
that preserves natural numbers objects,
comes with an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\mathbb{N}}:\mathsf{F}(\mathbb{N}_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\mathbb{N}_{2}

where
\mathbb{N}_{1}
and
\mathbb{N}_{2}
are the natural numbers objects of
\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{2}
respectively.
Now we define the first of the desired displayed bicategories.

{defi}

Suppose that the elements maps
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
for
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}
have codes for the natural numbers.
Let
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
be a functor that preserves elements maps
and the natural numbers objects.
We say that
\mathsf{F}

preserves codes for natural numbers

if the following diagrams commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathbb{N}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1})\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\diamond\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{N}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2}}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{U}_{\mathbb{N}}

over
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
whose

•

objects over a univalent elementary topos
\mathsf{C}
with an elements map
\mathsf{el}

are codes for the natural numbers;

•

morphisms over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}})
are proofs that
\mathsf{F}
preserves codes for natural numbers.

The type of 2-cells over an arbitrary natural transformation is the unit type.

10.2.2.
Subobject Classifiers

We use the same ideas to define when a universe contains the subobject classifier.
Specifically,
we require there to be a term in the empty context
whose associated type is isomorphic to the subobject classifier.
Smallness of
\Omega
is proven in a similar way.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits,
and let
\mathsf{el}
be an elements map for an object
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.
Suppose that we have a subobject classifier
\Omega
in
\mathsf{C}
.
A
subobject classifier code
for
\mathsf{el}

consists of a morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}:\langle\rangle\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

together with an isomorphism
\mathsf{i}^{\Omega}:\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Omega})\mathrel{\cong}\Omega
.

Proposition 31
.

If
\mathsf{el}
has a subobject classifier code,
then
\Omega
is small.

Proof 10.5
.

The smallness of
\Omega
follows from
\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}
and
\mathsf{i}^{\Omega}
.

To construct the desired displayed bicategory,
we define when functors preserve codes for subobject classifier.
Here we use that we have an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\Omega}:\mathsf{F}(\Omega_{1})\mathrel{\cong}\Omega_{2}
whenever
\mathsf{F}
preserves subobject classifiers.

{defi}

Suppose that the elements maps
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
for
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}
have codes for subobject classifiers.
If
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
is a functor that preserves elements maps
and subobject classifiers,
we say that
\mathsf{F}

preserves codes for subobject classifiers

if the following diagrams commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}\>\langle\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\langle\rangle}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diamond}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}_{2}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Omega_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}_{1})\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\diamond\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{c}^{\Omega}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{i}^{\Omega}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Omega}_{2}}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{U}_{\Omega}

over
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
whose

•

objects over a univalent elementary topos
\mathsf{C}
with an elements map
\mathsf{el}

are codes for subobject classifiers;

•

morphisms over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}})
are proofs that
\mathsf{F}
preserves codes for subobject classifiers.

The type of 2-cells over an arbitrary natural transformation is the unit type.

10.2.3.
Propositional Resizing

Next we consider closure conditions for propositional resizing,
and here we meet a complication compared to the natural numbers and subobject classifier.
Whereas it was not necessary to require any stability conditions for
\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega
,
we need to consider suitable stability conditions for resizing codes.
The reason is that propositional resizing takes types in arbitrary contexts,
but for
\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega
it was sufficient to only consider a type in the empty context.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits,
and let
\mathsf{el}
be an elements map for an object
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.
A
family of resizing codes
for
\mathsf{el}

consists of a map that assigns to each monomorphism
m:x\rightarrow y

a morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m):y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

and an isomorphism
\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m):\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))\mathrel{\cong}x
making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

We require the following diagram to commute for each
s:w\rightarrow y
and
m:x\rightarrow y

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)}

where
m^{\prime}
is defined using the following pullback.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Let us make a couple of remarks on
Section

10.2.3
.
A monomorphism
m:x\rightarrow y
represents an internal proposition in context
y
by
Proposition

19
,
and a morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m):y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
represents a term of type
\mathsf{u}
in context
y
.
Hence, a family of resizing codes assigns to each internal proposition in context
y

a term of type
\mathsf{u}
in context
y

whose associated type is isomorphic to
x
.
Such a family thus interprets propositional resizing.

In addition, we only required a stability condition for
\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)
in
Section

10.2.3
,
because stability for
\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)
is derivable.
Specifically,
this stability requirement means that the upper square of the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

We can prove that the upper square indeed commutes,
because
m
is a monomorphism
and
\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)
is an isomorphism in the slice category.
The desired stability property follows from the fact that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}(s,\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}(s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Type theoretically,
monomorphisms correspond to internal propositions (
Proposition

19
),
and our proof uses that all inhabitants of a proposition are equal.

From our notion of resizing codes,
one can deduce the analogous closure condition for small maps:
every monomorphism is small.

Proposition 32
.

If
\mathsf{el}
is equipped with a family of resizing codes,
then each monomorphism is small.

Proof 10.6
.

The smallness of a monomorphism
m
is witnessed by
\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)
and
\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m)
.

With these notions in place,
we are ready to define the displayed bicategory of resizing codes for a universe.
Recall that every functor
\mathsf{F}
that preserves finite limits,
also preserves monomorphisms.

{defi}

Suppose that the elements maps
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
for
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}
have resizing codes,
and let
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
be a functor that preserves finite limits and elements maps.
We say that
\mathsf{F}

preserves resizing codes

if the following diagram commutes for every monomorphism
m:x\rightarrow y
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\Gamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(m))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\mathsf{resize}}(\mathsf{F}(m))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{resize}}

over
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
whose

•

objects over a univalent elementary topos
\mathsf{C}
with an elements map
\mathsf{el}

are resizing codes;

•

morphisms over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}})
are proofs that
\mathsf{F}
preserves resizing codes.

The type of 2-cells over an arbitrary natural transformation is the unit type.

Whereas in
Sections

10.2.1
and
10.2.2

we require functors to both preserve the code and the isomorphism,
we do not do so for resizing codes in
Section

10.2.3
.
Again the reason is that the analogous preservation requirement would hold vacuously,
because all inhabitants of propositions are equal.

10.2.4.
\Sigma
-types

Now we consider closure conditions for when a universe contains
\Sigma
-types.
Intuitively,
we want to say
whenever we have types
A
and
B
in
\mathsf{u}

where
B
might depend on
A
,
then their dependent sum also is in
\mathsf{u}
.
Since
\Sigma
-types are interpreted using composition,
this closure condition is translated as follows to the language of categories with finite limits.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent category with finite limits
with an elements map
\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.
A
family of
\Sigma
-codes
for
\mathsf{el}

is a map that assigns to each
a:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
and
b:\mathsf{el}(a)\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

a morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b):y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

and an isomorphism
\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}(a,b):\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}(b)

making the left triangle in the diagram below commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}(a,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b)}

We also require a stability condition for
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}
and
\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}
,
which says that for all morphisms
s:x\rightarrow y
,

a:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
, and
b:\mathsf{el}(a)\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

we have that
s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b)=\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)
,
which we display in the diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(s\cdot a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b)}

We also require the following diagram to commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(a,b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}(a,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}

Whereas stability for
\mathsf{i}^{\mathsf{resize}}
was derivable in
Sections

10.2.3
and
10.2.4
,
we cannot derive the second coherence condition in
Section

10.2.4
.
Intuitively,
this is because
\Sigma
-types are not necessarily propositions.

If a universe is equipped with a family of
\Sigma
-codes,
then its associated class of small maps satisfy the expected closure condition.

Proposition 33
.

If a universe is equipped with a family of
\Sigma
-codes,
then the composition of small maps is again small.

Proof 10.7
.

Suppose that
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow z
are small.
Then we have morphisms
c_{f}:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
and
c_{g}:z\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

and isomorphisms
h_{f}:\mathsf{el}(c_{f})\mathrel{\cong}x
and
h_{g}:\mathsf{el}(c_{g})\mathrel{\cong}y

making the following diagrams commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(c_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(c_{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(c_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(c_{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

To see that
f\cdot g
is small,
we use the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(c_{g},h_{g}\cdot c_{f}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(h_{g}\cdot c_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(c_{f})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(c_{g})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(c_{g},h_{g}\cdot c_{f}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(h_{g}\cdot c_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(c_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(c_{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(c_{g},h_{g}\cdot c_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The desired code for
f\cdot g
is
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(c_{g},h_{g}\cdot c_{f})
.
We also need to show
\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}(c_{g},h_{g}\cdot c_{f}))
is isomorphic to
x
,
and for that we prove that
\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}\cdot\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot h_{f}
is an isomorphism.
Note that
\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}
and
h_{f}
are isomorphisms by assumption.
To see why
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}
is an isomorphism,
we use that it is the pullback of
h_{g}

and that pullbacks of isomorphisms are again isomorphisms.

To construct the displayed bicategory of
\Sigma
-codes,
we define when functors preserve
\Sigma
-codes.
Here we use the same ideas as for
Sections

10.2.1
and
10.2.2
:
functors need to preserve both
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}
and
\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}
.

{defi}

Suppose that we have elements maps
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
for
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}
respectively,
and a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}

with an elements preservation family
F_{\mathsf{el}}
.
Let
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{1}
and
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{2}
be
\Sigma
-codes for
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
respectively.
We say that
\mathsf{F}

preserves
\Sigma
-codes

whenever the following diagrams commute for all
a:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}_{1}
and
b:\mathsf{el}(a)\rightarrow\mathsf{u}_{1}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{1}(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{2}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{1}(a,b)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{1}(a,b))\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{2}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{-1}\cdot(\mathsf{F}(b)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(b)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}_{1}(a,b))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}_{2}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}

Here we define
a^{\prime}
to be
\mathsf{F}(a)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}

and
b^{\prime}
to be
(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{F}(b))\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}
,
as displayed below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{F}(a)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}(a))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{F}(a)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}_{2}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{U}_{\Sigma}

over
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
whose

•

objects over a univalent elementary topos
\mathsf{C}
with an elements map
\mathsf{el}

are
\Sigma
-codes for
\mathsf{el}
;

•

morphisms over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}})
are proofs that
\mathsf{F}
preserves
\Sigma
-codes.

The type of 2-cells over an arbitrary natural transformation is the unit type.

10.2.5.
\Pi
-types

The last type former that we consider,
is the
\Pi
-type former,
and our next goal is to define when a universe is closed under dependent products.
To do so,
we use similar ideas as for
\Sigma
-types:
we say that we have an operation on morphisms to the universe,
and we require this operation to be stable under substitution up to isomorphism.
Functors are required to preserve the operation and the isomorphism.
In the following,
we use some additional notation related to locally Cartesian closed categories.
Specifically, we need that dependent products are functorial in both arguments
and that they are stable under substitution.

{nota}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a locally Cartesian closed category.
Given a morphism
f:y\rightarrow z
, we write
\Pi_{f}:\mathsf{C}/y\rightarrow\mathsf{C}/z

for the right adjoint of
f^{*}:\mathsf{C}/z\rightarrow\mathsf{C}/y
,
which we call the dependent product.
Specifically,
if we have morphisms
f:y\rightarrow z
and
g:x\rightarrow y
,
then
\Pi_{f}g:\mathsf{C}/y
is a morphism,
which we write as
\mathsf{p}_{f,g}:\Pi_{f}g\rightarrow z
.

The dependent product operation is functorial in both arguments.
Suppose that we have morphisms
h_{x}
and
h_{y}
as in the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h_{x}^{*}(y_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then we have a morphism
\Pi_{h_{x}}h_{y}:\Pi_{f_{1}}g_{1}\rightarrow\Pi_{f_{2}}g_{2}
.
This morphism is defined using the unit and counit of the adjunction.
If both
h_{x}
and
h_{y}
are isomorphisms,
then
\Pi_{h_{x}}h_{y}
is an isomorphism as well.

Dependent products also are stable under pullback.
Stability means that
whenever we have morphisms
s:z^{\prime}\rightarrow z
,

f:y\rightarrow z
and

g:x\rightarrow y
,
then we have an isomorphism
s^{\Pi}_{f,g}:s^{*}(\Pi_{f}g)\mathrel{\cong}\Pi_{f^{\prime}}g^{\prime}

where
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
are defined using the following pullbacks.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{s}^{*}(g)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\overline{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

The desired isomorphism is constructed in
Section

7.2
.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a univalent locally Cartesian closed category
with an elements map
\mathsf{el}
for
\mathsf{u}:\mathsf{C}
.
A
family of
\Pi
-codes
for
\mathsf{el}

is a map that assigns to each
a:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
and
b:\mathsf{el}(a)\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

a morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b):y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

together with
an isomorphism
\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}(a,b):\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b))\mathrel{\cong}\Pi_{\mathsf{el}(a)}\mathsf{el}(b)

that makes the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{\mathsf{el}(a)}\mathsf{el}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}(a,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{p}_{a,b}}

We also require a stability condition for
\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}
and
\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}
,
which says that for all morphisms
s:x\rightarrow y
,

a:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}
, and
b:\mathsf{el}(a)\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

we have that
s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b)=\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)
,
which we display in the diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(s\cdot a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b)}

We required
\mathsf{el}(s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b))
to be a pullback of
s
and
\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b))
,
so we have an isomorphism
h:s^{*}(\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b)))\mathrel{\cong}\mathsf{el}(s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b))
.
We also require the following diagram to commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(\prod_{\mathsf{el}(a)}\mathsf{el}(b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(a,b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot a)}(\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{a^{\prime}}b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{*}(\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\Pi}_{\mathsf{el}(a),\mathsf{el}(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}(s\cdot a,\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{f}g}

Here the isomorphisms
f
and
g

and the morphisms
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are defined using the following pullback squares.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{s}^{*}(\mathsf{el}(b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(s\cdot a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s^{*}(\mathsf{el}(a))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}\cdot b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\overline{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(s\cdot a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Again the analogous closure condition for small maps follows from a family of
\Pi
-codes.

Proposition 34
.

If the morphisms
f:y\rightarrow z
and
g:x\rightarrow y
are small,
then
\mathsf{p}_{f,g}:\Pi_{f}g\rightarrow z
is small as well.

Proof 10.8
.

Proven the same way as
Proposition

33

using
\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}
and
\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}
instead of
\mathsf{c}^{\Sigma}
and
\mathsf{i}^{\Sigma}
.

To define the displayed bicategory of
\Pi
-codes,
we define when a functor preserves such codes.
Whenever we have a functor
\mathsf{F}
between locally Cartesian closed categories
\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{2}
,
then,
by definition,
we have an isomorphism
\mathsf{F}_{\Pi}(f,g):\mathsf{F}(\Pi_{f}g)\mathrel{\cong}\Pi_{\mathsf{F}(f)}\mathsf{F}(g)

for each
f:y\rightarrow z
and
g:x\rightarrow y
.

{defi}

Suppose that we have elements maps
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
for
\mathsf{u}_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{u}_{2}:\mathsf{C}_{2}
respectively,
where
\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\mathsf{C}_{2}
are univalent locally Cartesian closed categories.
Let
\mathsf{F}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
be a functor that preserves exponentials
and let
F_{\mathsf{el}}
be an elements preservation family for
\mathsf{F}
.
Let
\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{1}
and
\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{2}
be
\Pi
-codes for
\mathsf{el}_{1}
and
\mathsf{el}_{2}
respectively.
We say that
\mathsf{F}

preserves
\Pi
-codes

whenever the following diagrams commute for all
a:y\rightarrow\mathsf{u}_{1}
and
b:\mathsf{el}(a)\rightarrow\mathsf{u}_{1}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{1}(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{2}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{1}(a,b)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\prod_{\mathsf{el}_{1}(a)}\mathsf{el}_{1}(b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{1}(a,b)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{2}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{\mathsf{el}_{2}(a^{\prime})}\mathsf{el}_{2}(b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\prod_{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(a))}\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(b)))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}_{1}(a,b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\Pi}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(a),\mathsf{el}_{1}(b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{i}^{\Pi}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(a)}g}

Here we define
a^{\prime}
to be
\mathsf{F}(a)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}

and
b^{\prime}
to be
(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{F}(b))\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}

as displayed below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{F}(a)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}(a))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{u}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{u}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(\mathsf{F}(a)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{c}^{\Pi}_{2}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}}

The morphism
g:\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{*}(\mathsf{el}_{2}(b^{\prime}))\rightarrow\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(b))
is defined as the following composition.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{*}(\mathsf{el}_{2}(b^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}^{-1}\cdot(\mathsf{F}(b)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(b)\cdot\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{u}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(\mathsf{F}(b))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{el}_{1}(a))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{el}_{2}(a^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{eq}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{mor}}_{2}(b^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{el}^{\mathsf{sub}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}(b)^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}}}

We define the displayed bicategory
\mathsf{U}_{\Pi}

over
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}
whose

•

objects over a univalent elementary topos
\mathsf{C}
with an elements map
\mathsf{el}

are
\Pi
-codes for
\mathsf{el}
;

•

morphisms over
(\mathsf{F},\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{el}})
are proofs that
\mathsf{F}
preserves
\Pi
-codes.

The type of 2-cells over an arbitrary natural transformation is the unit type.

10.2.6.
Topos Universes

Now finally everything is in place for the biequivalence that we aimed to construct in this section.
We start by collecting the displayed bicategories that we constructed so far
to obtain the displayed bicategories of topos universes.

{defi}

We define the displayed bicategory

\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{U}}^{\mathsf{D}}

over
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}}

to be

\mathsf{U}_{\mathbb{N}}\times\mathsf{U}_{\Omega}\times\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{resize}}\times\mathsf{U}_{\Sigma}\times\mathsf{U}_{\Pi}

The bicategory

\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{U}}

is defined to be
\int{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{U}}^{\mathsf{D}}}
.
In addition, we define
the displayed bicategory

\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{U}}^{\mathsf{D}}

over
\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
to be

\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{u}}^{*}(\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{u}})
,
and we define
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{U}}
to be
\int{\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{U}}^{\mathsf{D}}}
.

Proposition 35
.

The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{U}}^{\mathsf{D}}
is univalent.

Proof 10.9
.

It suffices to show that each of the displayed bicategories

\mathsf{U}_{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathsf{U}_{\Omega}
,
\mathsf{U}_{\mathsf{resize}}
,
\mathsf{U}_{\Sigma}
, and
\mathsf{U}_{\Pi}
.
For
\mathsf{U}_{\mathbb{N}}
,
it suffices to show that each proof that the identity preserves codes for natural numbers
from
(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1},\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1})
to
(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2},\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2})
gives rise to an equality
between
(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1},\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}_{1})
and
(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2},\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}_{2})
,
and details are given in the formalization.
The univalence of the other displayed bicategories is proven in the same way.

By
Proposition

29
,
it follows that
\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{U}}^{\mathsf{D}}
is univalent as well.
Let us state what the objects of the bicategory
\mathsf{CompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}}
are.
An object is given by a DFL comprehension category
\chi:\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\downarrow}

that satisfies the local property
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}}
fiberwise
and with a universe object
\mathsf{u}:{\mathsf{D}}\>\langle\rangle

and elements map
\mathsf{el}
.
This universe object is required to satisfy various closure conditions,
and we explicate the closure condition for
\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\chi
is a fiberwise equivalence by
Proposition

14
,
each slice category
\mathsf{C}/\Gamma
has a natural numbers object,
and we write
\mathbb{N}
for the natural numbers object of
\mathsf{C}/\langle\rangle
.
By definition,
we have a morphism
\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}:\langle\rangle\rightarrow\mathsf{u}

and an isomorphism
\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathsf{el}(\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}})\mathrel{\cong}\mathbb{N}
.
These morphisms give us a term
\overline{\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}}:\mathsf{Tm}_{\langle\rangle}(\mathsf{u})

together with an isomorphism
\overline{\mathsf{i}^{\mathbb{N}}}
between
\mathsf{el}{\overline{\mathsf{c}^{\mathbb{N}}}}

and the fiberwise natural numbers object in
\mathsf{D}
,
which proves the expected derivation rules.
For the other type formers,
we can give a similar argument.

We finish this section with the desired biequivalence.

Problem 10.10
.

To construct a biequivalence
\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N},\mathsf{U}}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{DFLCompCat}_{\mathsf{ElemTop}_{\mathbb{N}},\mathsf{U}}
.

Construction 10.0
(for Problem
10.10
)
.

By
Sections

10.1
and
10.2
.
∎

11.
Related Work

In this section, we discuss related work,
and we start by recalling how our work compares to that of Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
]
.
Clairambault and Dybjer
[
31
, Theorem 6.1]
constructed a biequivalence between
the bicategory of categories with finite limits
and the bicategory of democratic CwFs that support extensional identity types and
\Sigma
-types,
and a biequivalence between
the bicategory of locally Cartesian closed categories
and the bicategory of democratic CwFs that support extensional identity types,
\Sigma
-types and
\Pi
-types.
This paper is very much in the spirit of Clairambault and Dybjer,
and we give analogous statements in
Sections

6.3
and
7.2
.
The main differences arise from the fact we work in univalent foundations and with univalent categories,
while Clairambault and Dybjer work in set-theoretic foundations.
Because of the difference in foundations,
we use comprehension categories, whereas they use categories with families,
and we do not use split fibrations in our development.
This changes how we define the pseudofunctor from categories to comprehension categories (
Section

6.1
),
because we do not need to construct an equivalent split fibration
[
18
]
.

The methods and results in
Section

8
are based on the work by Maietti
[
68
]
.
However,
Maietti only proved soundness and completeness for various dependent type theories
[
68
, Theorems 5.8 and 5.28]
,
while our goal is to construct biequivalences.
For this reason,
we strengthened our notion of local property (
Section

8.1
)
to guarantee pseudofunctoriality.

In
Sections

9
and
10
,
